![обложка|156](<../attachments/param%C4%81tm%C4%81-sandarbha-trans.-bhanu-swami--%D0%BE%D0%B1%D0%BB%D0%BE%D0%B6%D0%BA%D0%B0--262ddb93ec8e2b35.jpeg>)

Paramātmā Sandarbha is the third Sandarbha. This work describes Paramātmā, since Bhāgavatam delineates three aspects of the Lord: Bhagavān, Paramātmā and Brahman. One Sandarbha has already been dedicated to Bhagavān. Paramātmā is similar to Bhāgavan, having a form and qualities. However, Paramātmā in its three forms as Mahāviṣṇu, Garbhodakaśāyī and Kṣīrodakaśāyī functions in relation to creation, maintenance and destruction of the material universe. Bhagavān functions in relation to devotees in the spiritual world. As well as describing Paramātmā, this work describes the jīva in some detail, since the jīva-śakti takes shelter of Paramātmā. Some parts, particularly of the commentary, deal with arguments against Māyāvāda philosophy.

# Paramātmā Sandarbha \[Trans. Bhanu Swami\]

## Invocation

May Śrī Krṣṇa remain ever glorious!

I have repeated examined the original work, which was written by Gopāla Bhaṭṭa, who greatly pleased Rūpa and Sanātana--parts of which were in order, parts of which were out of order and parts of which were incomplete. Putting it in a systematic order, I have written this work.

## 1.

Now Paramātmā will be described. Though the Lord exists in Vaikuṇṭha as the supreme ātmā, the term Paramātmā refers to the expansion of the Lord within the material world. This form along with the jīva in the material world is described in two verses:

*kṣetrajña etā manaso vibhūtīr*  
*jīvasya māyā-racitasya nityāḥ*  
*āvirhitāḥ kvāpi tirohitāś ca*  
*śuddho vicaṣṭe hy aviśuddha-kartuḥ*

**The jīva becomes absorbed in the covering on the** ***jīva*** **known as the mind, which performs actions opposed to the Lord and which is made of** ***māyā.*** **These transformations are without beginning, sometimes visible and sometimes invisible.**

*kṣetrajña ātmā puruṣaḥ purāṇaḥ*  
*sākṣāt svayaṁ jyotir ajaḥ pareśaḥ*  
*nārāyaṇo bhagavān vāsudevaḥ*  
*sva-māyayātmany avadhīyamānaḥ*

**The Supreme Lord is the knower of all bodies, is all pervasive, has a human form, is the cause of the universe, is self-revealing, is without material birth and is Lord of all others. He is the shelter of all jīvas, is a expansion of Bhagavān, and the abode of all beings. He is fixed in himself by his svarūpa śakti. SB 5.11.12-13**

The jīva

Though beyond māyā (śuddhaḥ) the jīva observes with attention (vicaṣṭe), and becomes absorbed in the well known (etāḥ) actions (vibhūtīḥ) of the antaḥkaraṇa (manasaḥ) made of māyā. That jīva is called the kṣetrajña since he is the knowers of the field comprised of his two bodies (gross and subtle). It is said:

*yayā sammohito jīva ātmānaṁ tri-guṇātmakam \|*  
*paro ’pi manute ’narthaṁ tat-kṛtaṁ cābhipadyate \|\|*

**Bewildered by that** ***māyā,*** **the** ***jīva*****, though separate from the three** ***guṇas,*** **considers himself made of the three** ***guṇas*** **and takes on material existence created by the** ***guṇas.*** **SB 1.7.5**

How is the mind made of māyā? It is undertaken (racitasya) by the jīva (jīvasya)’s upādhi, by his identity with it. What is the antaḥkaraṇa? It acts against the Lord (aviśuddha-kartuḥ). What are the actions of the mind? They continue without beginning (nityāḥ). When do the actions take place? They manifest (avibhūtāḥ) during waking and dream states and are invisible (tirohitāḥ) during deep sleep.

The puruṣa

The other kṣetrajña is the puruṣaḥ who is the cause of the universe and who is eternal (purāṇaḥ). He is self-manifesting (sākṣād svayaṁ jyotiḥ), unlike the jīva who is dependent. He is without birth (ajaḥ). He is the lord even of Brahmā and others (pareśaḥ). He is the shelter of all the jīvas (Nārāyaṇa), since they are under his control. This form is called bhagavān since he is an aṁśa of Bhagavān, manifesting a portion of bhagavān’s qualities such as aiśvarya. He is called Vāsudeva because he is the abode of all beings. He is established in his svarūpa (ātmani) by his svarūpa śaktyā (sva-māyāyā). The verb has a passive reflexive meaning. Even though he enters into illusory māyā as the antaryāmī, he is situated in his svarūpa by his svarūpa śakti and is never contaminated by māyā. He remains separate because he is the knower of all fields as Vāsudeva (the abode of all beings).

**Distinction of Paramātmā and jīva**

One kṣetrajña, the jīva, is bewildered by māyā. The other kṣetrajña, the Paramātmā (ātmā in verse 5.11.13), is pure. Thus the chief kstetrajña is Paramātmā. It is said:

*sarvaṁ pumān veda guṇāṁś ca taj-jño*  
*na veda sarva-jñam anantam īḍe*

**The jīva who can know Paramātmā knows all about his body, but does not really know the omniscient Lord. I worship that Lord with infinite qualities. SB 6.4.25**

It the Gītā it is said:

*idaṁ śarīraṁ kaunteya kṣetram ity abhidhīyate \|*  
*etad yo vetti taṁ prāhuḥ kṣetra-jña iti tad-vidaḥ \|\|*

**O son of Kuntī, this body is called the field*****.*** **Those in knowledge call the person who knows this field the knower of the field.**

*kṣetra-jñaṁ cāpi māṁ viddhi sarva-kṣetreṣu bhārata \|*  
*kṣetra-kṣetrajñayor jñānaṁ yat taj jñānaṁ mataṁ mama \|\|*

**O descendent of Bharata, know that I also am the knower of the field in all bodies. According to me, knowledge means knowledge of the field and knowledge of the two types of knowers of the field. BG 13.2-3**

*kṣetra-jñaṁ cāpi māṁ viddhi* means “know me to be the knower of *all* fields (bodies0.” This is not like the jīva who knows only his own body. The statement does not indicate identity of the jīva and the Lord by suggesting that a person should know a single, conscious substance without qualities. Otherwise the phrase “in all bodies” would have no meaning. Later the Lord explains the distinction:

*jñeyaṁ yat tat pravakṣyāmi yaj jñātvāmṛtam aśnute \|*  
*anādi mat paraṁ brahma na sat tan nāsad ucyate \|\|*

**I will now speak about the object of knowledge, knowing which you will attain liberation. This supreme Brahman, Paramātmā, is without beginning, takes shelter of me, and is beyond material cause and effect. BG13.13**

*sarvataḥ pāṇi-pādaṁ tat sarvato ’kṣi-śiro-mukham \|*  
*sarvataḥ śrutimal loke sarvam āvṛtya tiṣṭhati \|\|*

**Everywhere this Brahman has his hands and feet. Everywhere Brahman has his eyes, heads and mouths. Everywhere are his ears. Brahman covers everything in the universe. BG 13.14**

The distinction is also clear where the Lord teaches about the jīvas practice of knowledge starting with freedom from pride (BG 13.7-12) The jīva must be endowed with the list of qualities to realize the Lord. As well *mayi cānanya-yogena bhaktir avyabhicāriṇī* : knowledge means steady bhakti to me with undivided attention. 1

If Kṛṣṇa had desired to identify the jīva with the Lord, saying something similar to tat tvam asi: you are that (Chāndogya Upaniṣad 6.8.7), he would not have said ksetra-kṣetrājñayor jñānam: I consider knowledge to be knowledge of the field and the two types of knowers of the field. He would have said “I consider knowledge to be knowledge of the field and the two Lords who are knowers of the field.”

In explaining the Chāndogya Upaniṣad, Brahma-sūtra 1.3.20 says anyārthas tu paramarśaḥ: the mention of the jiva has another purpose, to show ultimately the Lord. Similarly in the Gītā verses, jīva is mentioned to show the one object of knowledge, the Lord, for the jīva and this meaning is suitable to the context.

One should also not take the meaning to be simply knowledge of the field and the jīva its knower (excluding the Lord) as in atheistic Sāṅkhya philosophy (which proposes only prakṛti and puruṣa), since the word mām, referring to the Lord, is also mentioned. One should also not take the Vivartavāda meaning, that the jīva is the Lord under illusion since such a statement is never proved by scriptures like the Gītā and the Vedas. It amounts to Buddhist philosophy. Furthermore, the explanations of Vivartavāda are illogical, like those of the Buddhists.

Impersonal knowledge (nirviśeṣa-jñānam) is not the means of liberation since in the previous chapter of the Gīta that impersonal knowledge was rejected (BG 12. 1-4) Moreover the unalloyed devotees are glorified in the same section (BG 12.6) and the Lord promises to quickly deliver them from the ocean of death. (BG 12.7) Persons attached to impersonal jñāna are not respected.

The Lord also says:

*yat karmabhir yat tapasā jñāna-vairāgyataś ca yat*  
*yogena dāna-dharmeṇa śreyobhir itarair api*  
*sarvaṁ mad-bhakti-yogena mad-bhakto labhate ’ñjasā*  
*svargāpavargaṁ mad-dhāma kathañcid yadi vāñchati*

**Everything that can be achieved by** ***karma,*** **penance,** ***jñāna, vairāgya,*** **mystic** ***yoga,*** **charity,** ***dharma*** **and all other auspicious means of perfecting life is easily achieved by my devotee through** ***bhakti.*** **If somehow or other my devotee desires Svarga, liberation, or residence in my abode, he easily achieves such benedictions. SB 11.20.32-33**

It is also said in Mokṣa-dhārma:

*yā vai sādhana-sampattiḥ puruṣārtha-catuṣṭaye*  
*tayā vinā tad āpnoti naro nārāyaṇāśrayaḥ*

**The person who takes shelter of Nārāyaṇa attains the Lord without perform acts to attain the four goals of life.**

In the next chapter of the Gītā, the Lord points out devotion to the Lord particularly, so that the praise in the Chapter Twelve is not wasted. The section concludes with this:

*iti kṣetraṁ tathā jñānaṁ jñeyaṁ coktaṁ samāsataḥ \|*  
*mad-bhakta etad vijñāya mad-bhāvāyopapadyate \|\|*

**Thus I have described concisely the field, the process of knowing, and the object of knowledge. My devotee, understanding this, becomes qualified for** ***prema*****. BG 13.18**

This verse states that by having bhakti (with distinction between jīva and iśvara) one can understand these subjects. Thus the devotee is designated as the ksetra-jña of himself. The Lord, the object of knowledge, is designated as the kṣetra-jña of all beings. After pointing out the object of knowledge along with the field and knower of the field (jīva), Kṛṣṇa then shows that natures of the jīva and the Lord are indestructible.

*prakṛtiṁ puruṣaṁ caiva viddhy anādī ubhāv api \|*  
*vikārāṁś ca guṇāṁś caiva viddhi prakṛti-sambhavān \|\|*

**Know that both** ***prakṛti*** **and the** ***jīva*** **are without origin. And know that the body, the senses, happiness and distress all arise from** ***prakṛti*****, not the** ***jīva*****. BG 13.20**

Though the jīva is stated to be situated in matter in this verse, the jīva is indestructible by showing his non-material nature.

*upadraṣṭānumantā ca bhartā bhoktā maheśvaraḥ \|*  
*paramātmeti cāpy ukto dehe ’smin puruṣaḥ paraḥ \|\|*

**There is also a supreme person in the body, called Paramātmā, the great lord, who is the witness situated close to the** ***jīva*****, who is the permitter of the** ***jīva*****’*****s*** **actions, the supporter of the** ***jīva*** **and ruler of the** ***jīva.*** **BG 13.23**

This verse points out the superiority of the person known as Paramātmā over the jīva and shows the great difference.

*dvāv imau puruṣau loke kṣaraś cākṣara eva ca \|*  
*kṣaraḥ sarvāṇi bhūtāni kūṭastho ’kṣara ucyate \|\|*

**There are two conscious beings mentioned in the** ***Vedas*****: the conditioned** ***jīvas*** **and the liberated** ***jīvas*****. The bound** ***jīvas*** **are all these living entities with destructible bodies. The liberated** ***jīvas*** **are fixed with one form for all time. BG 15.16**

*uttamaḥ puruṣas tv anyaḥ paramātmety udāhṛtaḥ \|*  
*yo loka-trayam āviśya bibharty avyaya īśvaraḥ \|\|*

**The highest person is different from this. He is described in the scriptures as the Paramātmā, who, by entering the three worlds, supports the living entities, as the unchanging controller. BG15.17**

In verse 13.20, the Lord clearly says that the jīva also is indestructible. In verse 13.23, updraṣṭā means the supreme witness. Anumanṭa means one who initiates jīva’s actions according to his karma. Bhartā means nourisher. Bhokta means protector. Maheśvara means the owner of everything. Paramātmā means the antaryāmī of all beings.

In the last two verses (15.16-17), kūṭastha means “he who is fixed in one form of all time” according to Amara-koṣa. That refers to the pure jīva, since later it is said, “The supreme person is another.” Here also the body, the individual knower of his own body and the knower of all bodies is indicated. The two indestructible beings (akṣara) who are different (since the word anyaḥ is used) never give up their forms. Thus never do they become situated as impersonal oneness. The phrase mad-bhāvāyopapadyate (BG 13.18) indicates that the jīva attains great powers like the Lord (sārṣṭi) (not that he becomes the Lord). Though the jīva and the Lord are both indestructible, the Lord is recognized as the object of worship in order that the jīva rid himself of his material contamination, since he has little power.

The phrase idam śarīram (BG 13.2) should be considered in this way. Idam refers to the visible body of the individual. The body or field refers to the individual body, as a member of a class (each jīva having a body), since the phrase “all bodies” (sarva-kṣetreṣu) is also mentioned.

The jīva knowing his body (BG 13.2) is also indicated in Bhāgavatam 5.11.25 quoted above. As well it is said:

*deho 'savo 'kṣā manavo bhūta-mātrām*  
*ātmānam anyaṁ ca viduḥ paraṁ yat*  
*sarvaṁ pumān veda guṇāṁś ca taj-jño*  
*na veda sarva-jñam anantam īḍe*

**The body, life airs, senses, internal senses, gross elements and sense objects do not know themselves or other things or the** ***jīva.***  **The** ***jīva*** **knows all of these items and the** ***guṇas*** **which cause them. He also can know Paramātmā, but does not really know the omniscient Lord. I worship that Lord with infinite qualities. SB 6.4.25**

Know me as the knower of all fields (BG 13.3) means “know me, svayam Bhagavān, as the knower of all individuals bodies and the whole universe, rather than the knower of one’s own body.” It was said:

*atha vā bahunaitena kiṁ jñātena tavārjuna \|*  
*viṣṭabhyāham idaṁ kṛtsnam ekāṁśena sthito jagat \|\|*

**But what is the use of knowing all these individual** ***vibhūtis*****, Arjuna? Please understand: by just one portion of myself, I am firmly established in this universe. BG 10.42**

Where there is no alternative, one must take the trouble to use lakṣaṇā (interpretation). However, if one wishes to say that the two knowers of fields are identical the statement should have read “Know me to be that knower of the field” rather than “Know me to be the knower of all fields also.” Bhāgavatam 5.11.12 also says there are two knowers of the field.

Brahma-sūtras also expresses the difference between jīva and the Lord.

*guhāṁ praviṣṭāv ātmānau hi tad-darśanāt*

**The two beings in the space within the heart are the jīva and the Lord because that is described elsewhere. Brahma-sūtra 1.2.11**

Gītā also shows the difference in the summary of the section (upasaṁhāra) by stating that the jīva is situated in prakṛti.

*puruṣaḥ prakṛti-stho hi bhuṅkte prakṛti-jān guṇān \|*  
*kāraṇaṁ guṇa-saṅgo ’sya sad-asad-yoni-janmasu \|\|*

**The** ***jīva*****, identifying with** ***prakṛti*** **in the form of his body, experiences happiness and distress which are born from** ***prakṛti*****, by taking birth in various bodies of** ***devatās*** **and animals. This is caused by his desires for objects made of the** ***guṇas*****. BG 13.22**

Thus there is unity because of the agreement or initial statement (upakrama) and the final statement (upasaṁhāra). The same type of agreement is expressed by the author of Brahma-sūtras:

*asad vyapadeśān neti cen na dharmāntareṇa vākya-śeṣāt*

**If you say that scripture says that** ***asat*** **(non-existence of effect in the cause) existed in the beginning, the answer is no, because the last sentence indicates a different quality, non-manifestation. Brahma-sūtra 2.17**

Next consider this statement:

*kṣetra-kṣetrajñayor jñānaṁ yat taj jñānaṁ mataṁ mama \|\|*

**Knowledge of the knower of the field and the field should be considered my knowledge. BG 13.3**

Because knowledge gained through the knowledge gathering senses in the body (kṣetra) will be shown to arise from Paramātmā and knowledge of the jīva was shown to be knowledge confined within individual bodies, that all that knowledge should be considered my knowledge (mama jñānam matam) since shadows of portions of my knowledge reside in their bodies and I have entered into each of the knowers of the fields (jīvas) with small portions of my knowledge. Thus it is correctly said that the chief knower of the field is Paramātmā.

Bhagavān himself appears as Paramātmā, but the jīva appears from his portion (Paramātmā) united with the Lord’s special power (taṭasha-śakti). This is according to the following principle.

*ajani ca yan-mayaṁ tad avimucya niyantṛ bhavet*

**The product (jīva) which has the qualities of the cause cannot be separated from the cause. The cause is thus the controller of the effect. SB 10.87.30**

This has been said in the Gītā (10.42) with the verse quoted: by just one portion of myself (Paramātmā), I am firmly established in this universe. In Viṣṇu Purāṇa it is also said:

*yasyāyutāyutāṁśāṁśe viśva-śaktir iyaṁ sthitā*  
*para-brahma-svarūpam yat praṇamāma tam avyayam*

**I offer respects to the indestructible supreme Brahman, by whose portion of a ten thousandth portion the śakti of this universe is situated.**

In the same scripture, the Lord is described as the possessor of pure śakti. (Viṣṇu Purāṇa 1.9.44) This is shown in verses like the following.

*Kalā-kāṣṭhā-nimeṣādi-kāla-jñānātma-rūpiṇe*  
*dhyeyāya viṣṇurūpāya paramākṣara-rūpiṇe*

**I offer respects to the form of Viṣṇu, the highest indestructible form, the object of meditation, the form of knowledge and time, measured in portions including seconds. Viṣṇu Purāṇa 3.5.18**

In Nārada-pañcarātra Nārada says:

*śuddha-sargam ahaṁ deva jñātum icchāmi tattvataḥ*  
*sarga-dvayasya caivāsya yaḥ paratvena vartate*

**O Lord! I desire to know in truth about the pure creation which is superior to the two creations.**

“The two creations” refers to matter and the jīva.

*yaḥ sarva-vyāpako devaḥ para-brahma ca śāśvatam*  
*cit-sāmānyaṁ jagaty asmin paramānanda-lakṣaṇam*  
*vāsudevād abhinnaṁ tu vahny-arkendu-śata-prabham*  
*vāsudevo 'pi bhagavāṁs tad-dharmā parameśvaraḥ*  
*svāṁ dīptiṁ kṣobhayaty eva tejasā tena vai yutam*  
*prakāśa-rūpo bhagavān acyutaṁ cāsṛjad dvija*

**O brāhmaṇa! The all pervading Lord, the supreme Brahman, eternal, consciousness of the universe,the highest bliss, brilliant like a hundred fires, suns and moons, and non-different from Vāsudeva, filled with all qualities, the supreme Lord, Bhagavān in visible form agitated his effulgence and created Acyuta endowed with his power.**

*so 'cyuto 'cyuta-tejaś ca svarūpaṁ vitanoti vai*  
*āśritya vāsudevaṁ ca khastho megho jalaṁ yathā*

**As a cloud in the sky spreads water, Acyuta, taking shelter of Vāsudeva, spreads his form, endowed with infallible power.**

*kṣobhayitvā svam ātmānaṁ satya-bhāsvara-vigraham*  
*utpādayām āsa tadā samudrormir jalaṁ yathā*

**After agitating himself he created that effulgent form, just as water creates ocean waves.**

*sa cinmayaḥ prakāśātmā utpādyātmānam ātmanā*  
*puruṣākhyam anantaṁ ca prakāśa-prasaraṁ mahat*

**The Lord as the independent soul created by himself him own form known as the puruṣa, who is infinite, great and filled with brilliance.**

*sa ca vai sarva-jīvānāṁ āśrayaḥ parameśvaraḥ*  
*antaryāmī sa teṣāṁ vai tarakānām ivāmbaram*

**The form of the puruṣa is the shelter of all jīvas. He is their inner soul, antaryāmī. He is like the sky sheltering the stars.**

*sendhanaḥ pāvako yadvat sphuliṅga-nicayaṁ dvija*  
*anicchataḥ prerayati tadvad eṣa paraḥ prabhuḥ*

**As a blazing fire manifests many sparks, so the Supreme Lord, without desire, emits many individual souls.**

*prāg-vāsanā-nibandhānāṁ bandhānāṁ ca vimuktaye*  
*tasmād viddhi tad-aṁśāṁs tān sarvāṁśāṁ tam ajaṁ prabhum*

**For liberation from all bondage caused by previous desires, please understand about the jivas and unborn Lord of all jīvas.**

It is said that Pradyuma is the antaryāmī of Brahmā, Viṣṇu is the antaryāmī of Manu, and Saṅkarṣaṇa is the antaryāmī of Śiva. It should be understood that, accepting various forms, the Lord, when he appears within the world, acts as the antaryāmī of various jīvas by his various expansions (aṁśas). That Saṅkarṣaṇa and the puruṣa are the origin of Śiva is confirmed by scripture. prakṛtim ātmanaḥ saṅkarṣaṇa-saṁjñām: Śiva worships Saṅkarṣaṇa, his source, (SB 5.17.16)

*ādāv abhūc chata-dhṛtī rajasāsya sarge*  
*viṣṇuḥ sthitau kratu-patir dvija-dharma-setuḥ*  
*rudro ’pyayāya tamasā puruṣaḥ sa ādya*  
*ity udbhava-sthiti-layāḥ satataṁ prajāsu*

**In the beginning, the** ***puruṣa*** **appeared as Brahmā through** ***rajoguṇa*** **for creation, as Viṣṇu, the lord of sacrifice, and protector of** ***dharma*** **of the** ***brāhmaṇas*****, for maintenance, and as Śiva through** ***tamo-guṇa*** **for destruction. In this way creation, maintenance and destruction of the living entities takes place at all times. SB 11.4.5**

*bhūtātmā cendriyātmā ca prādhānātmā tathā bhavān*  
*ātmā ca paramātmā ca tvam ekaḥ pañcadhā sthitaḥ*

**You are situated in five ways: as the soul of the elements, the senses, pradhāna, as the jīva and as Paramātmā. Viṣṇu Purāṇa 5.18.50**

Thus the puruṣa as antaryāmī of all beings is termed Paramātmā. This is the meaning Paramātmā in *brahmeti paramātmeti bhagavān iti śabdyate.* (SB 1.2.11) Commenting on the phrase namas tubhyam bhagavate, brahmane paramātmani, Śrīdhara Svāmī says paramātmane sarva-jīva-niyatre: Paramātmā is the controller of all jīvas. Saying that the Paramātmā becomes puruṣa by a covering of māyā is metaphorical only. For it is said in Viṣṇu Purāṇa:

*nānto 'sti yasya na ca yasya samudbhavo 'sti*  
*vṛddhir na yasya pariṇāma-vivarjitasya*  
*nāpakṣayaṁ ca samupaity avikalpa-vastu*  
*yas taṁ nato 'smi puruṣottamam ādyam īḍyam*

**I offer respects to the supreme Lord, the first person, worthy of worship, who has no end and no creation, who has no increase, since he is devoid of change and who has no decrease since he is unchanging.**

*tasyaiva yo 'nu guṇa-bhug bahudhaika eva*  
*śuddho 'py aśuddha iva mūrti-vibhāga-bhedaiḥ*  
*jñānānvitaḥ sakala-sattva-vibhūti-kartā*  
*tasmai nato 'smi puruṣāya sadāvyayāya*

**I offer respects to the puruṣa, always unchanging, who follows behind the Lord, who possesses all six qualities and enjoys bliss, and though one, has many forms such as Brahmā, who though pure, appears to be impure (attached to his creation), who is filled with knowledge and who distributes various qualities to all beings through various forms such as Dakṣa and Manu. Viṣṇu Purāṇa 6.8.58-59**

The verse is translated according to Śrīdhara Svāmī’s meanings for anu, guṇa, bhuk, bahudhā, aśuddha, mūrti-vibhāga-bhedaiḥ, and sakala-sattva-vibhūti-kartā.

In Mokṣa-dharma it is said:

*yat tat sūkṣmam avijñeyaṁ avyaktam acalaṁ dhruvam*  
*indriyair indriyārthaiś ca sarva-bhūtaiś ca varjitam*  
*sa hy antarātmā bhūtānāṁ kṣetrajñaś ceti kathyate*  
*tri-guṇa-vyatirikto vai puruṣaś ceti kalpitaḥ*

**The antaryāmī, who is subtle, unknown, invisible, immovable, fixed in form, and devoid of material elements, material senses and sense objects, is called the knower of the field and the puruṣa, beyond the three guṇas. Mahābhārata 12.321.28-29**

Śrutis also describe the antaryāmī as a pure being.

*eko devaḥ sarva-bhūteṣu gūḍhaḥ*  
*sarva-vyāpī sarva-bhūtāntarātmā*  
*karmādhyakṣaḥ sarva-bhūtādhivāsaḥ*  
*sākṣī cetaḥ kevalo nirguṇaś ca*

**The Lord alone has entered all beings. He is all-pervading. He is the** ***antaryāmī*** **of all beings. He is the controller of karma. He is the basis of all beings. He is the witness and the giver of consciousness. He is the possessor of** ***śakti.*** **He is beyond the** ***guṇas. Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad*** **6.11**

*ajām ekāṁ lohita-śukla-kṛṣṇāṁ*  
*bahvīḥ prajāḥ janayantīṁ sa-rūpāḥ*  
*ajo hy eko juṣamāṇo 'nuśete*  
*jahāty enāṁ bhukta-bhogam ajo 'nyaḥ*

**One unborn entity, satisfied, follows another unborn entity--a female entity who is red, white and black and who produces many offspring of similar nature. Another male gives up this female who has been enjoyed. Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad 4.5**

Therefore the two verses quoted at the beginning starting with kṣetrajña etā manaso vibhūtīh, spoken by the brāhmaṇa to Rahūgaṇa were correctly explained (showing two knowers of the field, jīva and Paramātmā).

## 2.

Three forms of Paramātmā

The qualification for the manifestation of Paramātmā should be understood to be bhakti, as previously mentioned. There are three types of appearance, as explained in Nārada-tantra:

*viṣṇos tu trīṇi rūpāṇi puruṣākhyāny atho viduḥ*  
*prathamaṁ mahataḥ sraṣṭṛ dvitīyaṁ tv aṇḍa-saṁsthitaṁ*  
*tṛtīyaṁ sarva-bhūtasthaṁ tāni jñatvā vimucyate*

**The wise know that the** ***puruṣa*** **consists of three forms of the original Viṣṇu. The first is the creator of the** ***mahat-tattva*****. The second is situated within the universe. The third is situated within each living entity. Knowing these three, one becomes liberated.**

The first form is the single witness of the jīva and prakṛti when they come together in the totality of all universes. This form is described in yathāgneḥ kṣudrā visphuliṅgā vyuccaranti (as small sparks emanate from a fire) and sa aikṣata (he glanced.) He is called Saṅkarśaṇa or Mahāviṣṇu. Brahma-saṁhita explains this:

*yathā tal-liṅgaṁ bhagavān śambhur jyotiḥ-rūpaḥ sanātanaḥ*

**The Lord’s eternal form called Śambhu, whose body is consciousness, is represented figuratively by the** ***liṅgam.***

*tasminn āvirabhūl liṅge mahā-viṣṇur jagat-patiḥ*

**Having appeared with an indistinct form, Maha-viṣṇu then appeared with a distinct form as the master of all living entities in the universe.**

His form is described in Brahma-samhitā starting with the words sahasra-śīrṣā puruṣa.

*nārāyaṇaḥ sa bhagavān āpas tasmāt sanātanāt*  
*āvirāsīt kāraṇārṇo- nidhiḥ saṅkarṣaṇātmakaḥ*  
*yoga-nidrāṁ gatas tasmin sahasrāṁśaḥ svayaṁ mahān*

**The Supreme Lord is called Nārāyaṇa because the waters of the Kāraṇa ocean arise from his eternal form. Nārāyaṇa is non-different from Saṅkarṣaṇa. This Mahā-viṣṇu lies in** ***yoga-nidrā*** **in this ocean. Countless** ***avatāras*** **reside in him. Brahma-saṁhitā 5.12**

*tad-roma-bila-jāleṣu bījaṁ saṅkarṣaṇasya ca*  
*haimāny aṇḍāni jātāni mahā-bhūtāvṛtāni tu*

**The** ***jīvas*** **in combination with** ***prakṛti*** **had been resting within the hair holes of Saṅkarṣaṇa (Mahā-viṣṇu). They then take the form of golden eggs, the primitive forms of universes, covered with the five great elements before they combine. Brahma-saṁhitā 5.13**

Śambhu (meaning parent) is special aṁśa (liṅga) of Lord Govinda (expressed in yasyāyutāyutāṁśāṁṣe viśva-śaktīr iyam sthitā, Viṣṇu Purāṇa 1.9.53 quoted above), since Govinda manifests the puruṣa forms. This expansion is called liṅga, or special expansion.

The second puruṣa is the antaryāmī of all jīvas in a particular universe. Tat sṛṣṭvā tad evānupraviśat: having created the universe the Lord entered into it. (Taittirīya Upaniṣad 2.62.) There are many forms of these souls of the universes, since there are many universes. Sometimes the subtle antaryāmī is called Pradyumna and the gross form is called Aniruddha. These forms related to creation are aṁśas of forms of Saṅkarṣaṇa, Pradyumna and Aniruddha situated in Mahā-vaikuṇṭha. And the forms which act as the presiding deities of citta, ahaṅkāra, intelligence and mind (Vāsudeva Caṅkarṣaṇa, Pradyumna and Aniruddha) are also aṁśas of the Vaikuṇṭha forms.

The third purusa, the antaryāmī of the individual jīva is described as follows:

*dvā suparṇā sayujā sakhāyā*  
*samānaṁ vṛkṣaṁ praiṣasvajāte*  
*tayor anyaḥ pippalaṁ svādv atty*  
*anaśnann anyo 'bhicakāśīti*

**Two beautiful birds associate in the same tree as friends. Of the two, one relishes eating the fruit. The other, not eating, shines. Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad 3.1.1**

He has many forms since there are many jīvas.

The manifestation of the first puruṣa is described as follows:

*ādyo ’vatāraḥ puruṣaḥ parasya*  
*kālaḥ svabhāvaḥ sad-asan manaś ca \|*  
*dravyaṁ vikāro guṇa indriyāṇi*  
*virāṭ svarāṭ sthāsnu cariṣṇu bhūmnaḥ \|\|*

**Mahā-viṣṇu, an expansion of the Lord of Vaikuṇṭha, is time,** ***svabhāva*****, effect and cause,** ***mahat-tattva*****, the five gross elements, false ego, the three** ***guṇas*****, the senses, the universal form, the totality of** ***jīvas,*** **the individual j*****īvas*** **as non-moving and moving beings. All of these are related to the Supreme Lord. SB 2.6.42**

The instigator of prakṛti (puruṣaḥ) is an expansion of the Lord (parasya). He who was described as having a thousand heads is the pastime form of the Lord, this first avatāra in the material world (ādyo’vatāraḥ). Here and elsewhere the word avatāra refers to the Lord appearing within the material realm known as ekapada-vibhūti. The above verse was spoken by Brahmā to Nārada.

## 3.

The second purusa is described as follows:

*kālena so ’jaḥ puruṣāyuṣābhi-*  
*pravṛtta-yogena virūḍha-bodhaḥ*  
*svayaṁ tad antar-hṛdaye ’vabhātam*  
*apaśyatāpaśyata yan na pūrvam*  
*mṛṇāla-gaurāyata-śeṣa-bhoga-*  
*paryaṅka ekaṁ puruṣaṁ śayānam*

**By his practice of meditation on the Lord for a hundred human years he attained realization of the Lord. He saw the Lord, who manifested himself in his heart, who could not be seen previously by searching. He saw the one Lord sleeping on the broad bed of Śeṣa, white like a water lily, floating upon the waters of devastation. SB 3.8.22**

This puruṣa with a thousand heads is situated on the Garbodhaka Ocean. He is Pradyumna. Puruṣayuṣā means “for a hundred years.” Brahmā performed bhakti-yoga for a hundred years.

*parārdhya-keyūra-maṇi-praveka-*  
*paryasta-dordaṇḍa-sahasra-śākham*  
*avyakta-mūlaṁ bhuvanāṅghripendram*  
*ahīndra-bhogair adhivīta-valśam*

**As a sandalwood tree is decorated with fragrant flowers and branches, the Lord’s body was decorated with valuable jewels and pearls. As the sandalwood tree spreads its branches everywhere, the Lord spreads his arms over the universe. As the tree’s root cannot be seen, the Lord has pradhāna beneath him (avyakta-mūlam). As the sandalwood tree is the king of trees, the Lord protects the world by his great power. He controls (indram) the fourteen worlds which are his feet. As a sandalwood tree is covered with many snakes, so the Lord’s shoulders were touched by the hoods of Ananta. SB 3.8.29**

Avyakta-mūlam in SB 3.8.29 means he had pradhāna beneath him. He controls (indram) the fourteen worlds which are protected by his feet (bhuvanāṅghri-pāḥ). This description is from a conversation between Maitreya and Vidura.

## 4.

The third purusa is described as follows:

*kecit sva-dehāntar-hṛdayāvakāśe*  
*prādeśa-mātraṁ puruṣaṁ vasantam \|*  
*catur-bhujaṁ kañja-rathāṅga-śaṅkha-*  
*gadā-dharaṁ dhāraṇayā smaranti \|\|*

**Some** ***yogīs*** **meditate upon the Paramātmā measuring one** ***pradeśa,*** **who is residing in the heart within the body, and who holds the lotus, wheel, conch and club in his four hands. SB 2.2.8**

A pradeśa is the distance between the forefinger and the thumb. hṛdy apekṣayā tu manuṣyādhikāratvāt: the Lord is said to be the size of a thumb because of the heart size of humans, who are qualified for meditation in the heart. (Brahma-sūtra 1.3.25) This verse is spoken by Śukadeva.

## 5.

Though the puruṣas are many they are actually one. An example is given to illustrate this.

*yathānilaḥ sthāvara-jaṅgamānām*  
*ātma-svarūpeṇa niviṣṭa īśet*  
*evaṁ paro bhagavān vāsudevaḥ*  
*kṣetrajña ātmedam anupraviṣṭaḥ*

**Just as the air enters all moving and stationary beings as** ***prāṇa*** **and controls them, the Supreme Lord Vāsudeva, the knower of all bodies, pervading everywhere, enters this universe. SB 5.11.14**

Ātmā-svarūpeṇa means “with the prāṇa.” Īṣet means “he controls.” Idam refers to the universe. Śruti says:

*eko devaḥ sarva-bhūteṣu gūḍhaḥ*  
*sarva-vyāpī sarva-bhūtāntarātmā*  
*karmādhyakṣaḥ sarva-bhūtādhivāsaḥ*  
*sākṣī cetā kevalo nirguṇaś ca*

**The Lord alone has entered all beings. He is all-pervading. He is the** ***antaryāmī*** **of all beings. He is the controller of karma. He is the basis of all beings. He is the witness and the giver of consciousness. He is the possessor of** ***śakti.*** **He is beyond the** ***guṇas. Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad*** **6.11**

This verse is spoken by Jaḍa-bharata to Rahūgaṇa.

## 6.

This oneness is further explained.

*eka eva paro hy ātmā sarveṣām api dehinām*  
*nāneva gṛhyate mūḍhair yathā jyotir yathā nabhaḥ*

**Those who are bewildered perceive the Paramātmā, residing in all embodied beings, as many, just as one may perceive reflections of light or sky in pots. SB 10.54.44**

Dehīnām means the jīvas. Ātmā means Paramātmā. Baladeva speaks to Rukmiṇī.

## 7.

*eka eva paro hy ātmā bhūteṣv ātmany avasthitaḥ*  
*yathendur uda-pātreṣu bhūtāny ekātmakāni ca*

**The one Paramātmā is situated within all material bodies and within everyone’s soul, just as the moon is reflected in innumerable reservoirs of water. Every material body is the effect of Paramātmā. SB 11.18.32**

One supreme (para) ātmā lives in all jīvas (bhūteṣu) in his svarūpa (ātmani) and is not contaminated like the jīvas. Paramātmā is identified (ekātmakāni) with the jīva’s body since he is its cause. The Lord speaks this verse to Uddhava.

## 8.

Guṇāvatāras

Having explained that the one puruṣa has many forms, now the puruṣa’s aṁśas will be described. There are two types of aṁśas: svāṁśa and vibhinnāṁśa. The vibhinnāṁśas are the jīvas, composed of taṭastha-śakti. Svāṁśas are various with types like guṇāvatāras and līlāvataras. The līlāvataras will be explained in Kṛṣna-sandarbha in the appropriate place.

The guṇāvataras (of the second puruṣa) are described as follows:

*ādāv abhūc chata-dhṛtī rajasāsya sarge*  
*viṣṇuḥ sthitau kratu-patir dvija-dharma-setuḥ*  
*rudro ’pyayāya tamasā puruṣaḥ sa ādya*  
*ity udbhava-sthiti-layāḥ satataṁ prajāsu*

**In the beginning, the** ***puruṣa*** **appeared as Brahmā through** ***rajoguṇa*** **for creation, as Viṣṇu, the lord of sacrifice, and protector of** ***dharma*** **of the** ***brāhmaṇas*****, for maintenance, and as Śiva through** ***tamo-guṇa*** **for destruction. In this way creation, maintenance and destruction of the living entities takes place at all times. SB 11.4.5**

The first puruṣa, controller of the three guṇas at once (sa ādyaḥ puruṣaḥ) became (abhūt) Brahmā (śata-dhṛtiḥ) for creation of the universe (asya sarge) by rajoguṇa (rajasā) in the beginning (adau), as a pastime for situating himself in the guṇas. He became Viṣṇu, being situiated (stithau) by sattva-guṇa. Sattva-guṇa however is not directly mentioned in order to say that it is improper to indicate Viṣṇu, who is completely beyond the guṇas, has even an indirect relationship with the guṇas. Viṣṇu is the giver of results when he acts as the protector of the jīvas (kratu-patiḥ). Brahmā includes Viṣṇu’s form of Yajña as a līlāvatāra in the second canto. He is the protector (setuḥ) of the dharma of the twice born. He became Rudra for destruction of the universe by tamoguṇa. In this way, creation, maintenance and destruction take place. This verse was spoken by Drumila to King Nimi.

In Mokṣa-dharma the appearances of Brahmā and Śiva are also described.

*brāhme rātri-kṣaye prāpte tasya hy amita-tejasaḥ*  
*prasādāt pradurabhavat padmaṁ padma-nibhekṣaṇa*  
*tato brahmā samabhavat sa tasyaiva prasāda-jaḥ*  
*ahnaḥ kṣaye lalāṭāc ca suto devasya vai tathā*  
*krodhāviṣṭasya samjajñe rudraḥ saṁhāra-kārakaḥ*

**At the end of Brahmā’s night, the Lord produces a lotus out his unlimitedly powerful mercy. Brahmā, receiving the Lord’s mercy, appears in that lotus. At the end of his day, his son Śiva, the destroyer, appears from his forehead when Brahmā becomes angry. Mahābhārata 12.328.15-16**

The appearance of Viṣṇu is described in the Third Canto:

*tal loka-padmaṁ sa u eva viṣṇuḥ*  
*prāvīviśat sarva-guṇāvabhāsam*  
*tasmin svayaṁ vedamayo vidhātā*  
*svayambhuvaṁ yaṁ sma vadanti so ’bhūt*

**O Vidura! Viṣṇu entered that lotus of all the planets, which reveals all the enjoyable objects for the** ***jīvas.*** **Brahmā, known as self-born, composer of the Vedas, appeared within that lotus. SB 3.8.15**

The puruṣa who is Nārāyaṇa or Viṣṇu entered, in possession of his full powers (prāvīviśat), the lotus constituted of the planets (loka-padmam) meant for producing objects for the jīvas’ enjoyment (sarva-guṇāvabhāsam). Brahmā appears in the lotus, put there by Viṣṇu, in order to carry out creation, from elements already there, like a potter making pots out of clay. The same is described in other places as well:

*sattvaṁ rajas tama iti prakṛter guṇās tair*  
*yuktaḥ parama-puruṣa eka ihāsya dhatte \|*  
*sthity-ādaye hari-viriñci-hareti saṁjñāḥ*  
*śreyāṁsi tatra khalu sattva-tanor nṝṇāṁ syuḥ \|\|*

**The one supreme** ***puruṣa*****, accepting the** ***guṇas*** **of** ***prakṛti*** **known as** ***sattva, rajas*** **and** ***tamas,*** **for creation, maintenance and destruction, is called Viṣṇu, Brahmā and Śiva. The best results for the devotees will come from Viṣṇu with** ***śuddha-sattva*** **body. SB 1.2.23**

## 9.

Thus it is said:

*yo vā ahaṁ ca giriśaś ca vibhuḥ svayaṁ ca*  
*sthity-udbhava-pralaya-hetava ātma-mūlam*  
*bhittvā tri-pād vavṛdha eka uru-prarohas*  
*tasmai namo bhagavate bhuvana-drumāya*

**I offer my respects to the Supreme Lord, the tree of the worlds, who is Brahmā, the cause of creation, Śiva, the cause of destruction and independent Viṣṇu, the cause of maintenance, and who, though one, after dividing up** ***pradhāna*****, increases into three branches (*****guṇāvatāras*****), and then into extended branches (Prajāpatis and Manus). SB 3.9.16**

Tripat means the three branches: Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Śiva. The Lord is described as a tree, with the guṇāvatāras as branches. Brahmā speaks to Garbodakaśāyī Viṣṇu.

## 10.

The appearance of the three guṇāvatāras in the world is described:

*tapyamānaṁ tri-bhuvanaṁ prāṇāyāmaidhasāgninā*  
*nirgatena muner mūrdhnaḥsamīkṣya prabhavas trayaḥ*  
*apsaro-muni-gandharva-siddha-vidyādharoragaiḥ*  
*vitāyamāna-yaśasas tad-āśrama-padaṁ yayuḥ*

**Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Śiva, spreading great fame, seeing that the three worlds, like fuel, were burning because of the fire emanating from the sage’s head by the fire of** ***prāṇāyāma,*** **went to his hermitage along with Gandharvas, Siddhas Vidyādharas and the Nāgas. SB 4.1.22-23**

The sage mentioned here is Atri. Maitreya speaks the verse.

## 11.

*sarasvatyās taṭe rājann ṛṣayaḥ satram āsata*  
*vitarkaḥ samabhūt teṣāṁ triṣv adhīśeṣu ko mahān*

**Once, O King, as a group of sages were performing a Vedic sacrifice on the banks of the Sarasvatī River, a controversy arose among them as to which of the three chief deities is supreme. SB 10.89.1**

Following this there is a story involving the three avatāras. It is well known that Brahmā and others always go to the Milk Ocean where Viṣṇu resides in order to request protection of the universe, as mentioned in Padmā Purāṇa. That place is well known as Viṣṇu’s planet. In the Viṣṇu-sahasra-nāma, Viṣṇu is also called kṣīrābdhi-mandira: he who has his palace in the Milk Ocean. Aniruddha is also known as the lord Śvetadvīpa, since Aniruddha appears directly as the third puruṣa. This verse is spoken by Śukadeva.

## 12.

The relative importance of the three is revealed in that story. This is also explained elsewhere:

*sattvaṁ rajas tama iti prakṛter guṇās tair*  
*yuktaḥ parama-puruṣa eka ihāsya dhatte \|*  
*sthity-ādaye hari-viriñci-hareti saṁjñāḥ*  
*śreyāṁsi tatra khalu sattva-tanor nṝṇāṁ syuḥ \|\|*

**The one supreme** ***puruṣa*****, accepting the** ***guṇas*** **of** ***prakṛti*** **known as** ***sattva, rajas*** **and** ***tamas,*** **for creation, maintenance and destruction, is called Viṣṇu, Brahmā and Śiva. The best results for the devotees will come from Viṣṇu with** ***śuddha-sattva*** **body. SB 1.2.23**

Though there is one supreme person, when related to the guṇas, he accepts designations as Viṣṇu, Brahmā and Śiva. That means he appears as these three persons. Though he accepts these designations among them, the best results (dharma, artha, kāma, mokṣa and bhakti) arise from Viṣṇu, the deity in charge of sattva (sattva-tanoḥ).

The meaning is this. When Brahmā and Śiva are worshipped by a person with material coveriengs, dharma, artha and kāma, which arise from rajas and tamas out of great foolishness, do not give much happiness. When these two are worshipped by a person without upādhis, one can gain liberation, but not directly and not immediately. By repeated searching out and discovering that these forms are aṁśas of Paramātmā, liberation arises from Paramātmā. Brahmā and Śiva do not give the best results since since they do not reveal directly the form of Paramātmā.

Worshipping Visnu with material ideas (upādhi), dharma, artha and kāma give happiness since sattva brings about auspiciousness. However, worshipping Viṣṇu without material desires (niṣkāma-without artha, dharma and kāma) produces liberation since it is said sattva sañjāyate jñānam: knowledge arises from sattva. (BG 14.17) and kaivalyaṁ sāttvikaṁ jñānam: knowledge concerning the *jīva* apart from the body is in *sattva.* (SB 11.25.24)

Skanda Purāṇa says:

*bandhako bhava-pāśena bhava-pāśāc ca mocakaḥ*  
*kaivalyadaḥ paraṁ brahma viṣṇur eva sanātanaḥ*

**Viṣṇu binds the jīvas with the ropes of the material world and liberates the jīva from the same ropes. Eternal Viṣṇu, the supreme Brahman, is the giver of liberation.**

By completely giving up designations (without upādhi), the fifth goal, bhakti appears, since the Lord appears in the form of Paramātmā. Thus all auspicious results are obtained from Viṣṇu.

## 13.

Some rebellious persons argue, based on statements saying the three are non-different. To that we answer as follows. Though they are in different positions (in regards to guṇas) one supreme puruṣa is the controller. Thus it is impossible to take them as different. Such statements of non-differents are true in that sense. But difference is unavoidable because they are difference by begin direct and indirect means of realizing Brahman. An example is given:

*pārthivād dāruṇo dhūmas tasmād agnis trayīmayaḥ \|*  
*tamasas tu rajas tasmāt sattvaṁ yad brahma-darśanam \|\|*

**Smoke is superior to dull wood, and fire, sacred to the Vedas, is superior to smoke. Similarly** ***rajas*** **is superior to** ***tamas*****, and** ***sattva*** **is superior to** ***rajas*****, since it is favorable for realizing the Lord. SB 1.2.24**

Smoke from fire is superior to the wood (dāruṇaḥ), used to kindle the fire for sacrifice, which is inert like earth (pārthivān), not related to fire like smoke. It is devoid of manifesting action related to Vedic duties. Smoke is related to the Vedas in that it to a greater degree the place of appearance of Vedic duties than wood. Superior to the smoke is the fire, directly the place of Vedic actions. Similarly rajoguṇa endowed with some sattva, represented by smoke, which enables one to see Brahman (*brahma-darśanam*) is superior to tamoguṇa, remote from sattva-guṇa, represented by wood. Rajoguṇa allows one to see Brahman in the sense that it reveals the Vedic actions, which represent the appearance of the Lord. The word tu indicates that compared to tamas, which is fully destructive, rajas gives a slight understanding of Brahman in the form of a slight manifestation of shadow qualities, since rajas is the caue of covered (upādhika) jñāna. Complete realization of Brahman is not possible since rajas causes agitation. Sattva, represented by the fire, is directly the means of seeing Brahman: it is the means of realizing the form and qualities of Brahman directly since sattva is pure and peaceful by nature.

Thus, Brahmā and Śiva are indirect means of attaining the Lord and Viṣṇu is the direct means.

Sūta speaks the verse.

Vāmana Purāṇa says:

*brahma-viṣṇv-īśa-rūpāṇi trīṇi viṣṇor mahātmanaḥ*  
*brahmaṇi brahma-rūpaḥ sa śiva-rūpaḥ śive sthitaḥ*  
*pṛthag eva sthito devo viṣṇu-rūpī janārdanaḥ*

**The three forms of Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Śiva arise from the great Viṣṇu. Viṣṇu takes the form of Brahmā in Brahmā and the form of Śiva in Śiva. The form of Viṣṇu is however situated separately.**

It is said in Brahma-saṁhitā:

*bhāsvān yathāśma-śakaleṣu nijeṣu tejaḥ*  
*svīyam kiyat prakaṭayaty api tadvad atra \|*  
*brahmā ya eṣa jagad-aṇḍa-vidhāna-kartā*  
*govindam ādi-puruṣaṁ tam ahaṁ bhajāmi \|\|49\|\|*

**I worship the Supreme Lord Govinda who becomes Brahmā, the creator of the universe (by bestowing his powers to that** ***jīva)*****, just as the sun displays a small portion of its powers of heat and light in all the sun stones which represent it.**

*kṣīraṁ yathā dadhi vikāra-viśeṣa-yogāt*  
*sañjāyate na hi tataḥ pṛthag asti hetoḥ \|*  
*yaḥ śambhutām api tathā samupaiti kāryād*  
*govindam ādi-puruṣaṁ tam ahaṁ bhajāmi \|\|45\|\|*

**I worship the Supreme Lord Govinda who becomes the form of Śiva who is said to be non-different from Govinda, but who is also different because of his contact with the transformations of** ***prakṛti*****, just as milk becomes yogurt, which can be said to be non-different from its cause, but acts in a different manner.**

*dīpārcir eva hi daśāntaram abhyupetya*  
*dīpāyate vivṛta-hetu-samāna-dharmā \|*  
*yas tādṛg eva hi ca viṣṇutayā vibhāti*  
*govindam ādi-puruṣaṁ tam ahaṁ bhajāmi \|\|46\|\|*

**I worship the Supreme Lord Govinda who expands as Kṣīrodakaśāyī-viṣṇu (through Mahāviṣṇu and Garbodakaśāyī-viṣṇu), who is non-different from him, just as a large lamp lights up a second lamp, which lights up a third lamp -- which illuminates with the same quality of light as the original lamp.**

One should not think that Brahman becomes transformed though the word vikāra is used in the verse explaining Śiva, since transformation of Brahman is rejected in all cases.

*śrutes tu śabda-mūlavāt:* the defects of the agent do not apply to Brahman because of scriptural statements, which are the only proof concerning inconceivable subjects. Brahma-sūtra 2.1.27

It is said:

*bṛhad upalabdham etad avayanty avaśeṣatayā*  
*yata udayāstam-ayau vikṛter mṛdi vāvikṛtāt*  
*ata ṛṣayo dadhus tvayi mano-vacanācaritaṁ*  
*katham ayathā bhavanti bhuvi datta-padāni nṛṇām*

**Some define you as matter since it remains after destruction of the universe. It is like the transformation of clay into a pot. Some** ***śrutis*** **testify that the universe appears and disappears while Brahman remains unchanged. Some of the** ***śrutis*** **take you to be the mind, words and actions of this world, but, by indicating you in terms of material objects like pots, such a statment appears to be ignorance to us. SB 10.87.15**

The three examples given in Brahma-saṁhitā should be understood as follows. The sun representing the Lord, reveals himself a little in the sun stone, representing the person known as Brahmā. The Lord, represented by milk, is not fully present in the person known as Śiva, represented by yogurt. However the Lord (original flame) is fully revealed in the form of Viṣṇu (guṇāvatara) represented by a second flame.

## 14.

Three verses explain this.

*śivaḥ śakti-yutaḥ śaśvat tri-liṅgo guṇa-saṁvṛtaḥ*  
*vaikārikas taijasaś ca tāmasaś cety ahaṁ tridhā*

**Śiva is always united with his personal energy, the material nature. Manifesting himself in three features in response to the entreaties of nature’s three modes, he thus embodies the threefold principle of material ego in goodness, passion and ignorance.**

*tato vikārā abhavan ṣoḍaśāmīṣu kañcana*  
*upadhāvan vibhūtīnāṁ sarvāsām aśnute gatim*

**The sixteen elements have evolved as transformations of that false ego. When a devotee of Śiva worships his manifestation in any one of these elements, the devotee obtains all sorts of corresponding enjoyable opulences.**

*harir hi nirguṇaḥ sākṣāt puruṣaḥ prakṛteḥ paraḥ*  
*sa sarva-dṛg upadraṣṭā taṁ bhajan nirguṇo bhavet*

**The Lord, however, has no connection with the material modes. He is the Supreme Lord, the all-seeing eternal witness, who is transcendental to material nature. One who worships him becomes similarly free from the material modes. SB 10.88.3-5**

Śiva is united with his energy, with the upādhi called prakṛti in state of equilibrium of the guṇa, from the beginning, at all times (śaśvat). When the guṇas become agitated, the upādhis manifest as the three guṇas (tri-liṅgaḥ). Śiva becomes covered by the guṇas which have become manifested (guṇa-saṁvṛtaḥ). “It is said Śiva is covered by tamoguṇa. Why do you say he is covered by all the guṇas?” The material ego (aham) takes three forms of sattva, rajas and tamas. He is the ego’s presiding deity.

From that ego, controlled by the Lord’s representative, arose the sixteen elements. A person who worships (upadhāvan) Śiva, related to all types of wealth (sarvāsāṁ vibhūtīnāṁ) among all these elements (amīṣu), since he is covered by them, attains results he desires.

It is well known (hi) or for this reason (hi), Viṣṇu is not touched (paraḥ) by the coverings (prakṛteḥ). Thus he is beyond the guṇas (nirguṇaḥ). How can he be related to the guṇas? The reason is given. He is directly the lord (puruṣaḥ). There is no obstruction between this form of Viṣṇu and the Lord, as in the case of a reflection. It is said vidyāvidye mama tanū viddhi: understand that *vidyā* and *avidyā* are my *śaktis.(* SB 11.11.3) As in this verse, sometimes it is said the Lord is possessor of sattva-śakti since he is said to have a body (tanu). However the Lord handles these energies by his glance alone. He bestows knowledge (sarva-dṛk) to Brahmā, Śiva and all others and is the original witness (upadraṣṭā). Thus a person who worships Viṣṇu attains results beyond the guṇas. Śukadeva speaks these verses.

## 15.

Thus it is said that this Viṣṇu is non-different from the puruṣa.

*sṛjāmi tan-niyukto ’haṁ haro harati tad-vaśaḥ \|*  
*viśvaṁ puruṣa-rūpeṇa paripāti tri-śakti-dhṛk \|\|*

**I (Brahmā) create under his order, and Śiva destroys under his order. Holding his three energies, the Lord protects the universe as the Paramātmā. SB 2.6.32**

Śruti also explains this:

*sa brahmana srjati sa rudrena vilapayati. so 'nutpattir alaya eva harih parah paramanandah*

**The Lord, full of the highest bliss, beyond the guṇas, without birth and destruction, creates the universe through Brahmā and destroys it through Śiva. Mahā Upaniṣad**

Brahmā speaks to Nārada.

## 16.

Bhāgavatam also says:

*atrānuvarṇyate 'bhīkṣṇaṁ viśvātmā bhagavān hariḥ*  
*yasya prasāda-jo brahmā rudraḥ krodha-samudbhavaḥ*

***Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*** **describes the Supreme Lord, soul of the universe, from whose satisfaction Brahmā is born and from whose anger Rudra takes birth. SB 12.5.1**

Suta speaks the verse. Viṣṇu (Paramātmā) is not mentioned in the verse directly, since it is understood that he is non-different from the Supreme Lord (bhagavān hariḥ). Similarly in the following verse that is the implication of the words sa eva viṣṇuḥ (he who is Viṣṇu):

*tal loka-padmaṁ sa u eva viṣṇuḥ*  
*prāvīviśat sarva-guṇāvabhāsam*  
*tasmin svayaṁ vedamayo vidhātā*  
*svayambhuvaṁ yaṁ sma vadanti so ’bhūt*

**O Vidura! Viṣṇu entered that lotus of all the planets, which reveals all the enjoyable objects for the** ***jīvas.*** **Brahmā, known as self-born, composer of the Vedas, appeared within that lotus. SB 3.8.15**

Śruti also says:

*puruso ha vai narayano 'kamayata. atha narayanād ajo 'jayata. yatah prajāh sarvani bhutani*

**The puruṣa, who is Viṣṇu, desired. Then from Nārāyaṇa Brahmā was born. From Brahmā came all the living beings. Nārāyaṇa Upaniṣad**

*nārāyaṇaḥ param brahma tattvam nārāyaṇaḥ param*  
*ṛtam satyam param brahma puruṣam kṛṣṇa-piṅgalam*

**Nārāyaṇa (the puruṣa avatāra) is the supreme Brahman. Nārāyaṇa is the real object. The supreme Brahman is eternal truth. He is a person with dark complexion. Mahānārāyaṇa Upaniṣad**

*eko narayana asin na brahmā na ca śaṅkarah. sa munir bhutva samacintayat tata eva vyajāyanta viśvo hiraṇyagarbho 'gnir varuṇa-rudrendrah*

**One Nārāyaṇa (Paramātmā) existed in the beginning. Brahmā and Śiva did not exist. Being silent he meditated. From him arose the universe, Brahmā, Agni, Varuṇa, Śiva and Indra. Nārāyaṇa Upaniṣad**

Therefore it is correct to speak of the puruṣāvatāra as Viṣṇu.

## 17.

Position of Viṣṇu compared to Śiva and Brahmā

“But it is also said that Brahmā and Śiva are non-different from Viṣṇu. trayāṇām eka-bhāvānāṁ yo na paśyati vai bhidām: one should not see difference between the three of us who have one nature. (SB 4.7.54) na te mayy acyute 'je ca bhidām aṇv api cakṣate: these devotees do not differentiate between Viṣṇu, Brahmā and me. (SB 12.10.22) And in other Purāṇas it is said that seeing different between them leads to hell.”

True, we never speak of difference between them since all these have been described as being forms of the supreme Lord, being one entity. But though Śiva and Brahmā appear to have different natures, handling destruction and creation, one should not consider them to be controllers independent from Viṣṇu. Viṣṇu is the soul of the universe. (viśvātmakaḥ SB 12.5.1) Thus it is said brahmani brahma-rūpaḥ: he is the form of Brahmā within Brahmā. And we do not imagine difference between direct and indirect forms of the Lord. This difference is stated in the highest scriptures. The scriptures stating this have been given. Accepting the different between them in the scriptures, we accept Dattatreya to be the Lord and not Soma (Brahmā) or Dūrvāsa (Śiva).

In Brahma-vaivarta Purāṇa, Brahmā speaks:

*naham śivo na canye ca tac-chakty-ekaṁśa-bhaginah*  
*bāla-krīḍanakair yadvat krīḍate 'smābhir acyutah*

**I and Śiva are not supreme. We are portions of Viṣṇu’s śakti. The Lord plays with us like toys.**

In śruti it is said:

*yaṁ kāmaye tam ugraṁ kṛṇomi taṁ brahāṇaṁ tam ṛṣim taṁ sumedhām:*

**I make whomever I desire into Śiva; I make whomever I desire into Brahmā or a sage.** ***Ṛg Veda*** **10.125.05.2**

It is also said in the Vedas *mama yonir apsv antaḥ*: My (Durgā) birth is in the water. (Ṛg Veda 10.125.7) “in the water” means my birth is “from Kāraṇodaśāyī Viṣṇu who resides in the water” āpo nārā iti proktā: āpas means water. Yoniḥ means cause.

Skanda Purāṇa says:

*brahmeśanādibhir devair yat prāptum naiva śakyate*  
*tadvat svabhāvah kaivalyam sa bhavan kevalo harih*

**Perfect detachment which cannot be accomplished by Brahmā, Śiva or other devatās is achieved only by you as this is your nature, O Lord!**

It is also said that seeing Brahmā and Śiva as equal to Viṣṇu is a fault:

*na labheyuh punar bhaktiṁ harer aikantikim jaḍāḥ*  
*ekagrya-manasaś cāpi viṣṇu-sāmānya-darśinaḥ*

**The foolish people who fervently see others to be equal to Viṣṇu will not attain bhakti to the Lord. Viṣṇu-tantra**

Elsewhere it is said:

*yas tu nārāyaṇam devam brahma-rudrādi-daivataiḥ*  
*samatvenaiva vikṣeta sa pāśaṇḍi bhaved dhruvam*

**The person who views Viṣṇu to be the same as Brahmā, Śiva and others will certainly become a falled soul. Padma Purāāṇa 6.236.9**

*Madhye vānamam āsīnaṁ vice-devā upāsate*

**The Viśvadevas worship Vāmana seated in the middle. Kaṭha Upaniṣad 2.2.3**

“In other scriptures it is said that Śiva is the supreme deity.” That is true. But that is negated by carefully examining strong and weak statements in scripture. In the Padma and Śiva Purāṇas, Śiva repeats Viṣṇu’s words to Pārvatī:

*tvām ārādhya tathā śambho grahiṣyāmi varaṁ sadā*  
*dvāparādau yuge bhūtvā kalayā mānuṣādiṣu*  
*svāgamaiḥ kalpitais tvaṁ tu janān mad-vimukhān kuru*  
*māṁ ca gopaya yena syāt sṛṣṭir eṣottarottarā.*

**O Śiva! Worshipping you, I will receive this gift from you. At the end of Dvāparā yuga, you should appear among human beings and bewilder people who are opposed to me, using imaginary scriptures. Hide me so that this creation of illusion will continue in the future.**

Varāha Purāṇa says:

*eṣa mohaṁ sṛjamy āśu yo janān mohayiṣyati*  
*tvaṁ ca rudra mahā-bāho moha-śastrāṇi kāraya*  
*atathyāni vitathyāni darśayasva mahā-bhuja*  
*prakāśaṁ kuru cātmānaṁ aprakāśaṁ ca māṁ kuru*

**I will quickly create illusion. O Śiva, you who will bewilder people! Make deceptive scriptures. Reveal what is false. Reveal yourself and hide me.**

In the Purāṇas, those of tamasic nature glorify Śiva. The Purāṇas which glorify Viṣṇu give real knowledge. Sattvat sañjāyate jñānam: knowledge is produced from sattva. (BG 14.17)

Matsya Purāṇa says:

*sāttvikeṣu ca kalpeṣu māhātmyam adhikaṁ hareḥ*  
*rājaseṣu ca māhātmyaṁ adhikaṁ brahmaṇo viduḥ*

**The sages know that the glorification of the Lord is greater sattvic Purāṇas. The rajasika Purāṇas glorify Brahmā.**

*tadvad agneś ca māhātmyaṁ tāmaseṣu śivasya ca*  
*saṅkīrṇeṣu sarasvatyāḥ pitṛāṁ ca nigadyate*

**In tamasic Purāṇas, Agni and Śiva are prominently glorified and in other works, Sarasvatī and the Pitṛs are prominently glorified.**

*vaiṣṇavaṁ nāradīyaṁ ca tatha bhāgavataṁ śubhe*  
*gāruḍaṁ ca tathā pādmaṁ vārāhaṁ śubha-darśane*

**O beautiful one, it is said that the Purāṇas in the mode of goodness are: 1. Viṣṇu, 2. Nārada, 3. Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam, 4. Garuḍa, 5. Padma, and 6. Varāha.**

*brāhmāṇḍaṁ brāhma-vaivartaṁ mārkaṇḍeyaṁ tathaiva ca*  
*bhāviṣyaṁ vāmanaṁ brāhmyaṁ rājasāni nigadyate*

**"It is said that the Purāṇas in the mode of passion are: 1. Brāhmāṇḍa, 2. Brāhma-vaivarta, 3. Mārkaṇḍeya, 4. Bhāviṣya, 5. Vāmana, and 6. Brahmā.**

*mātsyaṁ kūrmaṁ tatha laiṅgaṁ śaivaṁ skāndaṁ tathaiva ca*  
*āgneyakaṁ tathaitāni tāmasāni nigadyate*

**It is said that the Purāṇas in the mode of ignorance are: 1. Matsya, 2. Kūrma, 3. Liṅga, 4. Śiva, 5. Skanda, and 6. Agni.**

In Skanda Purāṇa, Śiva tells Kārttikeya:

*śiva-śāstreṣu tad grāhyaṁ bhagavac-chāstra-yogi yat*  
*paramo viṣṇur evaikas taj jñanaṁ mokṣa-sādhanaṁ*  
*śāstrāṇāṁ nirṇayas tv eṣas tad anyan mohanāya hi*

**In the scriptures glorifying Śiva, one should accept what is in agreement with scriptures concerning Viṣṇu. Viṣṇu is the supreme Lord. Knowledge of him produces liberation. That is the conclusion of scriptures. Everything else brings about illusion.**

In Mokṣa-dharma it is said:

*1.sāṅkhyaṁ yogaḥ pañcarātraṁ vedāḥ pāśapataṁ tathā*  
*jñānāny etāni rājarṣe viddhi nānā-matāni vai*  
*2.sāṅkhyasya vaktā kapilaḥ paramarṣiḥ sa ucyate*  
*hiraṇyagarbho yogasya vettā nānyaḥ purātanaḥ*

**Know that Sāṅkhya, yoga, Pañcarātara, the Vedas and Śaiva scriptures present various philosophies. Kapila is said to be the great sage who spoke Sāṅkhya. Brahmā is the speaker of yoga long ago.**

*3. apāntaratamāś caiva vedācāryaḥ sa ucyate*  
*prācīnagarbhaṁ tam ṛṣiṁ pravadanti hi kecana*  
*4.umā-pator bhūta-patiḥ śrīkaṇṭho brahmaṇaḥ sutaḥ*  
*uktavān idam avyagro jñānaṁ pāśupataṁ śivaḥ*

**Apāntaratā is said to be the ācārya for the Vedas. Some call him Prācīnagarbha. Peaceful Śiva, the husband of Pārvatī, lord of the ghosts, son of Brahmā, with beautiful throat, spoke Śaiva philosophy.**

*5. pañcarātrasya kṛtsnasya vaktā tu bhagavān svayam*  
*sarveṣu ca nṛpa-śreṣṭha jñāneṣv eteṣu dṛśyate*  
*6. yathāgamaṁ yathā-jñānaṁ niṣṭhā nārāyaṇaḥ prabhuḥ*  
*na cainam evaṁ jānanti tamo-bhūtā viśām-pate*

**The Lord is the speaker of all of the Pañcarātra texts. O best of kings! Among all types of knowledge, in all scriptures and philosophises, the conclusion is Lord Nārāyaṇa. O king! Those who are ignorant do not know him.**

*7. tam eva śāstra-kartāraḥ pravadanti mānīṣiṇaḥ*  
*niṣṭhāṁ nārāyaṇam ṛṣiṁ nānyo 'stīti vaco mama*  
*8. niḥsaṁśayeṣu sarveṣu nityaṁ vasati vai hariḥ*  
*saṁśayān hetu-valān nādhyāvasati mādhavaḥ*

**The wise writers of scriptures proclaim the sage Nārāyāṇa to be the conclusion. I speak nothing else. The Lord dwells constantly in persons having no doubt. Mādhava does not reside in persons who are doubtful and are prone to logic.**

*9. pañcarātra-vido ye tu yathā krama-parā nṛpa*  
*ekānta-bhāvopagatās te hariṁ praviśanti vai*

**Those who know Pañcarātra scriptures through parampara, attaining pure bhāva for the Lord, attain the Lord.**

*10. saṅkhyaṁ yogaś ca sanātane dve vedāś ca sarve nikhile 'pi rājan*  
*sarvaiḥ samastair ṛṣibhir nirukto nārāyaṇo viśvam idam purāṇam*

**O king! Sāṅkya, yoga and all the Vedas are eternal. All the sages proclaim Nārāyaṇa.**

Apāntaratamā is the name of Veda Vyāsa in a different birth. The verses should be explained as follows. Various philosophies are shown in the above verses in order to state that Nārāyaṇa approved by Pañcarātra is the highest form. Pañcarātra is considered the best. Dvau bhūta-sargau loke ‘smin daiva āsura eva ca: There are two types of beings, the devatās and the demons. (BG 16.16) It should be understood that the various philosophies mentioned above follow the nature of the demons. Those who are devatā in nature conclude that Nārāyaṇa, as stated in Pañcarātara is supreme, by examining all scriptures. (verse 5) The demons are criticized in verse 6.

Viṣṇu-dharma and Agni Purāṇa state this:

*dvau bhūta-sargau loke 'smin daiva āsura eva ca*  
*viṣṇu-bhaktaḥ smṛto saiva āsuras tad-viparyayaḥ*

**There are two types of beings: devatās and demons. The devatās are the devotees of Viṣṇu and the demons are the opposite.**

“But in various scriptures we see various philosophies.” Verse seven answers this objection. (verse7) The writers of scriptures proclaim Nārāyaṇa. The writers of scriptures other than Pañcarātra are of two types: those with a little knowledge and those with all knowledge. Those with a little knowledge who speak one portion of the truth, like describing a small part of the ocean, speak a little about the fact that scriptures conclude in Nārāyaṇa, the complete truth. Those who have all knowledge have the following intention: we do not write scriptures to bewilder the demons but for enlightening the devotees. Seeing scriptures mixed with rajas and tamas with many difficult sādhanas, and despairing at seeing the difficulty in understanding the Vedas, the sages deeply examine the Pañcaratra which clearly states that Nārāyana is the purest truth, the essence of the meaning of the Vedas, and that his worship is blissful. That is expressed in verse 8. Therefore one should study Pañcarātra in order to understand the meaning of the Vedas. (verse 9) This is all summarized in verse 10.

Since the excellence of Nārāyaṇa is established in Pañcarātra, how much more excellent is the form of Kṛṣṇa described in Bhāgavatam, which is taught uniquely many times in verses like ātmārāmaś ca munuyaḥ (SB 1.7.10)

According to what has been said, Śiva is not supreme among Brahmā, Viṣṇu and Śiva. The Śaiva scriptures eqaute Bhagavān and the puruṣavatāras with Śiva in order glorify the Lord. When the question arose concerning the positions three guṇa avatāras in Bhāgavatam, the crest jewel of all scriptures, Śiva is shown to be different from the Lord and puruṣa avatāras.

Verses like the following occur:

*brahmādayo yat-kṛta-setu-pālā*  
*yat-kāraṇaṁ viśvam idaṁ ca māyā*  
*ājñā-karī yasya piśāca-caryā*  
*aho vibhūmnaś caritaṁ viḍambanam*

**Even Brahmā and others observe the rules created by Śiva. He is the cause of this universe, and** ***māyā*** **is dependent on him, following his order. His conduct as a ghost is surprising. This great Lord is only appearing to be like a ghost. SB 3.14.29**

It should be understood that here Śiva is equated with the Lord because he is a guṇa avatāra.

But there is the following statement as well in the Eighth Canto describing Śiva as supreme:

*na te giri-trākhila-loka-pāla-*  
*viriñca-vaikuṇṭha-surendra-gamyam*  
*jyotiḥ paraṁ yatra rajas tamaś ca*  
*sattvaṁ na yad brahma nirasta-bhedam*

**O protector of mountains (Śiva)! Your supreme light known as Brahman which is devoid of** ***rajas, tamas*** **and** ***sattva*** **and devoid of distinctions is not known by the** ***devatās,*** **Viṣṇu or Indra. SB 8.7.31**

But Śiva is not supreme. Such statements are made according to the Vedic reasoning that “the devatās should increase in strength by praising them with greatness.” This praise is given so that Śiva will destroy the kālakūtā poison. prīte harau bhagavati prīye 'haṁ sacarācaraḥ: when the Lord is pleased, I (Śiva) am also pleased, along with all other living creatures. (SB 8.7.40)

In the Ninth Canto and Fifth canto Śiva states the position of Viṣṇu:

*vayaṁ na tāta prabhavāma bhūmni*  
*yasmin pare 'nye 'py aja-jīva-kośāḥ*  
*bhavanti kāle na bhavanti hīdṛśāḥ*  
*sahasraśo yatra vayaṁ bhramāmaḥ*

**My dear son! We cannot compete with the Supreme Lord within whom the thousands of universes in which we wander in bewilderment arise and are destroyed with time. SB 9.4.56**

*ete vayaṁ yasya vaśe mahātmanaḥ*  
*sthitāḥ śakuntā iva sūtra-yantritāḥ*

**All of us—*****mahat-tattva,*** **the** ***devatās,*** **the elements and senses---are like birds tied by a thread. SB 5.17.23**

Or another meaning of SB 8.7.31 is this. The light of Śiva is the consciousness known as Paramātmā. Even the Lord cannot completely know that, for it is said:

*dyu-pataya eva te na yayur antam anantatayā*  
*tvam api yad-antarāṇḍa-nicayā nanu sāvaraṇāḥ*

**Because you are unlimited, neither the lords of heaven nor even you yourself can ever reach the end of your glories. SB 10.87.41**

According to Brahma-saṁhitā, Sadāśiva is a particular limb of the Lord and nothing else. In speaking of Govinda, the cause of all causes it is said:

*niyatiḥ sā ramā-devī tat-priyā tad-vaśaṁvadā*  
*tal-liṅge bhagavān śambhur jyoti-rūpaḥ sanātanaḥ*  
*yā yoniḥ sā parā śaktiḥ*

**Ramā, under the control of the Lord, is self-manifesting and dear to the Lord. The Lord’s eternal form called Śambhu, whose body is consciousness, is represented figuratively by the** ***liṅgam.***

*śaktimān puruṣaḥ so ’yaṁ liṅga-rūpī maheśvaraḥ \|*  
*tasminn āvirabhūl liṅge mahā-viṣṇur jagat-patiḥ \|*

**This powerful** ***puruṣa*** **who assumes an indistinct form is actually the Supreme Lord. Having appeared with an indistinct form, Maha-viṣṇu then appeared with a distinct form as the master of all living entities in the universe.**

Understanding this distinction, identifying Śiva with this form is criticized:

*athāpi yat-pāda-nakhāvasṛṣṭaṁ*  
*jagad viriñcopahṛtārhaṇāmbhaḥ \|*  
*seśaṁ punāty anyatamo mukundāt*  
*ko nāma loke bhagavat-padārthaḥ \|\|*

**Who can be called by the name Bhagavān except Mukunda whose toe-nail water purifies the universe along with Śiva and becomes** ***arghya*** **for Brahmā? SB 1.18.21**

Sūta speaks this verse.

## 18 .

Thus Śiva correctly says tasmāt nāhaṁ śivo na cānye ca tac-chakty-ekāṁśa-bhāginaḥ: I, Śiva and others, are not the Lord, but single portions of his śakti. (Brahma-vaivarta Purāṇa)

*brahmā bhavo ’ham api yasya kalāḥ kalāyāḥ*

**Great** ***devatās*** **like Brahmā and Śiva, and even the goddess of fortune and I, are simply parts of his parts. SB 10.68.37**

Balarāma speaks this verse.

## 19.

The jīva

The jīva is one among the retinue of Paramātmā. The jīva’ṣ secondary (taṭastha) characteristics were already stated in explaining kṣetra-jñā etāḥ (SB 5.11.12) Using the descriptions found in Padma Purāṇa, Jāmātṛ, an esteemed guru of the ancient Śrī sampradāya following Rāmānuja’s philosophy, has described the jīva’s primary (svarūpa) qualities. In explaining praṇava, the following is found in Padma Purāṇa, Uttara khaṇḍa (6.226.34-37):

*jñānāśrayo jñāna-guṇas cetanaḥ prakṛteḥ paraḥ*  
*na jāto nirvikāraś ca eka-rūpaḥ svarūpa-bhāk*

**The jīva is the shelter of knowledge, has the quality of knowledge, is conscious and beyond prakṛti. It has no birth and no change. It has its own single form.**

*aṇur nityo vyāpti-śīlas cid-ānandātmakas tathā*  
*aham-artho 'vyayaḥ kṣetrī bhinna-rūpaḥ sanātanaḥ*

**The jīva is small, eternal, and spreads out. It has knowledge and bliss, and the sense of “I.” It does not decrease, is the knower of the body, and is different from other jīvas eternally.**

*adahyo 'cchedya akledya aśoṣyo 'kṣara eva ca*  
*evam-ādi-guṇair yuktaḥ śeṣa-bhūtaḥ parasya vai*

**The jīva cannot be burned, cut, moistened or dried out. It cannot be destroyed. Endowed with these qualities the jīva is subordinate to the Lord.**

*makāreṇocyate jīvaḥ kṣetrajñaḥ paravān sadā*  
*dāsa-bhūto harer eva nānyasyaiva kadācana*

**The jīva is the sound m in om. The jīva is a secondary knower of the field, but always subordinate to the Lord and no one else.**

Based on this, Jamatṛ Muni teaches as follows:

*ātmā na devo na naro na tiryak sthāvaro na ca*  
*na deho nendriyaṁ naiva manaḥ prāṇo na nāpi dhīḥ*

**The jīva is not a devatā, not a human not an animal or plant. It is not a body or a sense, nor is it mind, prāṇa or intellect.**

*na jaḍo na vikārī ca jñāna-mātrātmako na ca*  
*svasmai svayam-prakāśaḥ syād eka-rūpaḥ svarūpa-bhāk*

**It is not insentient, not subject to change and not just knowledge alone. It is aware of itself and has its own individual form.**

*cetano vyāpti-śīlaś ca cid-ānandātmakas tathā*  
*aham-arthaḥ pratikṣetraṁ bhinno 'ṇur nitya-nirmalaḥ*

**The jīva is consciousness-giving, spreads through the body, and is made of knowledge and bliss. Each jīva has its own identity, and is separate from other jīvas. It is very small and eternally pure.**

*tathā jñātṛtva-kartṛtva- bhoktṛtva-nija-dharmakaḥ*  
*paramātmaika-śeṣatva- svabhāvaḥ sarvadā svataḥ*

**It is a knower, doer, and enjoyer by its very nature. By its nature it is subservient to Paramātmā at all times.**

The explanation of Jāmātri is according to Rāmānuja’s commentary.

That the jīva is a devatā, human or animal (first point of Jāmātṛ) is also rejected in Tattva-sandarbha, quoting the following verse.

*aṇḍeṣu peśiṣu taruṣv aviniściteṣu*  
*prāṇo hi jīvam upadhāvati tatra tatra*  
*sanne yad indriya-gaṇe 'hami ca prasupte*  
*kūṭa-stha āśayam ṛte tad-anusmṛtir naḥ*

***Prāṇa*** **follows the** ***jīva*** **in bodies born from eggs, wombs, seeds and perspiration. When the senses and** ***ahaṅkāra*** **cease to function in deep sleep, the fixed** ***ātmā*** **alone remains, without the covering of the subtle body, but with memory of the experience of deep sleep. SB 11.3.39**

That jīva is a material body is rejected:

*vilakṣaṇaḥ sthūla-sūkṣmād dehād ātmekṣitā sva-dṛk*  
*yathāgnir dāruṇo dāhyād dāhako ’nyaḥ prakāśakaḥ*

**The** ***ātmā*****, self-revealing (sva-dṛk), the observer (īkṣitā), is different from the subtle and gross bodies, just as fire, the burning agent and illuminator, is different from wood which is burned, since the revealer (knower) is different from the revealed. SB 11.10.8**

The jīva is different (vilakṣaṇa) from the body because it is the observer (īkṣitā), a knower (prakāśakaḥ), and knows itself (sva-dṛk). The Lord speaks this verse.

## 20.

Insentience (jaḍa) in the jīva is rejected:

*jāgrat svapnaḥ suṣuptaṁ ca guṇato buddhi-vṛttayaḥ*  
*tāsāṁ vilakṣaṇo jīvaḥ sākṣitvena viniścitaḥ*

**Waking, sleeping and deep sleep, the three functions of the intelligence, are caused by the three** ***guṇas*****. The** ***jīva is*** **ascertained to be different from these three states since it is only the witness of them. SB 11.13.27**

The Lord is also famous as the fourth state (turya) but this is different from the state of the jīva. Yā tu mayi turye sthito jahyāt: when one concentrates on me, the fourth state (turye), one gives up material consciousness. (SB 11.13.28) It is said:

*virāṭ hiraṇyagarbhaś ca kāraṇaṁ cety upādhayaḥ*  
*īśasya yat tribhir hīnaṁ turīyaṁ tat padaṁ viduḥ*

**When the Lord is devoid of the three forms of Kṣīrodakaśāyī, Garbhodakaśāyī and Karaṇodakaśāyī, he is known as the fourth form (Vāsudeva). Śrīdhara Svāmī commentary on SB 11.15.16**

The fourth form refers to Vāsudeva of the catur-vyūha. The Lord speaks the above verse.

## 21.

Change (vikāra) in the jīva is rejected:

*visargādyāḥ śmaśānāntā bhāvā dehasya nātmanaḥ*  
*kalānām iva candrasya kālenāvyakta-vartmanā*

**The various phases of one’s material life, beginning with birth and culminating in death, are all properties of the body and do not affect the soul, just as the apparent waxing and waning of the moon does not affect the moon itself. Such changes are enforced by the imperceptible movements of time. SB 11.7.48**

The appearance and disappearance of the various phases of the moon occurs because of watery nature of the moon globe and the reflection of the sun’s rays. The moon itself does not change. Similarly the body changes its state because of invisible time. But the ātmā does not change. Dattātreya speaks to Yadu.

## 22.

The ātmā is not just knowledge itself (na jñāna-mātrātmaka) according to Padma Purāṇa. Though it is knowledge itself, it also possesses the power of knowing (jñāna-śakti), just as light possesses the power of illumination.

*nātmā jajāna na mariṣyati naidhate 'sau*  
*na kṣīyate savana-vid vyabhicāriṇāṁ hi*  
*sarvatra śaśvad anapāyy upalabdhi-mātraṁ*  
*prāṇo yathendriya-balena vikalpitaṁ sat*

**The** ***jīva*** **does not undergo birth, death, growth, or deterioration since he is the seer of all conditions of changing bodies. That** ***jīva*** **is knowledge alone (upalabdhi-mātram), existing constantly in the body, but is endowed with change by the power of the senses, just as** ***prāṇa*** **is one entity, but moves from body to body. SB 11.3.37**

This is shown in Tattva-sandarbha. Thought the jīva is knowledge itself or awareness itself, clearly it has the power of knowing objects. It was also said at the beginning śuddho vicaṣṭe hy aviśuddha-kartuḥ: the jīva, though pure, becomes absorbed in the covering on the *jīva* known as the mind, which performs actions opposed to the Lord and which is made of *māyā.* (SB 5.11.12)

It is described in another way:

*guṇair vicitrāḥ sṛjatīṁ sa-rūpāḥ prakṛtiṁ prajāḥ*  
*vilokya mumuhe sadyaḥ sa iha jñāna-gūhayā*

**Seeing** ***prakṛti,*** **which creates the bodies of the living beings, similar to itself but with great variety through the** ***guṇas,*** **the** ***jīva*** **becomes immediately bewildered by** ***avidyā.***  **SB 3.26.5**

It should be understood that the intrinsic śakti of the jīva will give him knowledge of his real self (pratyak jñāna) whereas turning outwards (understood through the words vilokya, mumuhe and jñāna-gūhayā) the jīva in ignorance obtains material knowledge. *ajñānenāvṛtaṁ jñānaṁ tena muhyanti jantavaḥ:* The living entities are bewildered by that ignorance and blame the Lord. (BG 15.15) Kapila speaks the verse.

## 23.

Another śakti (māyā) is described:

*sa yad ajayā tv ajām anuśayīta guṇāṁś ca juṣan*  
*bhajati sarūpatāṁ tad anu mṛtyum apeta-bhagaḥ*

**The** ***jīva*** **contacts matter by the influence of** ***māyā,*** **takes on similar form due to** ***upādhis,*** **and enjoys material objects. He thus experiences** ***saṁsāra.*** **SB 10.87.38**

Śrīdhara’s commentary is as follows:

Because the jīva (saḥ) embraces ignorance (ajām) because of māyā (ajayā), it serves (juṣān) the body and senses (guṇān) or identifies with them as his self. After that (tad anu), with its qualities such as bliss (ānanda) hidden (apeta-bhāgaḥ), it adopts similar qualities and attains (bhajati) saṁsāra (mṛtum). The śrutis speak this verse.

## 24.

The bewildered jīva is further described as follows:

*tat-saṅga-bhraṁśitaiśvaryaṁ saṁsarantaṁ kubhāryavat*  
*tad-gatīr abudhasyeha kim asat-karmabhir bhavet*

**The man in the story is the** ***jīva*** **who, like a householder with an unfaithful wife (intelligence), loses all his powers in her association. What is the use of insubstantial** ***karmas*** **performed by a person who does not know his destination? SB 6.5.15**

By association with māyā, represented by a woman (taysāḥ), the jīva loses all his powers, his capacity for inherent knowledge etc. and follows her (saṁsarantam). What use are impermanent karmas for a person who does not know his svarūpa as a jīva? Haryaśvas speak this verse.

## 25.

This is further described.

*seyaṁ bhagavato māyā yan nayena virudhyate*  
*īśvarasya vimuktasya kārpaṇyam uta bandhanam*

**This** ***māyā*** **which cannot be understood by logic, which belongs to the Supreme Lord but is not his** ***svarūpa*****, is the cause of deprivation and ignorance for the** ***jīva*** **who has the possibility of knowledge and liberation. SB 3.7.9**

Īśvarasya in this verse means that the jīva has some capacity for knowledge. Maitreya speaks the verse.

## 26.

*vipralabdho mahiṣyaivaṁ sarva-prakṛti-vañcitaḥ*  
*necchann anukaroty ajñaḥ klaibyāt krīḍā-mṛgo yathā*

**Specifically conditioned by the Queen, cheated of his own nature, the foolish King, though he did not want to, followed her like a pet animal because of falling under another’s control. SB 4.25.62**

Purañjana, cheated by his queen, gave up (vañcitaḥ) his nature of knowledge and other qualities (sarva-prakṛti), not by his will, but by her will, and adopted her qualities (anukaroti) as his own. Though the jīva has a capacity for knowledge, his position of bondage is described in the following sūtra:

*parābhidhyānāt tu tirohitaṁ tato hy asya bandha-viparyayau*

**Dreams disappear by the will of the Lord. This is not surprising since the Lord (through māyā) is also the cause of bondage and liberation of the jīva. Brahma-sūtra 3.2.5**

Nārada speaks the above verse to Prācīnabarhi.

## 27.

Similar to the previous explanations given, Jāmātṛ says that the jīvas reveals itself to itself (svasmai svayaṁ prakāśaḥ). This is also made clear by words like vilakṣaṇa (jīva’s difference) and sva-dṛk (self revealing) in SB 11.10.8. There are two types of revelation: that of qualities (guṇa) and of objects (dravya). Revelation of qualities consists of the capacity of qualities to reveal the object in which they reside. Revelation of objects consists of the capacity of a special object to reveal itself and other things. Since the ātmā is an object, it belongs to this second class.

A light or an eye reveals itself and other objects by itself. It is not like a pot which requires something else to reveal it. Therefore the the light is called self-revealing (svayaṁ prakāśa). However, it does not reveal itself to itself (svasmai), since it is insentient. The ātmā however reveals itself and other objects to itself since it is conscious by nature (cid-rūpa).

The following definition is stated by others. Self revelation means not depending on another entity in carrying out its action for itself. Another definition is something capable of revealing objects, even though unconscious of itself. The first definition, being independent in its actions, means it performs actions for itself as its main quality, to distinguish the ātmā from the insentient objects like lamps (which have no intentions for themselves). The second definition is clear (indicating objects like lamps).

Thus the word sva-dṛk means “to be able to reveal objects by itself for itself.” Though jīva depends on Paramātmā for its self-revealing nature, it is not dependent like a pot on another entity’s revelation, since Paramātmā does not depend on another entity for its revelation, because he is the ultimate form.

Two verses describe this:

*mamāṅga māyā guṇa-mayy anekadhā*  
*vikalpa-buddhīś ca guṇair vidhatte*  
*vaikārikas tri-vidho ’dhyātmam ekam*  
*athādhidaivam adhibhūtam anyat*
