**O Uddhava! My** ***prakṛti*** **made of the** ***guṇas*** **produces many differences (bheda) and perceptions (buddhīḥ) of difference by the** ***guṇas.*** **Though** ***prakṛti*** **takes many forms, basically it has three:** ***adhyātma, adhidaiva,*** **and** ***adhibhūta.***

*dṛg rūpam ārkaṁ vapur atra randhre*  
*parasparaṁ sidhyati yaḥ svataḥ khe*  
*ātmā yad eṣām aparo ya ādyaḥ*  
*svayānubhūtyākhila-siddha-siddhiḥ*

**The eye, form, and the deity of the sun (a portion of the sun) mutually depend on each other to fulfill their functions in the gross eye. Paramātmā however is independent, like the sun in the sky-since Paramātmā is the sole cause of the three factors, is different from them as their cause, and is the revealer of all three since he is self-revealing. SB 11.22.30-31**

The many forms are described as adhyātma, adhibhūta and adhidaiva. Though there are many transformations by gross vision, there are three. These three are illustrated with the eye, form and a portion (vapuḥ) of the sun. Together they enter the gross eye (randhre) and mutually accomplish the act of seeing, not independently. The sun in the sky however accomplishes revelation by itself. Though perceived by the eye, the sun does not need another object to reveal itself. This would contradict the meaning of self-revealing. Just as the sun glove accomplishes on its own, so does Paramātmā (ātmā), because in the example given (yataḥ) Paramātmā which is the shelter (para ādyaḥ) of the three factors (esām) is self accomplishing in nature, because it has a conscious form (svayānubhūtyā). Not only that, Paramātmā is the cause of all mutual factors accomplishing their purpose (akhila-siddha-siddhiḥ).

The Lord speaks these verses.

## 28.

Since the jīva reveals itself by its own śakti (not depending on another entity), it has a single form (eka-rūpa-svarūpa-bhāk) as its nature, just as a lamp functions as one unit. In SB 11.3.38 already cited, in the phrase upaladbhi-mātram the word mātram indicates that its qualities emanate from its own form and nothing else.

Now, the jīva gives conscious (cetana) according to Jāmātṛ. Though it is a form of consciousness, it gives consciousness to the body, just as light from a lamp illuminates things. This was illustrated with an example in SB 11.10.8.

*vilakṣaṇaḥ sthūla-sūkṣmād dehād ātmekṣitā sva-dṛk*  
*yathāgnir dāruṇo dāhyād dāhako ’nyaḥ prakāśakaḥ*

**The** ***ātmā*****, self-revealing, the observer, is different from the subtle and gross bodies, just as fire, the burning agent and illuminator, is different from wood which is burned, since the revealer (giver of consciousness) is different from the revealed.**

Prakāśakaḥ means that that it is a giver of consciousness. This is the cause of its difference from the body. The quality of pervasion (vyāpti-śīla) is illustrated in verses like 7.7.19. The word vyāpaka in that verse means that Paramātmā has as his nature ability to enter in all conscious entities since he is most subtle.

Jāmātṛ mentions that the jīva is not just knowledge. The reason was given, but another reason is that the jīva is cid-ānandātmaka. It is knowledge as the opposite of unsentiency (jaḍa-pratiyoga) This cit quality in the jīva is not omniscience as in the Lord’s case.2 and it is bliss and knowledge (cid-ānandātmaka) as the opposite of suffering (duḥka-pratiyoga). This is different from the bliss experienced in prema.3 Its nature of knowing was already illustrated. When the ātmā knows itself, it is free of suffering.4 Jīva accomplishes his nature as bliss by being the shelter of unconditional prema.

*tasmāt priyatamaḥ svātmā sarveṣām api dehinām*  
*tad-artham eva sakalaṁ jagad etac carācaram*

**Therefore it is his own self that is most dear to every embodied living being, and it is simply for the pleasure of this self that the whole material creation of moving and nonmoving entities exists. In his present state the jīva experiences pleasure, a dull reflection of ānanda, a negation of his suffering, through actions in the material world.5 SB 10.14.54**

???

The verse is spoken by Śukadeva

## 29.

The jīva’s knowledge composed of bliss, it is not experienced second-hand “for you” as in the case of a reflection, but rather it is experienced for the self (aham-artha), since the jīva is ātmā or the self. This knowledge “for the self” is the sense of “I.” By the word “I” there is knowledge. The pure ātmā, though covered by prakṛti never becomes something else, since by the covering he merely identifies self with the body.

*evaṁ parābhidhyānena kartṛtvaṁ prakṛteḥ pumān*  
*karmasu kriyamāṇeṣu guṇair ātmani manyate*

**The** ***jīva*** **thinks himself the doer of activities which are done by the** ***guṇas*****, by false identification with** ***prakṛti.***  **SB 3.26.6**

By thinking himself prakṛti by absorption in prakṛti, (parābhidhyānena) he thinks himself to be the doer of activities done by the guṇas of prakṛti. Since it is impossible think oneself something else without first having an identity of “I” this is another type of sensing “I” for the jīva, being a covering of ahaṅkāra (sense of I) which arises from absorption in prakṛti. The sense of “I” is not a cause of saṁsāra since it is fixed in the pure svarūpa of the jīva. The two types of sensing “I” are shown in the the following:

*sanne yad indriya-gaṇe 'hami ca prasupte*  
*kūṭa-stha āśayam ṛte tad-anusmṛtir naḥ*

**When the senses and (material)** ***ahaṅkāra*** **cease to function in deep sleep, the fixed** ***ātmā*** **alone remains, without the covering of the subtle body, but with memory of the experience of deep sleep. SB 11.3.36**

Because the false identity of ahaṅkāra remains dormant in deep sleep, and because memory of the self is not destroyed, we have memory (tad-anusmṛtir naḥ) such as “I slept happily.” In that memory one does not even seek out one’s false identity. When in a state of false identity (in waking state) one seeks out one’s identity as an illusory witness. Kapila speaks the verse.

## 30.

This is also stated in the following:

*nṛtyato gāyataḥ paśyan yathaivānukaroti tān*  
*evaṁ buddhi-guṇān paśyann anīho ‘py anukāryate*

**Just as one may imitate persons whom one sees dancing and singing, similarly the soul, although never the doer of material activities, is thus forced to imitate the qualities of the intelligence. SB 11.22.53**

The Lord speaks the verse.

## 31.

This false identity is compared to seeing a dream:

*yad arthena vināmuṣya puṁsa ātma-viparyayaḥ*  
*pratīyata upadraṣṭuḥ sva-śiraś chedanādikaḥ*

**Because of** ***māyā*****, the jīva’s lack of knowledge and bliss makes its appearance without cause or purpose. The lack is illusory, just the seer of a dream experiencing his head being cut off is illusory. SB 3.7.10**

Upadraṣṭuḥ amuṣya means “of the jīva, who is the seer of the dream.” Maitreya speaks the verse.

## 32.

Since it has been proven that the sense of “I” belongs to the jīva’s svarūpa, it is also proved that this identity is different in each jīva (prati-kṣetera-bhinna). Of course statements like the following are seen:

*vastuno yady anānātva ātmanaḥ praśna īdṛśaḥ*  
*kathaṁ ghaṭeta vo viprā vaktur vā me ka āśrayaḥ*

**O** ***brāhmaṇas*****! If, when asking me who I am, you believe that I am also a** ***jīva,*** **this question cannot occur since there is no plurality of** ***ātmās*** **in the absolute. I would have to answer in terms of plurality of qualities and type. SB 11.13.22**

In this statement Haṁsa, taking the role of a regular jīva, takes the jīvas as being only one entity, imitating a common jñānī guru from a material viewpoint. Though the jīvas are all separate aṁśas, not considering this, he speaks in terms of one jīva, suitable for jñāna, for the Kumāras whor desire jñāna. A statement of oneness follows in the next verse as well:

*pañcātmakeṣu bhūteṣu samāneṣu ca vastutaḥ*  
*ko bhavān iti vaḥ praśno vācārambho hy anarthakaḥ*

**If you ask “Who are you?” in terms of the five elements, which are actually one entity spread everywhere, your question should still not be asked. My use of words (addressing you as brāhmaṇas), following your example, is also meaningless. SB 11.22.23**

Though there is difference between individual jīvas, the Lord himself says śuni caiva śvapāke ca paṇḍitāḥ samadarśinaḥ: the wise man sees as one the dog and dog-eater. (BG 5.18) Nirdoṣaṁ hi samaṁ brahma: Brahman (jīva) is faultless and one. (BG 5.19) Brahman means the jīva in this verse.

*tasmiṁs tadā labdha-rucer mahā-mate*  
*priyaśravasy askhalitā matir mama \|*  
*yayāham etat sad-asat sva-māyayā*  
*paśye mayi brahmaṇi kalpitaṁ pare \|\|*

**Having attained an excellent taste for the Lord, my intelligence did not waver from the Lord. By my intelligence, I directly experienced that my gross and subtle bodies created by the Lord’s material energy were fixed only in the Supreme Lord. SB 1.5.27**

I see the conglomeration of cause and effect of the universe (sad-asat) produced by the illusion (māyayā) concerning the jīva (sva)—I am the body and the world with Indra is the Lord--in Brahman, in me, by my intelligence. Because the cause is one, one identifies self with Brahman. I am he or you are he. Though all jīvas are one, the jīva think in terms of various bodies:

*yāvat syād guṇa-vaiṣamyaṁ tāvan nānātvam ātmanaḥ*  
*nānātvam ātmano yāvat pāratantryaṁ tadaiva hi*

**As long as the** ***jīva*** **acts through his senses and creates various destinations, he will attain a variety of bodies. As long as such bodies exist, he will remain dependent on** ***karma.*** **SB 11.10.32**

*veṇu-randhra-vibhedena bhedaḥ ṣaḍjādi-samjñitaḥ*  
*abheda-vyāpino vāyos tathā tasya mahātmanaḥ*

**By different holes in the bamboo, different notes are defined, though air spread everywhere as one entity. The great soul sees difference in this way. Viṣṇu Purāṇa 2.14.32**

The above verse expressing oneness however actually refers to Paramātmā. Considering all this, the śrutis state the difference between jīvas as their view:

*aparimitā dhruvās tanu-bhṛto yadi sarva-gatās*  
*tarhi na śāsyateti niyamo dhruva netarathā*

**O shelter of everything! If the** ***jīvas*** **were unlimited and eternal, and all powerful, the scriptures would not say that they are under your control. But the** ***jīvas*** **are not so, and thus are under your control.**

After the word “if” (yadi) aparimita (unlimited in number) and dhruva (fixed) are clear, and support the śrutis’ view. The word sarva-gataḥ (all-pervading) is contrary, and supports the opponent’s view. Eko devaḥ sarv-bhūteṣu gūḍhaḥ: the one Lord resides within all beings (jīvas.) This statement supports the view of the supreme position of Paramātmā and the unlimited number of jīvas. The śrutis speak the above verse.

## 33.

Another reason is give for the jīvas being different. They are aṇu. Aṇu means atomic. This means that though they are located in different places, they cannot be divided further. The paramāṇu is the limit of division into parts. This particle gives consciousness to the whole body because of a special power just as a small amount of medicine in ointment form placed on the head has the power to spread nourishment through the whole body or just as a magnet has power to move iron. Sūkṣmāṇām apy ahaṁ jīvaḥ: of subtle things I am the jīva. (SB 11.16.11) This means that the jīva is the limit of smallness. It is not subtle in the sense of being difficult to know. Mahatāṁ ca mahān ahaṁ sūkṣmāṇaṁ apy aham jīvaḥ: I the greatest of the great and the smallest of the small. (SB 11.1.6.11) To interpret sūkṣṁa to mean difficult to know in this verse would go against the sense of the paired statements. (The Lord speaks this verse.) Mahat-tattva is called mahat because it is the cause of all elements in the world. It spreads everywhere. The word mahat does not mean that it is easy to understand in comparison to earth or other elements. When the jīva is called sūkṣma it thus means very small. Śrutis also say eṣo ‘ṇūr ātmā cetasā veditavyo yasmin prāṇaḥ pañcadhā saṁviveṣa” the jīva is atomic, understood by consciousness. In it the prāṇa of five types rests. (Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad 3.1.9)

*Bālāgra-śata-bhāgasya śatadhā kaliptasya ca*  
*Bhāgo jīvaḥ savijñehaḥ*

**The jīva should be understood be the size of a hair tip divided ten thousand times. Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad 5.9**

*Ārāgra-mātro hy aparo ‘pi dṛṣṭaḥ*

**The jīva is the size of the tip of a spoke. Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad 5.8**

## 34.

*aparimitā dhruvās tanu-bhṛto yadi sarva-gatās*  
*tarhi na śāsyateti niyamo dhruva netarathā*  
*ajani ca yan-mayaṁ tad avimucya niyantṛ bhavet*  
*samam anujānatāṁ yad amataṁ mata-duṣṭatayā*

**O shelter of everything! If the** ***jīvas*** **were unlimited and eternal, and all powerful, the scriptures would not say that they are under your control. But the** ***jīvas*** **are not so, and thus are under your control. The product which has the qualities of the cause cannot be separated from the cause. The cause is thus the controller of the effect. Those who think you are like other objects are in ignorance since they reject the conclusion of scripture. SB 10.87.30**

The meaning is this. The śrutis say that the jīva is an aṁśa of Paramātmā and arises from him. This is confirmed in mamaivaṁśo jīva-loke: the jīvas are my aṁsas. (BG 15.7) Thus the jīva cannot be all pervading. If the jīvas (tanu-bhṛtaḥ) who are unlimited in number and eternal (aparimitāḥ) and were all pervading (sarva-gatāḥ), it would not be that there would not be a rule that they are controlled (śāsyateti niyamaḥ na syāt) since they could not be pervaded by anything else, being equal everywhere. It is not that there is no scriptural rule that the Lord is the controller and the jīva is controlled. O fixed Lord (dhruva)!

Yato vā imāni bhūtāni jāyante: from the Lord all these jīvas arise. (Taittirīya Upaniṣad 3.1) The jīvas are produced from the Lord and exist as the controlled in relation to the Lord who is the controller, since the Lord is all-pervading and the jīvas are pervaded. This is so because in all cases one sees the relationship of effect and cause. What produces (ajani) materials of this world (yan mayam) must be the controller (niyantṛ) of what is produced. It does not free (vimucya) the product at all. It pervades it completely.

A person who maintains (anujānatām) that Paramātmā, the material cause, is similar (samam) to other beings is ignorant (amatam), because his intelligence is impure (mata-duṣṭatayā). Hearing some impure opinions he speaks contrarily. Śruti says:

*asamo vā eṣa paro na hi kaścid evaṁ dṛśyate sarve tv ete naro jāyante ca mriyante ca chidrā hy ete bhavanty aparo na jāyate na mriyate sarve hy apūrṇaś ca bhavanti*

**The Lord should not been seen as equal to others. All men are born and die and have faults. The Lord is not born and does not die. All others are imperfect. Catur-veda-śikhā**

*na tat-samaś cābhyadhikaś ca dṛśyate*

**No one is equal or greater than the Lord. Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad 6.8**

*atha kasmād ucyate brahma bṛṁhati bṛhmayati ca*

**Why is he called Brahman? He increases and causes increase.**

*bṛhatvād brṁhaṇatvāc ca yad brahma paramaṁ viduḥ*

**They know the Lord as the supreme Brahman because he is great and nourishes others. Viṣṇu Purāṇa 1.12.57**

Therefore Paramātmā alone is all pervading.

*eko devaḥ sarva-bhūteṣu gūḍhaḥ sarva-vyāpī sarva-bhūtāntarātmā*

**The one Lord is within all beings. He is all pervading, and resides as the soul of all beings. Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad 6.11**

Therefore the jīva is very small. The jīva is described as nityaḥ sarva-gataḥ sthānūḥ in the Gītā. Sthāṇuḥ means jīva, a particle (aṇuḥ) who is situated in or takes shelter (sthā) of the Lord. Sarva-gataḥ primarily refers to Bhagavān, who is all pervading. In relation to the jīva, it means the jīva’s enter all types of bodies. 6

The śrutis speak the Bhāgavatam verse.

## 35.

The jiva has a pure svarūpa (nitya-nirmalaḥ). That was already illustrated in śuddho vicaṣṭe hy aviśuddha-kartuḥ: the jīva, though pure, becomes absorbed in the activities of the impure mind. (SB 5.11.12, explained at the beginning).

Even the jīva in his pure state is also a knower (jñātritva). That was illustrated also. Knowledge is eternal since it is the intrinsic quality of the eternal jīva. Therefore that knowledge is without change.

Sometimes an unconscious entity becomes the doer since one sees the body perform actions by relationship with a conscious entity. Na ṛte tvat kriyate kiñcanāre: without your will, nothing can act. (Ṛg Veda 10.112.9) Accepting that all action arises by relationship with the Lord as antaryāmī, the jīva as pure entity also in acts as a doer.

*dehendriya-prāṇa-mano-dhiyo 'mī*  
*yad-aṁśa-viddhāḥ pracaranti karmasu*

**Just as unheated iron cannot burn, the body, senses, life airs and intelligence move to their objects only when endowed with consciousness by the Lord. SB 6.16.24**

Because the initiative to act takes place by a predominance of upādhis or material conditioning, the initiative to act is often describes as a quality of the upādhis:

*kārya-kāraṇa-kartṛtve kāraṇaṁ prakṛtiṁ viduḥ*

**They say that** ***prakṛti*** **is the cause of the** ***jīva’s*** **actions, being the cause of the body, senses and sense** ***devatā*****s. Sb 3.26.8**

By the predominance of Paramātmā, jīva’s initiative to act takes place without upādhis:

*sāttvikaḥ kārako ’saṅgī rāgāndho rājasaḥ smṛtaḥ*  
*tāmasaḥ smṛti-vibhraṣṭo nirguṇo mad-apāśrayaḥ*

**A performer of action who is free of attachment is in** ***sattva*****, a performer of action overcome by sense objects is in** ***rajas*****, and a performer of action who is devoid of inquiry is in** ***tamas.*** **But a performer of action who has taken shelter of me alone is beyond the** ***guṇas.***  **SB 11.5.26**

The Lord speaks this verse.

## 36.

Just as the Lord is an enjoyer (experience) since he has consciousness, the jīva is an enjoyer since he has a form of consciousness (cid-rūpa).

*bhoktṛtve sukha-duḥkhānāṁ puruṣaṁ prakṛteḥ param*

**They say that the Supreme Lord, superior to** ***prakṛti*****, is the cause of enjoyment and suffering of the** ***jīva.***  **Sb 3.26.8**

The verb viduḥ( they know) is understood from the previous sentence. Kapila speaks the verse.

## 37.

Now the jīva as a dependent (śeṣatva) of Paramātmā will be discussed. The jīva is an aṁśa (śeṣaḥ) of Paramātmā or is secondary to Paramātmā. That is the jīva’s nature (svabhāva). This is the case at all times (sarvadā) even when the jīva is liberated. That is jīva’s svarūpa (svataḥ), not that Brahman when cut in pieces becomes jīva. By the Lord’s intrinsic inconceivable śakti, the jīva is by nature dependent as an aṁśa, like a particle of a ray of light. This is the meaning svataḥ.

Jīva has the nature of the Lord’s śakti because he is part of the taṭastha śakti, because he eternally takes shelter of the Lord, being like his ray, because he is separate from the Lord, being different, and because he is assists the production of the material world. hetur jīvo 'sya sargādeḥ: the cause of *sarga, visarga* of the universe is the j*īva.* (SB 12.7.18) As well, when pradhāna is in a state of equilibrium, the jīva remains even though it is a substance (dravya) by nature.

The following describes the jīva as a śakti of the Lord, since he is a type of prakṛti.

*viṣṇu-śaktiḥ parā proktā kṣetrajñākhyā tathāparā*  
*avidyā karma-samjñānyā tṛtīyā śaktir iṣyate*

**Viṣṇu has his spiritual śakti, the jīva-śakti, and the third śakti, acit-śakti, called avidyā-karma.**

*tayā tirohitatvāc ca śaktiḥ kṣetrajña-samjñitā*  
*sarva-bhūteṣu bhūpāla tāratamyena vartate*

**The jīva, because of being covered by the māyā-śakti, exists in various states in various bodies. Viṣṇu Purāṇa**

*apareyam itas tv anyāṁ prakṛtiṁ viddhi me parām \|*  
*jīva-bhūtāṁ mahā-bāho yayedaṁ dhāryate jagat \|\|5\|\|*

**This is my inferior energy. But understand my superior energy (prakṛti) which is different from this inferior energy. It is the** ***jīvas*****, who employ the inferior energy for their enjoyment. BG 7.4**

In the Viṣṇu Purāṇa verse, since the third śakti is separately classed, the idea that the jiva’s sakti arises from relation with māyā śakti is refuted. It exists separately as its nature. Thus the Gītā says mamaivāṁśaḥ: the jīva is my aṁśa. (BG 15.7) And thus it is said apareyam itas tv anyām: separate from the material śakti is the jīva śakti. (BG 7.5) In SB 5.11.12 (kṣetrajña etā ṁanaso vibhūtīḥ) the word kṣetrajña continues to be used even when the jīva is pure since the word kṣetra is used in a generic sense (not just material body).

Thus though jīva is a śakti it is different from the material śakti, since it is taṭastha. It is called taṭastha because it cannot be classed as māyā since it surpasses māyā-śakti (being conscious) and it cannot be classed as Paramātmā since Paramātmā is not subject to jīva’s fault of being overcome by ignorance. Though it is the śakti of Paramātmā, Paramātmā is not tinged by jīva’s faults just as the sun is not covered though one ray of the sun can be covered by shadow. Nārada-pañcarātra describes the taṭasha position:

*yat taṭasthaṁ tu cid-rūpaṁ sva-samvedyād vinirgatam*  
*rañjitaṁ guṇa-rāgeṇa sa jīva iti kathyate*

**The jīva is called taṭastha because it is a conscious form which, leaving its knowledge of itself, becomes tinged by the attraction to material guṇas.**

Thus, in Viṣṇu Purāṇa also, the jīva is classed as being in an intermediate position. In Śruti also the jīva is considered distinct from the Lord:

*asmān māyī sṛjate viśvam etat tasmiṁś cānyo māyayā sanniruddhaḥ*

**Brahmā creates this universe by the Lord’s power. In the universe, the jīva is overcome by māyā. Śvetāśvatara Upniṣad 4.9**

*tayor anyaḥ pippalaṁ svādv atti*

**Of the two, the jīva eats the fruit of the pippala tree. Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad 3.1.1**

In Viṣṇu Purāṇa it is said:

*vibheda-janake 'jñāne nāśam ātyantikaṁ gate*  
*ātmano brahmaṇo bhedam asantaṁ kaḥ kariṣyati.*

**When ignorance produced from seeing differences because of various bodies is destroyed, who would not see difference between the jīva and the Lord? Viṣṇu Purāṇa 6.7.94**

Everyone should see the difference between the jīva and the Lord. The word asantam indicates if one were to think there was no difference, it would be completely incorrect. Even in the state of liberation, the jīva does not lose his identity as an aṁśa, since he is by nature a śakti of the Lord.

In the state of liberation, on being freed from ignorance and being situated in his svarūpa, the jiva, attaining the Lord, attains qualities similar to the Lord. Śruti says nirañjanaḥ paramaṁ sāmyam upaiti: The pure jīva attains similarity to the Lord (Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad 3.1.3)

*idaṁ jñānam upāśritya mama sādharmyam āgatāḥ \|*  
*sarge ’pi nopajāyante pralaye na vyathanti ca \|\|2\|\|*

**Those who have attained qualities similar to mine, by realizing this knowledge through** ***guru,*** **do not take rebirth in this world, and do not experience pain at the time of death. BG 14.2**

Also it is said:

*brahma veda brahmaiva bhavati*

**Knowing the Lord one becomes like the Lord. Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad 3.2.9**

*tat-sābhāvyāpattir upapatteḥ*

**The jīva attains qualities similar to the Lord’s. Brahma-sūtra 3.1.23**

Being the Lord’s śakti, sometimes the two are equated, because the śakti and the śaktiman are intimately connected with each other, because in the absence of the śaktiman, śakti is also absent, and because they are both conscious entities. Still it is not improper to point out difference since one sees various śaktis in one particular object. The followers of Rāmānuja consider the designation of non-difference of jīva and the Lord, the controlled and the controller to be like designating the individual and species of a cow. Viṣṇu Purāṇa thus says:

*yo 'yaṁ tavāgato deva samīpaṁ devatā-gaṇaḥ*  
*sa tvam eva jagat-sraṣṭā yataḥ sarva-gato bhavān*

**O Lord! Whatever devatā comes in front of you, you are he, since you are the creator of the universe and pervade everywhere.**

*sarvaṁ samāpnoṣi tato pi sarvaḥ*

**You pervade everywhere, and therefore you are everything. BG 11.40**

For those desiring jñāna, the Lord teaches scriptures of non-difference and for the devotee, the Lord teaches scriptures of difference. Sometimes the jīva is called a reflection of the Lord.

*yathā puruṣa ātmānam ekam ādarśa-cakṣuṣoḥ*  
*dvidhābhūtam avekṣeta tathaivāntaram āvayoḥ*

**Just as a man, though one entity, will the image of himself in a mirror and in the eye are different, you should see that I and you are also different. SB 4.28.63**

This again is said to aid the vision of non-difference for those desiring jñāna. The jīva is not actually a reflection of the Lord. Even the proponents of non-difference must see the contradiction in such an example. Ambuvad agrahaṇād na tu tathatvam: there is no identity because one does not see distance between the Lord and the jīva as one sees distance between the sun and water to cause the reflection. (Brahma-sūtra 3.2.19)

The example of the reflection is accepted because of some similarity only.

*vṛddhi-hrāsa-bhāktvam antarbhāvād ubhaya-sāmañjasyād evam*

**The comparison with the sun is accepted in the sense that it illustrates the nature of greatness of the Lord and dependence of the jīva, since this is included in the scriptural conclusion and harmonizes the elements of the comparison. Brahma-sūtra 3.2.20**

Gītā also shows that the jīva is eternally an aṁśa of Parmātmā. Mamaivāṁśo jīva-loke jīva-bhūtaḥ sanātanaḥ: the jīvas are my aṁśas eternally. (BG 15.6) Even the totality of jīvas (samaṣṭi) is called aṁśa:

*eṣa hy aśeṣa-sattvānām ātmāṁśaḥ paramātmanaḥ*  
*ādyo ’vatāro yatrāsau bhūta-grāmo vibhāvyate*

**This universal form is the soul of unlimited beings. It is aṁśa of Paramātmā. It is seen as the original** ***avatāra.*** **Within this form, all living beings appear. SB 3.6.98**

(aṁśa) of the individual beings (vyaṣṭi), since it is an aṁśa of the Lord. This form is called avatāra since Nārāyaṇa appears in him. Śuka speaks the verse.

## 38.

The individual jīva (in contrast to the totality of jīvas or samaṣṭi mentioned in the previous verse) is described:

*ekasyaiva mamāṁśasya jīvasyaiva mahā-mate*  
*bandho ’syāvidyayānādir vidyayā ca tathetaraḥ*

**O intelligent Uddhava! The bondage of the** ***jīva,*** **who is my one part or** ***taṭastha-śakti,*** **is created by** ***avidyā*** **and is without beginning. By** ***vidyā*****, he achieves liberation which has a beginning. SB 11.11.4**

Itaraḥ means liberation. The individual jīva (vyaṣṭi) is compared to a small ray. He who identifies himself as all beings is called the samaṣṭī or total. The Lord speaks the verse.

## 39.

The jīva is called an aṁśa because he is a śakti. That is described:

*sva-kṛta-pureṣv amīṣv abahir-antara-saṁvaraṇaṁ*  
*tava puruṣaṁ vadanty akhila-śakti-dhṛto ’ṁśa-kṛtam*

**The individual living entity, while inhabiting the material bodies he has created for himself by his karma, actually remains uncovered by either gross or subtle matter. This is so because, as the Vedas describe, he is part (aṁśa) of you, the possessor of all potencies. SB 10.87.20**

The śrutis say that the jīva is not covered by external and internal cause and effect (abahir-antar-saṁvaraṇam) and is aṁśa (aṁṣa-krṭam) of you who are endowed with all śaktis including jīva-śakti. The pure Lord is not an aṁśa. Therefore jīva is an aṁśa because he is a śakti. This is the indication of the śrutis who speak this verse. Jīva’s taṭastha nature is clear from the statement sa ajayā tyajā anuśayīta: the *jīva* contacts matter by the influence of *māyā,* takes on similar form due to *upādhis,* and enjoys material objects. (SB 10.87.38) It is not matter and it is not the Lord. The śrutis speak to the Lord.

## 40.

Non-difference of jīva and the lord is described for persons desiring jñāna.

*ahaṁ bhavān na cānyas tvaṁ tvam evāhaṁ vicakṣva bhoḥ*  
*na nau paśyanti kavayaś chidraṁ jātu manāg api*

**I and you are different, but you are not something different from me. Ah! See without anger! The wise never see difference in us at all. SB 4.28.62**

Paramātmā speaks to Purañjana.

## 41.

According to the principle previously mentioned, it appears that there is one because all the elements enter each other. To show that there appears to be oneness because of jīva entering into or closely contacting Paramātmā, the following verse is spoken by the Lord:

*parasparānupraveśāt tattvānāṁ puruṣarṣabha*  
*paurvāparya-prasaṅkhyānaṁ yathā vaktur vivakṣitam*

**O best among men! Because subtle and gross elements mutually enter into one another, philosophers may desire to calculate the number of elements in terms of causes or effects according to their personal desire and thus arrive at different totals. SB 11.22.7**

Śrīdhara says in his commentary, “Some persons desire to speak of the number elements as being few or many since they merge into each other.” The Lord speaks the verse.

## 42.

The jīva and the Lord are one since they are both conscious entities:

*puruṣeśvarayor atra na vailakṣaṇyam aṇv api*  
*tad-anya-kalpanāpārthā jñānaṁ ca prakṛter guṇaḥ*

**There is little difference between the Lord and the** ***jīva.*** **To think of them as extremely different is useless.** ***Jñāna*** **arises from the** ***guṇas*** **of** ***prakṛti*** **and is thus useless for gaining proper vision. SB 11.22.11**

Why do they claim there are twenty-five elements (excluding the Lord)? This is answered with this verse. There is no difference (vailakṣaṇyam) since the jīva and the Lord are both conscious. To think of the two as completely different is wrong. By the similarlity, their relation as śakti and śaktimān is illustrated. They are thus non-different. The Lord speaks the verse.

## 43.

The Lord teaches difference for those desiring bhakti:

*yadā rahitam ātmānaṁ bhūtendriya-guṇāśayaiḥ*  
*svarūpeṇa mayopetaṁ paśyan svārājyam ṛcchati*

**When a person sees that he, the** ***jīva,*** **is completely free from the reservoir of the** ***guṇas*** **in the form of the body and senses, and has attained a relationship with me, he attains** ***dāsya-rasa*****. SB 3.9.33**

When the jīva sees himself (ātmānam) related with me (māyā upetum), the śaktimān, the sheter of the jīva-śakti, he attains sārṣṭy and other qualities. Garbodaśāyī speaks to Brahmā.

## 44.

The cause of difference is explained:

*anādy-avidyā-yuktasya puruṣasyātma-vedanam*  
*svato na sambhavād anyas tattva-jño jñāna-do bhavet*

**Because a person whether covered or not covered by beginningless ignorance cannot realize** ***ātmā*** **on his own, there must be a separate Supreme Lord who knows and gives knowledge. SB 11.22.10**

Śrīdhara Svāmī says in his commentary, “The jīva cannot realize ātmā on his own (svataḥ). The supreme Lord who knows everything knows the ātmā on his own. This indicates the proponent of twenty-six principles in the world.”

The quality which distinguishes the Lord from the jīva is the difference in his ability to know (which he can then give). Later Uddhav says tvatto jñānaṁ hi jīvānāṁ pramoṣas te ’tra śaktitaḥ: from you alone through your energy, the *jivas’* knowledge arises and disappears. (SB 11.22.28)

If the position of the Lord is made up of jīva’s ignorance (by becoming a portion under illusion), he, a false Lord, cannot give knowledge to the jīva. Thus the distinction between the Lord and the jīva must be real. The Lord himself reveals to the foolish people the fact that the jiva and the Lord are different while speaking from the position of the Lord (rather than a speculative logician).

There are many philosophies produced by thinkers as indicated in SB 11.22.9 and 11.22.4:

*yathā viviktaṁ yad-vaktraṁ gṛhṇīmo yukti-sambhavāt*

**I accept their conclusions as authoritative, because a logical explanation can always be given for each of the different theories.**

*māyāṁ madīyām udgṛhya vadatāṁ kiṁ nu durghaṭam*

**It is not difficult for disputants who have taken shelter of my** ***māyā*** **to argue. SB 11.22.4**

It is said:

*śrutiḥ pratyakṣam aitihyam anumānaṁ catuṣṭayam*  
*pramāṇeṣv anavasthānād vikalpāt sa virajyate*

**From the four types of evidence—Vedic knowledge, direct experience, traditional wisdom and logical induction—one can understand the temporary nature of the material world. Giving up doubt one remains steady in the supreme. SB 11.19.7**

This verse forbids a variety of opinions. The word vikalpa means doubt. From the four types of knowledge one becomes free of doubt, and comes steady in the real substance. Thus later it is said:

*karmaṇāṁ pariṇāmitvād ā-viriñcyād amaṅgalam*  
*vipaścin naśvaraṁ paśyed adṛṣṭam api dṛṣṭa-vat*

**The wise man should see that, because of transformations of** ***karma,*** **even the happiness on the planet of Brahma, what to speak of that experienced on earth, is temporary and inauspicious. SB 11.19.17-18**

Up to Brahmā everything is seen to be temporary. However this is not the case of Vaikuṇṭha.

## 45.

Three verses show that the jīva is dependent on the Lord as taught by Jāmātṛ-muni.

*ahaṁ mamābhimānotthaiḥ kāma-lobhādibhir malaiḥ*  
*vītaṁ yadā manaḥ śuddham aduḥkham asukhaṁ samam*  
*tadā puruṣa ātmānaṁ kevalaṁ prakṛteḥ param*  
*nirantaraṁ svayaṁ-jyotir aṇimānam akhaṇḍitam*  
*jñāna-vairāgya-yuktena bhakti-yuktena cātmanā*  
*paripaśyaty udāsīnaṁ prakṛtiṁ ca hataujasam*

**When the mind is free of the contamination of lust and greed arising from the false identity of I and mine, and becomes pure, without grief, without joy and peaceful, the** ***jīva*** **sees himself different from ignorance, full of knowledge, devoid of coverings, subtle (small), undivided, and unattached, as well as sees ignorance devoid of power to affect him, by a mind endowed with** ***jñana*** **and** ***vairāgya,*** **and principally with** ***bhakti.***  **SB 3.25.16-18**

The connection between the three verses is clear. The word aham indicates the ātmā which is nitya-nirmala. The word ātmānam (the jīva sees ātmā) indicates the sense of “I” since otherwise the jīva could not perceive himself as ātmā. The word kevalam indicates his individual form (eka-rūpa-svarūpa-bhāk). Prakṛteḥ param indicates that the jīva is without change (na vikārī). Bhakti-yuktena indicates that the ātmā is revealed by the mercy of Paramātmā. Nirantaram indicates that the jīva is an aṁśa of the Lord (paramātmaika-śeṣatvam) since his condition is eternal (nirantara). Svayam-jyoti indicates reveals that the ātmā reveals himself to himself (svasmai svayaṁ-prakāśa) and the jīva is not just knowledge (jñāna-mātrāmako na ca). Animānam indicates that the jīva is a particle or aṇu and different from other jīvas (prati-kṣetraṁ bhinna), (since he is not all-pervading). Akhaṇḍitam (indivisible) indicates the jīva is a knower, doer and enjoyer intrinsically and eteryally (jñātṛtva-kartṛtva-bhoktṛtva-nija-dharmaka) since knowledge and other qualities are never separated from him. Kapila speaks the verse.

## 46.

The following characteristics are not contradictory to what was said:

*ātmā nityo 'vyayaḥ śuddha ekaḥ kṣetra-jña āśrayaḥ*  
*avikriyaḥ sva-dṛg hetur vyāpako 'saṅgy anāvṛtaḥ*  
*etair dvādaśabhir vidvān ātmano lakṣaṇaiḥ paraiḥ*  
*ahaṁ mamety asad-bhāvaṁ dehādau mohajaṁ tyajet*

**Ātmā is without destruction, without decay, pure, the one entity, the knower of the field, the shelter of all things, without change, self-illuminating, the cause of all things, pervading his body, not influenced by matter, and not covered by anything. By contemplating these twelve features one should give up the false identity of me and mine related to the body, arising from ignorance. SB 7.7.19-20**

Avyayaḥ means without decay. Ekaḥ means without a conglomeration of parts like body and senses. Kestrajñaḥ means having the nature of a knower. Āśrayaḥ means being the shelter of senses. Avikriyaḥ means without action, since he is by nature a knower. Sva-dṛk means revealing himself to himself. Hetuḥ means the cause of creation. Sūta says:

*hetur jīvo 'sya sargāder avidyā-karma-kārakaḥ*  
*yaṁ cānuśāyinaṁ prāhur avyākṛtam utāpare*

**The cause of** ***sarga, visarga*** **of the universe is the j*****īva*** **who performs actions out of ignorance and is said to possess a material body, or, alternately, possess a spiritual body. SB 12.7.18**

Vyāpaka means pervading by nature. Asaṅgī anāvṛta means uninfluenced by matter and uncovered, since he has a form which is self-revealing. One should give up the impermanent generated from illusion related to body etc. but not the sense of “I” inherent in jīva’s svarūpa. Prahlāda speaks this verse to the sons of demons.

Thus some say that because the jīva is an aṁśa of the Lord, the jiva is compared to a small particle of light, like a part of a Kaustubha jewel. In defining the jīva, Skanda Purāṇa, Prabhāsa-khaṇḍa says:

*na tasya varṇo rūpaṁ vā pramāṇaṁ dṛśyate kvacit*  
*na śakyaḥ kathituṁ vāpi sūkṣmaś cānanta-vigrahaḥ*

**The jīva’s color, form or measureable dimension cannot be seen. One cannot describe it. The jīva is very small and innumerable in number.**

*bālāgra-śata-bhāgasya śatadhā kalpitasya ca*  
*tasmāt sūkṣmataro jīvaḥ sa cānantyāya kalpate*

**The jiva is smaller than a hundredth part of a hair tip divided a hundred times. They are unlimited in number.**

*āditya-varṇaṁ sūkṣmābhaṁ ab-bindum iva puṣkare*  
*nakṣatram iva paśyanti yogino jñāna-cakṣusā*

**The yogīs with eyes of knowledge see the jīva like a drop of water on a lotus, like a constellation or a small glowing light.**

## 47.

Thus the jīvas or taṭastha-śaktis are unlimited (ananta). There are two classes of these śaktis. One is favorable to the Lord without beginning (anāditaḥ) since these jīvas have knowledge of the Lord by their nature. The other jīvas are averse to the Lord without beginning (anāditaḥ) since they lack knowledge of the Lord by their nature. The first type of taṭastha-śakti is endowed with the manifestation of the antaraṅga-śakti and consists of the eternal associates of the Lord like Garuḍa. These are described in Padma Purāṇa, Uttara-khaṇḍa, which is quoted in Bhagavat-sandarbha, strating with the phrase tripād-vibhūter lokās tu. These jīvas of the first type are taṭastha in nature since they do not enter into the category of the Lord (īśvara) because they are established as jīvas.

The other type, overcome by māyā, which has gained access to the jīva because of his aversion to the Lord, is born repreatedly in the material world.

The Hāmsa-guhya prayers say:

*deho 'savo 'kṣā manavo bhūta-mātrām*  
*ātmānam anyaṁ ca viduḥ paraṁ yat*  
*sarvaṁ pumān veda guṇāṁś ca taj-jño*  
*na veda sarva-jñam anantam īḍe*

**The body, life airs, senses, internal senses, gross elements and sense objects do not know themselves or other things or the** ***jīva.***  **The** ***jīva*** **knows all of these items and the** ***guṇas*** **which cause them. He does not really know the omniscient Lord. I worship that Lord with infinite qualities. SB 6.4.25**

Also it is said in the Eleventh Canto:

*bhayaṁ dvitīyābhiniveśataḥ syād*  
*īśād apetasya viparyayo 'smṛtiḥ*

**For the** ***jīva*** **averse to the Lord, there will be** ***saṁsāra*** **consisting of identity with body and lack of identity with the soul, because of his absorption in the material coverings on the soul, arising from the Lord’s** ***māyā.*** **SB 11.2.37**

Already quoted Viṣṇu Purāṇa says:

*tayā tirohitatvāc ca śaktiḥ kṣetrajña-samjñitā*  
*sarva-bhūteṣu bhūpāla tāratamyena vartate*

**The jīva, because of being covered by the māyā-śakti, exists in various states in various bodies.**

Vidura speaks of both types:

*tattvānāṁ bhagavaṁs teṣāṁ katidhā pratisaṅkramaḥ*  
*tatremaṁ ka upāsīran ka u svid anuśerate*

**How many types of destruction are there for the various elements? Who remains serving the Lord during destruction, and who goes to sleep? SB 3.7.37**

Though the averse jīvas are pure, they take a material birth with coverings (upādhi) caused by the Supreme Lord who is endowed with this śakti. That material birth is a state of adhyātmā (material individuality) caused by identifying with the birth, because of appearance of the coverings.

*na ghaṭata udbhavaḥ prakṛti-pūruṣayor ajayor*  
*ubhaya-yujā bhavanty asu-bhṛto jala-budbuda-vat*  
*tvayi ta ime tato vividha-nāma-guṇaiḥ parame*  
*sarita ivārṇave madhuni lilyur aśeṣa-rasāḥ*

**Neither material nature nor the soul who tries to enjoy her are ever born, yet living bodies come into being when these two combine, pervaded by you, just as bubbles form where water and air join with** ***ākāśa.*** **And just as rivers merge into the ocean or the nectar from many different flowers blends into honey, so all these conditioned beings eventually merge back into you, the Supreme, along with their various names and qualities. SB 10.87.31**

The śrutis speak this verse in praise of the Lord. There is no birth of the prakṛti made of three guṇas or of the pure jīva (puruṣaḥ). The jīvas with upādhis or ādhyātmika forms (asu-bhṛt—living beings) are born because of Paramātmā who is endowed with these two śaktis (ubhaya-yujā). The śaktis called jīvas are pure when merged in Paramātmā since their desires are sleeping when the transformations of prakṛti are extinguished in pralaya. When the desires are agitated after prakṛti transforms at the time of creation, the jīvas attain a conditioned state and go forth into the material universe.

With this in mind it is said in the Third Canto:

*kāla-vṛttyā tu māyāyāṁ guṇa-mayyām adhokṣajaḥ*  
*puruṣeṇātma-bhūtena vīryam ādhatta vīryavān*

**Bhagavān, lord of Mahā-vaikuṇṭha, in his** ***svāṁśa*** **expansion as the** ***puruṣa*****, full of potency, at a certain moment of time, placed the** ***jīvas*** **into** ***māyā*** **which became agitated by the** ***guṇas.*** **SB 3.5.26**

The jīva (vīryam) is placed in prakṛti. Gītā says the same. Mama yonir mahad brahma tamsin garbhaṁ dadhāmy aham: I place the jīva into the womb of prakṛti. (BG 14.3) Śrīdhara Swāmī says that brahma in the Gītā verse means prakṛti and garbha means the jīva. Also in the Third Canto it is said:

*daivāt kṣubhita-dharmiṇyāṁ svasyāṁ yonau paraḥ pumān*  
*ādhatta vīryaṁ sāsūta mahat-tattvaṁ hiraṇmayam*

**The Supreme Lord placed the** ***jīvas*** **into the womb of** ***prakṛti,*** **whose** ***guṇas*** **were agitated by time.** ***Prakṛti*** **gave birth to the brilliant** ***mahat-tattva.*** **SB 3.26.19**

In this verse vīryam means cic-cakti according to Śrīdhara Svāmī. He agrees that the jīva is a śakti.

To show that the appearance of the conditioned jīva takes place suddenly, an example is given. It is like the appearance of bubbles in water. Then again at the time of destruction, all the conditioned jīvas (ime) merge into you (tvayi), Paramātmā (parame), the principle form like the sun, who is endowed with śakti of the pure jīva endowed with consciousness, like a ray. Śruti confirms this with svaṁ apīto bhavati: the jīva merges with the Lord (svam). The jīva merges into the Lord along with his various names such as devatā or human and with his qualities, good or bad.

As with taking up upādhis, an example is given of destruction of upādhis. It is like rivers merging in an ocean or various flavors merging in honey. When thej īva merges in the Lord by giving up names and forms of devatā or human, his svarūpa still remains since the jīvas are still aṁśas.

The śruti says:

*hantemās tisro devatā anena jīvenātmanānupraviśya nāma-rūpe vyākaravāṇi*

**Let me enter these three** ***devatās*** **(fire, water and earth) with the jīva and divide name and form. Chāndogya Upaniṣad 6.3.2**

*ajām ekāṁ lohita-śukla-kṛṣṇāṁ*  
*bahvīḥ prajāḥ sṛjamānāṁ sarūpāḥ*

**One unborn entity, satisfied, follows another unborn entity--a female entity who is red, white and black and who produces many offspring of similar nature. Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad 4.5**

*yathā nādyaḥ syandamanaḥ samudre*  
*astaṁ gacchanti nāma-rūpe vihāya*  
*tathā vidvān nāma-rūpad vimuktāḥ*  
*parāt paraṁ puruṣam upaiti divyam*

**Just as flowing rivers, giving up name and form, disappear in the ocean, so the man in knowledge freed from name and form attains the supreme, shining person. Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad 3.2.8**

*yathā saumyema madhu madhu-kṛto niṣṭiṣṭhanti nānā-rūpānāṁ vṛkṣāṇāṁ rasān samavahāram ekataṁ rasaṁ gamayanti. te yathā tatra vivekaṁ na labhante. amuṣyāhaṁ vṛkṣasya raso 'smy amuṣyāhaṁ vṛkṣasya raso 'smīty evam eva khalu saumyemāḥ prajāḥ sati sampadya na viduḥ sati sampadyāmahe*

**My dear boy, the partial dissolution resembles what happens when honeybees collect honey by extracting the nectar from the flowers of various kinds of trees and merge it all into a single mixture. Just as the mixed nectars cannot distinguish, ‘I am the juice of such-and-such a flower,’ or ‘I am the juice of another flower,’ so, dear boy, when all these living entities merge together they cannot consciously think, ‘Now we have merged together.’**

## 48.

Paramātmā’s śakti called taṭasthā has been described. The antaraṅga śakti should be understood as described previously (ing Bhāgavat Sandarbha). Now the bahiraṅga śakti will be described.

*eṣā māyā bhagavataḥ sarga-sthity-anta-kāriṇī*  
*tri-varṇā varṇitāsmābhiḥ kiṁ bhūyaḥ śrotum icchasi*

**I have now described** ***māyā,*** **the illusory energy of the Supreme Lord, which consists of the three modes of material nature, and causes creation, maintenance and annihilation of the material universe. Now, what more do you wish to hear? SB 11.3.16**

Antarikṣa speaks the Videha. I have described māyā (eṣā) cause of creation, maintenance and destruction, which belongs to Paramātmā (bhagavataḥ) who has qualities like aiśvarya arising from his svarūpa. It has three guṇas.

Prakṛti is also described in the Atharva śruti.

*sitāsitā ca raktaś ca sarva-kāma-dughā vibhoḥ*

**Prakṛti is black, white and red and bestows all desires of the Lord. Cūlika Upaniṣad**

Prakṛti has been described in the Gītā:

*daivī hy eṣā guṇa-mayī mama māyā duratyayā \|*

**My** ***māyā,*** **made of the** ***guṇas*****, fit for the** ***jīva's*** **pleasure, is hard to surpass. BG 7.14**

Guṇa-mayī efers to prakṛti.

## 49.

Māyā has two portions: the guṇa-rūpa or māyā portion which is the efficient cause (nimitta), and the dravya-rūpa, or pradhāna, the material cause (upādāna). These differences are described in four verses.

*atha te sampravakṣyāmi sāṅkhyaṁ pūrvair viniścitam*  
*yad vijñāya pumān sadyo jahyād vaikalpikaṁ bhramam*

**Now I shall describe to you Sāṅkhya, which has been perfectly established by ancient authorities. By understanding this, a person can immediately give up the illusion of identity with the body. SB 11.24.1**

The commentary: from the unity of Paramātmā arises all duality through prakṛti and puruṣa (jīva). Again that duality merges into Paramātmā. A person who understands this destroys duality. To say this, the Lord praises Sāṅkhya. Pradhāna means prakṛti.

## 50.

*āsīj jñānam atho artha ekam evāvikalpitam*  
*yadā viveka-nipuṇā ādau kṛta-yuge ’yuge*

**Originally, during Satya-yuga, when all men were expert in spiritual discrimination, and also during the period of annihilation, the seer and objects seen were merged in Brahman.**

The commentary of Śrīdhara is as follows. “Atha means completely. The seer (jñānam) and all objects to be seen by him (arthaḥ) were merged (avikalpitam) in Brahman (ekam).”

In the Third Canto it is said:

*bhagavān eka āsedam agra ātmātmanāṁ vibhuḥ*  
*ātmecchānugatāv ātmā nānā-maty-upalakṣaṇaḥ*

**The great Lord, the soul of the souls, alone existed before the creation of the universe, when the desire to create bodies of the** ***jīvas*** **was absent. SB 3.5.23**

The word Bhagavān is here equivalent to Brahman since they are one entity according to vadanti tat tat-vidas (SB 1.2.11). At the end of the discussion the Lord says:

*eṣa sāṅkhya-vidhiḥ proktaḥ saṁśaya-granthi-bhedanaḥ*  
*pratilomānulomābhyāṁ parāvara-dṛśa mayā*

**Thus I, the perfect seer of everything material and spiritual, have spoken this knowledge of Sāṅkhya, which destroys the illusion of doubt by analysis of creation and annihilation. SB 11.24.29**

Thus it is clear that the form of Bhagavān remains, since the Lord is described as the perfect seer of everything material and spiritual (parāvara-dṛśā māyā).

When was this condition? The oneness arose when people were very intelligent at the beginning, Satya-yuga, and appears again at the time of pralaya (ayuge).

## 51.

*tan māyā-phala-rūpeṇa kevalaṁ nirvikalpitam*  
*vāṅ-mano-’gocaraṁ satyaṁ dvidhā samabhavad bṛhat*

**That one real entity, remaining free from material dualities, divided himself into two categories, in a way which was beyond mind and words, with a manifestation of māyā which was glanced upon by Brahman. SB 11.24.3**

Śrīdhara Svāmī’s commentary says, “The Brahman (bṛhat) became two, in the illusory or playful form of māyā glanced upon by Brahman, a special manifestation (phalam) of Brahman. This act was beyond words and mind.”

## 52.

*tayor ekataro hy arthaḥ prakṛtiḥ sobhayātmikā*  
*jñānaṁ tv anyatamo bhāvaḥ puruṣaḥ so ’bhidhīyate*

**Of these two categories of manifestation, one is** ***prakṛti,*** **which embodies cause and effect. The other is the conscious living entity, designated as the** ***jīva.***

The commentary of Śrīdhara Svāmī says “Of the two divided parts (tayoh) one is the form of cause and effect (ubhyātmikā).”

Viṣṇu Purāṇa 1.2.24 says purāṇe viṣṇoḥ svarūpāt parato 'hi te 'nye rūpe prādhānaṁ puruṣaś ca vipra: O brāhmaṇa, from the svarūpa of Viṣṇu appear two other forms: pradhāna and the jīva. The commentary says: from the svarūpa of Viṣṇu who has no upādhis (parataḥ), the two entities pradhāna and puruṣa (anye), created by māyā, become manifest. The Lord speaks these verses.

## 53.

Elsewhere the difference is described according to the different functions of the aṁśas called substance (upādāna) and efficient cause (nimitta).

*kālo daivaṁ karma jīvaḥ svabhāvo*  
*dravyaṁ kṣetraṁ prāṇa ātmā vikāraḥ*  
*tat-saṅghāto bīja-roha-pravāhas*  
*tvan-māyaiṣā tan-niṣedhaṁ prapadye*

**Time, the agitator; fate; action, or cause; the conditioned** ***jīva*****; impressions; the sense objects; the** ***prakṛti; mahat-tattva;*** **false ego; the eleven senses and five gross elements; the body—all this constitutes your material illusory energy,** ***māyā*****, an endless cycle like that of seed and plant. I take shelter of you, the negation of this** ***māyā.***  **SB 10.63.26**

The commentary of Śrīdhara Svāmī says “Kālaḥ is the agitator. Karma or action is the nimitta or cause. Daiva is manifestation of future results of action. Svabhāvaḥ is the impression of daiva 9on the jīva). Jīva is the entity endowed with the daiva upādhi , kāla, karma and svabhava. Dravyam is sense objects. Ksetram is prakṛti. Prāṇaḥ is sūtra (prior to ahaṅkāra). Ātmā is ahaṅkāra. Vikāra refers to the eleven senses and the five gross elements, sixteen in total. Tat-saṅghātaḥ is the body. All these are a cycle (pravāhaḥ) like a seed and sprout. From the body, comes the seed, action (karma). From that comes a body, which is like a sprout of the action. From that comes another action or seed which produces another body. This is the flow or pravāha. I worship (prapadye) you (tat) who is the negation of this.”

Kāla, daiva, karma and svabhāva are the nimitta (guṇa-rūpa) portion and the rest are the upādāna (dravya-rūpa). The jīva endowed with kāla, daiva, karma and svabhāva is dual: both nimitta (being necessary for prakṛti to transform) and upādāna (being a substance, unlike time, daiva etc.). A portion of the śakti of nimitta goes into the upādana class, just as the sense of “I” of the jīva’s svarūpa, transformed by ignorance (avidyā,) leads into the sense of “I” of jīva’s upādhi (ahaṅkāra). It is said:

*ātmānaṁ cāsya nirbhinnam abhimāno ’viśat padam*  
*karmaṇāṁśena yenāsau kartavyaṁ pratipadyate*

**When the abode of** ***ahaṅkāra*** **in the heart of the universal form became differentiated, the presiding deity Rudra along with his portion the** ***ahaṅkāra*** **entered. By this** ***ahaṅkāra*****, identifying oneself became manifest. SB 3.6.25**

In the above verse ātmānam means ahaṅkāra. Abhimānaḥ is Śiva. Karmanā means “with the ahaṅkāra.” Nirbhinnam means that the basis or abode became differentiated. That is explained in the commentary on SB 3.6.12, where voice is described in the instrumental case as the adhyātmā aspect (whereas the place is adhibhūta and the deity of the sense is adhidaiva). Viśvanātha’s commentary on 3.6.12: Agni entered the mouth, its abode (*padam*), which became differentiated in that fetus along with its portion, the voice, by which the universal form could utter words. In this and the following verses the object differentiated in the accusative case (in this verse mouth) refers to *adhiṣṭhāna*, the *adhibhūta* aspect, the gross location in which the subtle sense resides. Agni and others in the nominative case are the presiding deities, the *adhidaiva* aspect of the universal form. The voice in the instrumental case, in this case voice (*vacā*), *adhyātma* aspect, the sense organ. The sense object or action of the organ, also *adhibhūta*, is in the accusative or genitive case. (In this verse it is speech).

7

Pradhāna, called kṣetra in the verse, which is the basis of all other parts of upādhāna, is a portion of the original māyā. Jīva is endowed with these upādhis but should be understood to beyond them in his pure state. Jvara speaks the verse to the Lord.

## 54.

The first two functions of the nimitta form of māyā are described:

*vidyāvidye mama tanū viddhy uddhava śarīriṇām*  
*mokṣa-bandha-karī ādye māyayā me vinirmite*

**O Uddhava! Understand that** ***vidyā*** **and** ***avidyā*** **are my** ***śaktis.*** **They are created by my** ***māyā,*** **are without beginning, and create liberation and bondage for the living beings. SB 11.11.3**

The commentary of Śridhāra Svāmī says, “Since bondage and liberation are manifested (tanyete) by avidyā and vidyā, they are called tanū. They are śaktis made of my māyā (māyayā me vinirmite). Since they are functions of māyā they cause bondage and liberation. The single case in bandha-mokṣa-karī indicates dual case. ‘Since they are effects of māyā they should not be eternal or without beginning.’ No, they are without beginning (ādye). As long as I inspire avidyā, bondage appears (verb is understood). When I give vidyā, then liberation appears.”

Since Vidyā and avidyā are stated in the verse to arise from māyā, they are the functions of māyā in actuality. Though it is said they are made (vinirmite), this means that they merely manifest by māyā whose actions are really infinite since they are actually without beginning.

“The jīva is by nature in a liberated state. Bondage however appears just by ignorance. With the appeaerance of vidyā, liberation manifests. Thus liberation is eternal.” This should not be said. Starting in SB 11.3.16, it is not stated that māyā produces liberation as its general characteristic. It is not correct since even the “liberation” described at the time of final pralaya is secondary in meaning. Thus the function of vidyā (part of māyā) is only a door to revealing the special vidyā belonging to the svarūpa-śakti. That vidyā belonging to māyā does not in itself produce liberation.

Avidyā has two functions: covering (avaraṇātikā) and confusing (vikṣepātmikā). The covering function sits in the jīva and covers his natural knowledge. The confusing function sits in the jīva and produces false knowledge.

## 55.

The nimitta portions should be considered. Three śaktis famous as māyā, portions of nimitta, are seen: jñāna, icchā and kriyā śaktis. The Lord’s jñāna-śakti is described in the Third Canto:

*sā vā etasya saṁdraṣṭuḥ śaktiḥ sad-asad-ātmikā*  
*māyā nāma mahā-bhāga yayedaṁ nirmame vibhuḥ*

**O great soul! That energy of the Lord, composed of the visible and invisible, which makes arrangement for the seer and seen, is called** ***māyā.*** **By this energy the Lord creates the universe. SB 3.5.25**

The commentary of Śrīdhara Svāmī says, “The energy which makes arrangement for the seer and seen, whose very nature (ātmikā) is visible (sat) and invisible (asat), is called māyā. Māyā (jñāna-śakti) has the visible and invisible as her nature because it makes arrangement for the visible (matter) and invisible (ātmā).”

The icchā śakti is also described there**.** ātmecchānugatāv ātmā: in the beginning the desire of the Lord to create bodies for the jīvas was absent. (SB 3.5.23)

The kriyā śakti is described in the Eleventh Canto. One should see the discussion related to SB 11.3.16 in section 48. (Māyā is the cause of creation, maintenance and destruction of the universe.)

Though the Lord’s direct knowledge, desire and action are not māyā but his śvarūpa-śakti, that knowledge, desire and action arising from the svarūpa-śakti are not used for actions related to the material world but rather for encouraging the devotees. The knowledge, desire and action related to the universe are secondary. This is will be considered later. The word knowledge in the present context means a different knowledge, desire and action, which are functions of māyā, acting as a shadow of real the spiritual functions.

The knowledge, desire and action which function in relation to māyā have two types: that fixed only in the supreme Lord because of his nature and that fixed in the jīva, since it is given by the Lord. The first (fixed in the Lord) takes the form of arrangements for the seer and seen, desire to create the universe and time etc. The second (fixed in the jīva) takes the form of vidyā, avidyā, desire to enjoy matter, and material action.

The qualities of the upādāna portion, pradhāna, are described:

*yat tat tri-guṇam avyakta nityaṁ sad-asad-ātmakam*  
*pradhānaṁ prakṛtiṁ prāhur aviśeṣaṁ viśeṣavat*

***Prakṛti*** **is called** ***avyakta*** **because, though having variety, it has a state of invisibility when the three** ***guṇas*** **are in balance. It is called** ***pradhāna*** **because, manifesting variety, it is the best, being the shelter of all the visible elements. It is called** ***prakṛti*** **because it is the very cause of all causes and effects. It is eternal. SB 3.26.10**

That which is a collection of the three guṇas is called avyakta, pradhāna, and prakṛti. It is called avyakta (unmanifest) because it is without qualities (aviśeṣam) when the three guṇas are in equilibrium. This means it is in an undeveloped state (avyākṛta). It is called pradhāna (chief) because, having particulars or effects (viśeṣavat), it is best among all the effects which are its parts--the particulars such as mahat-tattva--since it is the shelter of all these. It is calle prakṛti because it has the nature (ātmaka) of all effect and cause (sad-asat) such as mahat-tattva and the rest, since it is their cause. It is eternal. During pralaya, prakrṭi is in an undeveloped condition, a causal state, holding within it all its aṁśas, with the aṁśas or elmenets in an uncombined state which need to mixed with other elements for creation and maintenance. This is the undeveloped state. It is not Brahman since Brahman does not have guṇas, and it is not mahat-tattva or other elements since they are prakṛti’s effects, depending on it. Kapila speaks the verse.

Viṣṇu Purāṇa describes prakṛti:

*avyaktaṁ kāraṇaṁ yat tat pradhānam ṛṣi-sattamaiḥ*  
*procyate prakṛtiḥ sūkṣmā nityaṁ sad-asad-ātmakam*

**The invisible cause is called pradhāna by the best of sages. It is called subtle prakṛti, the source of effect and cause. It is eternal.**

*akṣayyaṁ nānyad-ādhāraṁ ameyam ajaraṁ dhruvam*  
*śabda-sparśa-vihīnaṁ tad- rūpādibhir asaṁhatam*

**It is indestructible, without any other support, immeasurable, without old age and fixed. It is without sound or touch and unaccompanied by form or other qualities.**

*tri-guṇaṁ taj jagad-yonir anādi-prabhavāpyayam*  
*tenāgre sarvam evāsīd vyāpyaṁ vai pralayam anu*

**It is composed of three guṇas, is the source of the universe, and flows without beginning. It existed before all else and pervades after pralaya.**

The followers of Vedānta consider pradhāna, the subtle state of the beginningless universe also called avyakta and avyākrta, to be dependent on the supreme Lord. Tad adhīnatvād arthavat:

*pradhāna* is useful, since it produces the world under the Lord's direction. (Brahma-sūtra 1.4.3) It is denied independence, which is advocated by Sāṅkhya.

*Ānumānikam apy ekeṣām iti cen na śarīra-rūpaka-vinyasta-gṛhīter darśayati ca*

**Though some say that** ***avyakta*** **in the text refers to** ***pradhāna*****, it is not so, because the** ***avyakta*** **represents the body in the previous metaphor of body representing a chariot, and the text shows this. Brahma-sūtra 1.4.1**

The word pradhāna is found in Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad 6.16:

*pradhāna-kṣetrajña-patir guṇeśaḥ*  
*saṁsara-bandha-sthiti-mokṣa-hetiḥ*

**The Lord is the master of pradhāna and the jīva. He is the lord of the guṇas. He is the cause of bondage in the world, living in the world and liberation from it.**

## 56.

Thus in two Sandarbhas (Bhagavata and Paramātmā) the three śaktis have been described. Verses have been gathered below in order to destroy error arising from not differentiating between them since the names are sometimes used interchangeably.

*māyā syād antaraṅgā yā bahiraṅgā ca sā smṛtā*  
*pradhāne 'pi kvacid dṛṣṭā tad-vṛttir mohinī ca sā*

**Māyā is a term for both the internal (spiritual) and external (material) śaktis. It is sometimes used to indicate pradhāna (upādāna aspect of māyā) which functions to cause bewilderment.**

*ādye traye syāt prakṛtis cic-chaktis tv āntaraṅgikā*  
*śuddha-jīve 'pi te dṛṣṭe tatheśa-jñāna-vīryayoḥ*

**Prakṛti is used in reference to māyā, bahiraṅga-śakti and pradhāna. Antaraṅga-śakti (internal śakti) is also called the cit-śakti, and is used in relation to the pure jīva and the Lord’s knowledge and power.**

*cin-māyā-śakti-vṛttyos tu vidyā-śaktir udiryate*  
*cic-chakti-vṛttau māyāyāṁ yogamāyā samā smṛtā*

**vidyā-śakti (knowledge) is said to describe both the cit-śakti and māyā-śakti. When the cit-śakti is called māyā it is actually yogamāyā.**

*pradhānāvyakṛtāvyaktaṁ traiguṇye prakṛtau param*  
*na māyāyāṁ na cic-chaktāv ity ādy uhyaṁ vivekibhiḥ*

**Pradhāṇa, avyākṛta, avyakta are names of prakrṭi which holds three guṇas. It does not refer to the cit-śakti or yoga-māyā.**

The result of māyā is the universe:

*tatas tenānuviddhebhyo yuktebhyo 'ṇḍam acetanam*  
*utthitaṁ puruṣo yasmād udatiṣṭhad asau virāṭ*

**By the Lord’s entrance into the elements, they combined together and formed an unconscious globe, from which the universal form composed of the mass of** ***jīvas*** **arose. SB 3.26.51**

The unconscious globe arose from the elements agitated (anuviddhebhyaḥ) by the Lord. From that globe arose the universal form.

*etad aṇḍaṁ viśeṣākhyaṁ krama-vṛddhair daśottaraiḥ*  
*toyādibhiḥ parivṛtaṁ pradhānenāvṛtair bahiḥ*  
*yatra loka-vitāno 'yaṁ rūpaṁ bhagavato hareḥ*

**The universal globe called** ***viśeṣa*****, is surrounded by layers of elements such as water, each ten times thicker than the previous layer, covered by a layer of** ***prakṛti*** **on the outside. Within the universe are the varieties of planets, which are a form of the Supreme Lord. SB 3.26.52**

It is said there that the planets are form of the puruṣa (bhagavataḥ). Kapila speaks these verses.

## 57.

Since it was said above that this universe is a form of the Lord, that would mean it is spiritual like the Lord’s form. That is refuted by the following:

*amunī bhagavad-rūpe mayā te hy anuvarṇite \|*  
*ubhe api na gṛhṇanti māyā-sṛṣṭe vipaścitaḥ \|\|*

**The wise do not accept these two forms of the Lord described by me since they are composed of matter. SB 2.10.35**

The two material purusas are considered to be the Lord for purposes of worship. They are the universe in gross form, virāṭ, and subtle form, hiraṇya-garbha. The wise do not accept these two which I have described to you. They do not worship them as actual forms of the Lord since they are your material abodes. This is the meaning. Śukadeva speaks the verse.

It is said in Viṣṇu Purāṇa:

*yad etad dṛśyate mūrtaṁ etaj-jñānātmanas tava*  
*bhrānti-jñānena paśyanti jagad-rūpam ayoginaḥ*

**Out of mistaken knowledge, those who are not yogīs accept the form of the universe which is visible to be your form, though you are composed of knowledge.**

Śruti also says nedaṁ yad idam upāsate: the world which the living beings worship is not the Lord. (Kena Upaniṣad 1.4) The first idam refers to Brahman according to Rāmanuja’s commentary and the second idam refers to the universe.

The wise do not accept these forms because they are created by māyā (māyā-sṛṣṭe). They do not arise from the Lord’s svarūpa-śakti. From this it is clear that the four armed form of the Lord is beyond māyā. It should be understood that, ordinary human forms made of matter within the universe, are often referred to as sattva, raja tamo-guṇa expansions of the purusa and guṇa avatāra (Viṣṇu, Brahmā and Śiva). With this in mind Markandeya Purāṇa says:

*viṣṇuḥ śarīra-grahaṇaṁ aham īśāna eva ca*  
*kāritas te yato 'tas tvaṁ kaḥ stotuṁ śaktimān bhavet*

**Since Viṣṇu, I (Brahmā) and Śiva are made to accept bodies by you (Durgā), who can sufficiently glorify you?**

The word śarīra (body) means individual bodies of living entities since, (if it were to mean actual bodies of Viṣṇu, Brahmā and Śiva) before accepting the bodies, there would no distinctions of Viṣṇu, Brahmā and Śiva, and they could not be so designated.

## 58.

It was said that the subtle and gross universal forms were created by māyā (māyā-sṛṣṭe, SB 2.10.35). The word māyā does not mean ignorance in this case. This would mean that Brahman becomes everything (duality with jīvas etc.) out of ignorance. The Lord who is knowledge itself, full of good qualities, devoid anything else, and devoid of false ego, cannot be the shelter of ignorance. Nor can he be the object of another’s ignorance. Nor can he be the cause of mistakes. Since he is a most astonishing entity, he must have inconceivable śakti, as seen in a cintāmaṇi. By that śakti, just as a medicine destroys sickness, he has limbs though he has no limbs, since he possesses mutual contrary qualities in himself.

There is proof of the Lord’s inconceivable śakti.

*vicitra-śaktiḥ puruṣaḥ purāṇo na cānyeṣāṁ śaktayas tādṛśaḥ syuḥ*

**The eternal Lord has astonishing śakti. No one else has such śaktis. Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad**

*ātmeśvaro 'tarkya-sahasra-śaktiḥ*

**The Lord of the jīvas has thousands of inconceivable śaktis. SB 3.33.3**

*ātmani caivaṁ vicitraś ca hi*

**As various marvelous creations arise from desire trees or cintāmaṇi according to authorities, so we can believe from scripture that various creations arise from the Lord. The Purāṇas confirm this. Brahma-sūtra 2.1.28**

One should not image ignorance in the Lord in order to avoid duality since ignorance covering the Lord is impossible. Because the Lord possesses inconceivable śakti and scriptures testify to this, the difficulty in explainign duality is removed. That inconceivable śakti makes duality easy to explain. Though Paramātmā exists with an unchanging nature, by his inconceivable śakti he is able to transform into the shape of the universe. It is just like the cintāmaṇi which produces objects or a magnet which moves iron. Bādarāyaṇa accepts this. Śrutes tu śabda-mūlatvāt: the defects of the agent do not apply to Brahman because of scriptural statements, which are the only proof concerning inconceivable subjects.

Since the Lord possesses such śakti, it is not correct to say that the word māyā means some sort of magic or illusion. Rather it means that which produces (mīyate) astonishing variety. Māyā means the Lord’s śakti which produces amazing objects. (Thus the universe is made by the Lord’s inconceibable śakti (māyā-sṛṣṭe). Transformation of Paramātmā (by means of his śakti) is thus the conclusion of scripture. This is described in Bhagavat-sandarbha. Though the Lord does not change, by the inconceivable śakti, change takes place in him. His “being” does not change in his svarūpa but changes in his dravya-śakti, which manifests an expanded form (vyūha as pradhāṇa) as in the case of cintāmaṇi.

Since he is the root, it is not false to say that Paramātmā acts as the material cause. In the Eleventh Canto it is said:

*prakṛtir yasyopādānam ādhāraḥ puruṣaḥ paraḥ*  
*sato ’bhivyañjakaḥ kālo brahma tat tritayaṁ tv aham*

***Prakrṭi*** **is the material cause, the** ***puruṣa*** **is the foundational cause. Time, the indirect cause, is the agitator of** ***prakṛti.*** **I am all three. SB 11.24.19**

Thus sometimes prakrṭi is called the material cause and sometimes the Lord is called the material cause. The śakti of transformation called māyā is of two types: the nimitta portion called māyā and the upādāna portion called pradhāna. The pure śakti is the nimitta and śakti endowed with vyūha (expansion) is the upādāna. Therefore sometimes māyā is also said to be unconscious (referring to upādāna). Vijñānam cāvijñānaṁ ca: it is both knowledge and ignorance. (Taittirīya Upaniṣad 2.6.1)

In the basic authority, Bhāgavatam, which deals with the creation in the Third Canto as the main subject, the work describes transformation of pradhāna (rather than the Lord) as the material cause because it speaks from the point of view of jñāna and vairāgya, in coordination with the general view of other Purāṇas. Similarly in some śrutis, vivarta (illusory appearance of Brahman as the world) is described, as an aṅga of jñāna and vairāgya. This does not mean however that the world is actually false. It means however that there is wrong attribution of the samaṣṭi (universal form) and vyaṣṭi (individual body), real entities produced by a transformation of pradhāna which is a vyūha (expansion) of Paramaṭmā, to Paramātmā or perhaps to his portion, the ātmā, because of identifying the person with what it possesses. Hearing descriptions of the universal form related to Paramātmā one could transfer the creatorship to the ātmā.

Attributing one object to be in another place is false, like mistaking a shell for silver. However vivarta-vāda mistakes an object to something else which has no existence. It is like attributing something to be a flower floating in the sky. That is impossible. There are also no examples of illusion based on illusion to infinity.

From seeing water previously, a form of water develops in the mind. It remains dormant in unrelated circumstances. On seeing an object similar to water that form awakens. Without searching out particulars of water, one mistakes the object for water. Water is not false, nor is the form in the memory. Nor is an object like a mirage false. However attributing something to be non-different from a recollected object is false, since it is not the actual object seen.

In dreams, with forms of objects seen in waking state held in the mind, the māyā of Paramātmā makes one identify it with an external object. But the dream object is not false.

*Māyā-mātraṁ tu kārtsnyenānabhivyakta-svarūpatvāt*

**The cause is the Lord's** ***māyā*** **because the dreams by nature are not revealed to everyone. Brahma-sūtra 3.2.3**

Thus though mistaking the universe for the pure ātmā or Paramātmā is false, the universe is not false. Thinking of the world as Paramātma is for unintelligent people since the universe is not directly the Lord, being produced *from* him and since it is not directly his place, unlike Vaikuṇṭha.

Though the Lord dwells in the world he is not touched by it. It is said asaktaṁ sarva-bhṛc caiva: he is unattached but maintains everything. (BG 13.15) In scriptures such as Viṣṇu Purāṇa 1.4.39, the false identity of a person with his body or house is described. Or Bhāgavatam says:

*tvām ātmānaṁ paraṁ matvā param ātmānam eva ca*  
*ātmāpunar bahir mṛgya aho ’jña-janatājñatā*

**Considering that you, Paramātmā to be an ordinary** ***jīva,*** **a person seeks you, not in Vṛndāvana, but internally, as merely the** ***jīva.*** **SB 10.14.27**

Thinking that you, the source (param) of everyone, are just the opposite (itaram), and that another jīva is the original ātmā, followers of Sāṅkhya and others search for the ātmā externally. Having gained a foothold by this, māyā produced the mistaken idea that the body is the ātmā. This is loss of all knowledge by ignorance.

*deho 'savo 'kṣā manavo bhūta-mātrām*  
*ātmānam anyaṁ ca viduḥ paraṁ yat*  
*sarvaṁ pumān veda guṇāṁś ca taj-jño*  
*na veda sarva-jñam anantam īḍe*

**The body, life airs, senses, internal senses, gross elements and sense objects do not know themselves or other things or the** ***jīva.***  **The** ***jīva*** **knows all of these items and the** ***guṇas*** **which cause them. He also can know Paramātmā, but does not really know the omniscient Lord. I worship that Lord with infinite qualities. SB 6.4.25**

*ātmāparijñāna-mayo vivādo*  
*hy astīti nāstīti bhidārtha-niṣṭhaḥ*  
*vyartho ’pi naivoparameta puṁsāṁ*  
*mattaḥ parāvṛtta-dhiyāṁ sva-lokāt*

**Those who do not know Paramātmā, who are fixed in goals other than me, engage in arguments concerning the reality or unreality of the world, and do not cease to argue, though they achieve no results. They have turned their thoughts from me, the Lord, who gives regard only to the devotees. SB 11.22.34**

It should be understood that transformation (pariṇāma) is the conclusion of Bhāgavatam, since vivarta (world is illusion) is a secondary feature propounded in the section on jñāna; since transformation is the chief feature in the section presenting the author’s viewpoint; and since pariṇāma attains even greater strength by agreeing with both sections. This pariṇāma is for the purpose of revealing the inconceivable powers of the Lord.

Stating that the universe is illusory is for the purpose of showing that one should meditate on the Lord since the universe is temporary and extrinsic to the Lord. It is not meant to show that the universe is false and not for the purpose of showing the the Lord and the jīva are the same svarūpa. Vaidharmyāc ca na svapnādivat: one cannot claim that external objects are only cognitions as in dreams, since there is difference in the dream object and object of waking perception. (Brahma-sūtra 2.2.29)

Thus it is said in Nārada Purāṇa says:

*jagad vilāpayām āsur ity ucyetātha tat-smṛteḥ*  
*na ca tat-smṛti-mātreṇa layo bhavati niścitam*

**It is said that by remembering the Lord one destroys attachment to the universe but by remembering the universe one does not destroy the attachment.**

In the main explanations about creation, the conclusion is that it takes place by transformation of pradhāna:

*kāla-vṛttyā tu māyāyāṁ guṇa-mayyām adhokṣajaḥ*  
*puruṣeṇātma-bhūtena vīryam ādhatta vīryavān*

**Bhagavān, lord of Mahā-vaikuṇṭha, in his** ***svāṁśa*** **expansion as the** ***puruṣa*****, full of potency, at a certain moment of time, placed the** ***jīvas*** **into** ***māyā*** **which became agitated by the** ***guṇas.*** **SB 3.5.26**

*tato ’bhavan mahat-tattvam avyaktāt kāla-coditāt*  
*vijñānātmātma-deha-sthaṁ viśvaṁ vyañjaṁs tamo-nudaḥ*

**Impelled by time, from the invisible** ***prakṛti*** **arose** ***mahat-tattva*****, composed of knowledge, in** ***sattva-guṇa*****. This manifests the universe situated within itself, and destroys ignorance. SB 3.5.27**

The Lord (adhokṣajaḥ) through his portion who glances at prakṛti (puruṣeṇa ātmā-bhūtena) places the jīva (vīryam) in prakṛti (māyām) made of the guṇas by māyā having the function of time (kāla-vṛttyā). Śruti says hantemās tisro devatā: the Lord entered the three *devatās* with the jīva and divided name and form. (Chāndogya Upaniṣad 6.3.2) Mahat-tattva, composed of knowledge (vijñānātma), dispels the ignorance of pralaya (tamo-nudaḥ). Maitreya speaks the verse.

## 59.

*eko nārāyaṇo devaḥ pūrva-sṛṣṭaṁ sva-māyayā*  
*saṁhṛtya kāla-kalayā kalpānta idam īśvaraḥ*  
*eka evādvitīyo ’bhūd ātmādhāro ’khilāśrayaḥ*

**The one form of Viṣṇu withdrew the universe previously created by his** ***māyā*** **through his time** ***śakti*** **at the end of Brahmā’s life, and remained alone, the support of himself and shelter of all his** ***śaktis*****.**

*Kālenātmānubhāvena sāmyaṁ nītāsu śaktiṣu*  
*sattvādiṣv ādi-puruṣaḥ pradhāna-puruṣeśvaraḥ*  
*parāvarāṇāṁ parama āste kaivalya-saṁjñitaḥ*  
*kevalānubhavānanda- sandoho nirupādhikaḥ*

**When he brought his energies such as** ***sattva*** **to equilibrium by his powerful energy of time, the Lord, who enjoys in himself, who is the controller of** ***prakṛti*** **and the** ***jīvas,*** **who is worshipped by liberated and conditioned** ***jīvas*****, remained in the form of complete bliss, without** ***māyā.***

*kevalātmānubhāvena sva-māyāṁ tri-guṇātmikām*  
*saṅkṣobhayan sṛjaty ādau tayā sūtram arindama*

**O subduer of the enemies! At the time of creation the Lord by the power of his** ***cit-śakti*** **agitates** ***prakṛti*** **made of the** ***guṇas*** **and creates** ***mahat-tattva (sūtra).***

*tām āhus tri-guṇa-vyaktiṁ sṛjantīṁ viśvato-mukham*  
*yasmin protam idaṁ viśvaṁ yena saṁsarate pumān*

**They say that this** ***sūtra,*** **the product of the three** ***guṇas,*** **creates the universe with great variety. By this** ***sūtra*** **the universe is pervaded, and by this** ***sūtra*** **the** ***jīva*** **takes rebirth.**

*yathorṇanābhir hṛdayād ūrṇāṁ santatya vaktrataḥ*  
*tayā vihṛtya bhūyas tāṁ grasaty evaṁ maheśvaraḥ*

**Just as from within himself the spider expands thread through his mouth, plays with it for some time and eventually swallows it, similarly, the Supreme Lord produces the universe and then withdraws it. SB 11.9.16-21**

By his independent māyā (tayā) of which time is a portion, he creates mahat-tattva. Śruti says:

*yathorṇa-nābhiḥ sṛjate gṛhnate ca*  
*yathā pṛthivyām oṣadhayaḥ sambhavanti*  
*tathā sataḥ puruṣāt keśa-lomāni*  
*tathākṣarāt sambhavatīha viśvam*

**As a spider creates and then withdraws a web, as plants sprout from the earth, and as hairs grow from a man, so the material universe has come from the Lord. Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad 1.1.17**

Dattātreya speaks the verse to Yadu.

## 60.

It has been said that the gross conscious and unconscious forms—the conditioned jīva and the elements like earth arise from Paramātmā, who has śaktis of the pure jīva and prakṛti, conscious and unconscious entities, in subtle form. The wise consider that the one Paramaṭma is both the efficient cause (nimitta), as pure Paramātamā, and material cause (upādāna) as the Lord endowed with śaktis. Prakṛtiś ca pratijñā-dṛṣṭāntanurdhāt: the Lord is also the material cause because only then is harmony between the propositions presented in the Upaniṣads and the examples given to illustrate the propositions. Gentle Śvetaketu! You are very proud, praised, thinking yourself a scholar. Have you asked about the ruler by which the unheard becomes heard, the inconceivable becomes conceived and the unknown becomes known. Chāndogya Upaniṣad 6.1.3

This proposition becomes possible if the ruler is also the material cause because the Brahman as *prakṛti* is non-different from the effects (universe). An efficient cause alone however is not non-different from the effect, as we see in the case of the potter and the pot. There is an example given. *yathā somyaikena mṛt-piṇḍena sarvaṁ mṛnmayaṁ vijñātaṁ syāt*: by knowing one portion of earth, all things made of earth are known. (Chāndogya Upaniṣad 6.1.4) The subject of this statement is knowing the material effects by knowing the material cause. This is not possible by knowing only the efficient cause.
