If one knows the potter the pot is not known. For harmony between the proposition and the example, Brahman is the material cause and from the explicit statements of scripture Brahman is also the efficient cause. 8 (Brahma-sūtra 1.4.23)

Paramātmā always remains pure. He is non-different from his śakti because śakti is included within the śaktimān. Accepting sat-kārya-vāda, the effect is a portion of the cause, residing as a specific quality in the cause and manifesting itself at certain time. Vācārambhaṇaṁ vikāro nāma-dheyaṁ mṛttikety eva satyam: clay takes on a name as it undergoes transformation by actions initiated by words, but clay alone is proven by authority. (Chāndogya Upanisad 6.14) This proves the the effect is none other than the cause and the cause is non-different from the effect.

Thus the universe is non-different from Paramātmā who is endowed with śakti to cause the universe. Paramātmā is non-different from the universe.

*idaṁ hi viśvaṁ bhagavān ivetaro*  
*yato jagat-sthāna-nirodha-sambhavāḥ \|*  
*tad dhi svayaṁ veda bhavāṁs tathāpi te*  
*prādeśa-mātraṁ bhavataḥ pradarśitam \|\|*

**This universe is like the Lord but the Lord is also different from the universe and the jīva also is like the Lord but different, for from the Lord arises the maintenance, destruction and creation of the universe. You know this naturally since you are an** ***avatāra*** **of the Lord. Thus I have shown to you a small portion of** ***bhakti*** **and knowledge concerning the Lord’s power. SB 1.5.20**

The universe is “like” the Lord (idam viśvaṁ bhagavān iva). Thus it is different from the Lord. In another case (itaraḥ), the jīva is similar to and different from the Lord. Aitadātmyam idaṁ sarvam: the world is non-different from the Lord. (Chāndogya Upaniṣad 6.8.7) sarvaṁ khalv idaṁ brahma: everything is Brahman. (Chāndogya Upaniṣad 3.14.1)

From the Lord (yataḥ) arises creation, maintenance and destruction of the universe. I have shown you (bhavataḥ) a small portion (pradeṣa-mātram) of knowledge. Nārada speaks to Vyāsa.

Paramātmā Sandarbha 2

## 61.

This is made clear in the following:

*so ’yaṁ te ’bhihitas tāta bhagavān viśva-bhāvanaḥ \|*  
*samāsena harer nānyad anyasmāt sad-asac ca yat \|\|*

**Paramātmā, maintainer of the universe, has thus been described in summary. The universe and** ***jīvas*** **are none other than the Lord, but arise from the Lord who is different from them. SB 2.7.50**

The Lord has been described in summary *(samāsena).* How? The secondary quality is described. He is the gross effect (*sat):* the conditioned jīva (conscious entity) and the manifested universe (unconscious entity). He is the subtle cause (*asat*): the pure jīva (conscious) and pradhāna (unconscious). He is not different (*na anyat*) from them since he is the śakti in the subtle aspect and the effect in the gross aspect. Haṁsa has said:

*manasā vacasā dṛṣṭyā gṛhyate ’nyair apīndriyaiḥ*  
*aham eva na matto ’nyad iti budhyadhvam añjasā*

**Within this world, whatever is perceived by the mind, speech, eyes or other senses is me alone and nothing besides me. All of you please understand this as it is. SB 11.13.24**

Though the Lord is non-different from the world he does not contact the faults of the world since he is also different *(anyasmāt*). Brahmā speaks to Nārada.

## 62.

The non-difference is described with logic in five verses:

*ādāv ante janānāṁ sad bahir antaḥ parāvaram*  
*jñānaṁ jñeyaṁ vaco vācyaṁ tamo jyotis tv ayaṁ svayam*

**Paramātmā is what exists in the beginning and in the end. It is external and internal objects. It is superior and inferior objects. It is the senses, the sense objects and objects expressed by words. Paramātmā produces ignorance and knowledge. SB 7.15.57**

Paramātmā, the cause of all things, exists *(sat*) as the beginning of all bodies (*janānām*), being the cause and as the end, as the conclusion. He is himself external things, to be enjoyed and internal objects, the enjoyer. He is superior and inferior. He produces ignorance *(tamas*) and knowledge *(jyotiḥ).* Others, without his sanction, cannot reveal anything on their own.

## 63.

“Why do things appear to be different from the Lord?”

*ābādhito ’pi hy ābhāso yathā vastutayā smṛtaḥ*  
*durghaṭatvād aindriyakaṁ tadvad artha-vikalpitam*

**Just as reflections, though not independent, are accepted as real, so objects perceived by the senses are accepted as independent, because of difficulty in proving them otherwise. SB 7.15.58**

Though completely (ā) negated *(bādhitaḥ*) by contrary logic to be independently existing, a reflection of the sun (*ābhāsaḥ*), is imagined by children to be an independent object (*vastutayā*), since it is seen to manifest separately. In the same way fools imagine objects of the senses to be various independent objects. They cannot see the truth because of difficulty (*durghaṭatvāt*) through thinking the objects to be independent.

## 64.

This is explained in two verses:

*kṣity-ādīnām ihārthānāṁ chāyā na katamāpi hi*  
*na saṅghāto vikāro ’pi na pṛthaṅ nānvito mṛṣā*

**The body made of the five elements is neither a combination of the elements nor a whole with the elements as its parts, since it is not completely separate and not intimately connected with them. Thus the body and the world are illusory. SB 7.15.59**

A body made of five elements seen as one object by the intelligence (*chāyā)* is not a mere collection of items *(saṅghāta*), not a whole having parts *(vikāraḥ)* and not a transformation of elements. It is not a collection of items such as a forest made of trees, since pulling the body in one place pulls the whole body. If one pulls one tree, the whole forest is not pulled. The body is also not a newly made whole, transformed out of parts or elements (*vikāraḥ). This is ārambha-vāda of the Vaiśeṣikas. The gross atoms combine into a new form, the whole made of parts.*1 What to speak of it being a transformation of prakṛti (*api*). This refers to prakṛti-pariṇāṁa. Objects result from a transformation of the effect in latent form in a subtle cause, prakṛti, into material objects like mind and pot.2

Why? Is the newly produced whole (the body) separate from the elements, a transformation separate from the elements. Or is it inseparably related to the elements? It does not appear to be completely separate *(pṛthak*) from the parts nor does it appear to be intimately related to the parts (*anvitaḥ*). Does the whole body exist in every part? If so, one would understand the whole body in a finger. Is the body spread partially in each part? This would lead to an unstable condition of dividing the body into infinite parts. Thus the situation of the body as an independent object is false.

## 65.

Having shown that the body cannot be determined to be independent in existence, it is now shown that the causes such as earth cannot be determined.

*dhātavo ’vayavitvāc ca tan-mātrāvayavair vinā*  
*na syur hy asaty avayaviny asann avayavo ’ntataḥ*

**Because the elements are only parts, they cannot exist without their sense objects, which are also parts. Since the whole universe and body are unreal, the elements and sense objects are also unreal. SB 7.15.60**

*Dhātavaḥ* means the five elements since the word means “those things which support *(dhārayanti*).” The five elements cannot exist without their subtle parts (*avayavaiḥ*), the sense objects (*tan-mātraiḥ),* since the elements are also only parts. Can the parts exist independently? Since the whole cannot be determined (*avayavini*), the parts also cannot finally be determined to exist. The world cannot be proved since the existence of the smallest parts, atoms, cannot be proved. It is said:

*evaṁ niruktaṁ kṣiti-śabda-vṛttam*  
*asan nidhānāt paramāṇavo ye*  
*avidyayā manasā kalpitās te*  
*yeṣāṁ samūhena kṛto viśeṣaḥ*

**What is known by the word “earth” exists in name only because earth is resolved into atomic particles, which are imagined by the mind out of ignorance. Out of their combination, a particular object is made. SB 5.12.9**

Thus the object that can be seen as one by intelligence is Paramātmā, the cause of everything. It was correctly said that Paramātmā is the beginning and end of all beings. (SB 7.15.57) In the Third Canto it was said:

*iti tāsāṁ sva-śaktīnāṁ satīnām asametya saḥ*  
*prasupta-loka-tantrāṇāṁ niśāmya gatim īśvaraḥ*  
*kāla-sañjñāṁ tadā devīṁ bibhrac chaktim urukramaḥ*  
*trayoviṁśati tattvānāṁ gaṇaṁ yugapad āviśat*

**Seeing the sleeping state in creating the universe because the elements were unmixed, the Lord, first by his energy of cohesion, appearing through time, and then as** ***antaryāmī,*** **entered the twenty-three elements simultaneously*****.***

*so ’nupraviṣṭo bhagavāṁś ceṣṭārūpeṇa taṁ gaṇam*  
*bhinnaṁ saṁyojayām āsa suptaṁ karma prabodhayan*

**The Lord, entering by his kriyā-śakti and awakening the dormant karmas of the jīvas, combined the elements together. SB 3.6.1-3**

In the śruti, it is well known that everything is the body of Paramātmā. *Yasya pṛthivī śarīram:* His body is the earth. (Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad 3.7.3) Thus what transforms into pradhāna as parts is Paramātmā alone, the *avayavī,* source of the parts, existing everywhere. And thus the world is not false.

## 66.

“If Paramātmā is the body, the source of all parts everywhere, then causes of good qualities and faults and giving designations like brāhmaṇa, should not exist. Therefore something else must be the source.” The following verse answers.

*syāt sādṛśya-bhramas tāvad vikalpe sati vastunaḥ*  
*jāgrat-svāpau yathā svapne tathā vidhi-niṣedhatā*

**In thinking elements are real, one makes an error by a mistaken attribution of qualities. Just as one has perception of waking and sleep states in a dream, so one has perceptions of good and bad which are stated in the Vedas. SB 7.15.61**

In having doubts about Paramātmā (*vastunaḥ*), not understanding his position as the source, there is the error of thinking of similarity--thinking of identifying with the body, different from the conception that everything is one. After examining the previous and later parts and putting them together, one makes the mistake of thinking “This is a body” because the parts are situated in one place, with a common support, and because one perceives each part as one with the whole body. It is like taking each tree to be the forest.

The Lord says:

*so ’yaṁ dīpo ’rciṣāṁ yadvat srotasāṁ tad idaṁ jalam*  
*so ’yaṁ pumān iti nṛṇāṁ mṛṣā gīr dhīr mṛṣāyuṣām*

**Although the flame is continually changing, foolish people say, “This is the light of the lamp.” Though the river is continually changing, foolish people say, “This is the water of the river.” Similarly, although the material body of a human being is constantly changing, those whose lives are filled with lack of discrimination say “This is so and so.” They lack discrimination and words to express it. SB 11.22.44**

Good and bad and thinking oneself to be a brāhmaṇa etc. exist like perception of waking and sleeping states while in a dream This is how the following verse is understood:

*para-svabhāva-karmāṇi na praśaṁsen na garhayet*  
*viśvam ekātmakaṁ paśyan prakṛtyā puruṣeṇa ca*

**One should neither praise nor criticize the conditioned nature and activities of other persons. Rather, one should see this world along with** ***prakṛti*** **and the** ***jīva*** **as one with the Lord. SB 11.28.1**

*kiṁ bhadraṁ kim abhadraṁ vā dvaitasyāvastunaḥ kiyat*  
*vācoditaṁ tad anṛtaṁ manasā dhyātam eva ca*

**That which is expressed by material words or contemplated by the mind in duality is false. What, therefore, is actually good or bad within this insubstantial world of duality, and how can the extent of such good and bad be measured? SB 11.28.4**

The verses should be explained in terms of the error of mistaking one object for another because of similarity in the objects (*sādṛśya-bhrama*). Duality is false (*dvaitasya avastunaḥ*). The meaning of this section is that Paramātmā is non-different from the world because it is impossible to determine independence of anything else. Nārada speaks these verses to Yudhiṣṭhira.

## 67.

*tvaṁ vāyur agnir avanir viyad ambu mātrāḥ*  
*prāṇendriyāṇi hṛdayaṁ cid anugrahaś ca*  
*sarvaṁ tvam eva saguṇo viguṇaś ca bhūman*  
*nānyat tvad asty api mano-vacasā niruktam*

**O Supreme Lord! You are the air, the earth, fire, sky and water, the objects of sense perception, the life airs, the senses, the inner organ, the pure jīva and the śakti which turns persons towards you*****.*** **You are everything, subtle and gross. Everything expressed, either by the words or by the mind, is nothing but you. SB 7.9.48**

*Hṛdayam* refers to the inner organ composed of mind, intellect, ego and cittā. *Cit* is the pure jīva, *Anugraha* is the śakti which turns people towards the Lord. Everything material *(saguṇaḥ*) and spiritual (*viguṇaḥ*) is you alone. Prahlāda speaks to Nṛsiṁha.

## 68.

The Lord’s difference from the jīva, māyā and the effects of māyā-śakti is made clear:

*yatholmukād visphuliṅgād dhūmād vāpi sva-sambhavāt*  
*apy ātmatvenābhimatād yathāgniḥ pṛthag ulmukāt*  
*bhūtendriyāntaḥ-karaṇāt pradhānāj jīva-saṁjñitāt*  
*ātmā tathā pṛthag draṣṭā bhagavān brahma-saṁjñitaḥ*

**Just as fire is actually different from a blazing coal, and similarly is different from the spark and the smoke, which arise from the fire, though by imagination they appear to be fire, similarly, Bhagavān, possessor of inconceivable powers, who also appears as impersonal Brahman, is different from** ***pradhāna*****, the conscious** ***jīva*****, and the body, senses and** ***antaḥkaraṇa,*** **because he is the witness of the others. SB 3.28.40-41**

The meaning is this. Because it is their cause (*sva-sambhavāt*), fire, their material cause, is different from ember, a spark and smoke, caused by fiery wood. They appear to be fire because of the presence of a portion of fire in them, such as the ability to burn (*ātmatvena abhimatāt*).

Similarly the Lord, the material cause of everything, is different from the jīva, represented by the spark, from pradhāna, represented by the ember, and from the body, senses and *antaḥkaraṇa* which cover the power of the Lord and which are represented by the smoke covering fire. Bhagavān, functioning as antaryāmī, is Paramātmā, his aṁśa (ātmā), and is called Brahman (*brahma-saṁjñitaḥ*) when he sometimes appears as undifferentiated pure consciousness to persons qualified for that. The Lord is different because he is the witness of all conditions, from beginning to end, of all things.

Those who are very foolish think that smoke is fire because of identifying it with the actual fire, since it arises from fire (*sva-sambhavāt*), from blazing wood *(ulmukāt).* The fire, which burns the wood and reveals it, is different from the wood which previously existed (but got burned up). Similarly the seer, having unlimited knowledge and known as Brahman, Paramātmā, and Bhagavān, is different from elements, the senses, the *antaḥkaraṇa*  and jīva, since they are made of his external and marginal śaktis. Kapila speaks the verse.

## 69.

Those who do not respect Paramātmā think the world, non-different from him to be *asat,* unreal. Those who know Paramātmā however accept the world as real, *sat.*

*sad iva manas tri-vṛt tvayi vibhāty asad ā-manujāt*  
*sad abhimṛśanty aśeṣam idam ātmatayātma-vidaḥ*  
*na hi vikṛtiṁ tyajanti kanakasya tad-ātmatayā*  
*sva-kṛtam anupraviṣṭam idam ātmatayāvasitam*

**The mind which does not have faith in you accepts the material world composed of the three guṇas as an illusion. But those who know you see you as the root of everything. Just as things made of gold are not to be rejected, since their substance is actual gold, so this world is to be accepted as real, being a manifestation from the Lord, who created it and then entered within it. SB 10.87.26**

The mind which is not absorbed *(asat)* in you *(tvayi*), but rather absorbed in the world made of three guṇas *(trivṛt*), just seems to exist (*sad iva*). If one does not accept the existence of Paramātmā, though he exists in the universe which is permeated by him, the world (made of mind) cannot be said to exist. This is as meaningless as a sacrificial ladle trying to taste soup without a person present. The non-existent (*asat*) portion (the world) is made of māyā of the three guṇas *(trivṛt*) and mind.

*yad idaṁ manasā vaṁśa cakṣurbhyāṁ śravaṇādibhiḥ*  
*naśvaraṁ gṛhyamāṇaṁ ca viddhi māyā-mano-mayam*

**The material universe that you perceive through your mind, speech, eyes, ears and other senses is a temporary creation that is equivalent to what is made of** ***māyā*** **when you perceive in the waking state and to what is made of the mind when you perceive in the dream state. SB 11.11.7**

The knowers of ātmā however understand this universe (*aśeṣam*), including the conditioned jīva (*ā-manujāt*), as real (*sat),* being your form (*ātmatayā*). They see the world as your real aṁśa.

An example is given. Those who are searching for gold do not consider whether the form is beautiful or ugly. They see it only as gold. The knowers of Paramātmā are similar.

Three reasons for accepting the world as the Lord are given through the example. 1. This world *(idam)* is made by you through the material cause endowed with your śakti *(sva-kṛtam*). 2. Though the effect is created later, you have entered it as antaryāmī without losing your nature as the cause. 3. And at pralaya it remains (*avasitam*) in the form of your śakti. This should be considered in the example. The śrutis speak the verse.

Viṣṇu Purāṇa expresses the same:

*jñāna-svarūpam akhilaṁ jagad etad abuddhayaḥ*  
*artha-svarūpaṁ paśyanto bhramyante moha-samplave*

**Foolish people see the world which is your form as a material thing. They are bewildered in an ocean of illusion.**

*ye tu jñāna-vidaḥ śuddha- cetasas te 'khilaṁ jagat*  
*jñānātmakaṁ prapaśyanti tvād-rūpaṁ parameśvara.*

**O Supreme Lord! Those who know you, having pure minds, see this whole world as non-different from you.**

## 70.

Paramātmā has been proved and vivarta has been refuted. The world is not false like the rope mistaken for a snake but is temporary like a pot. But even though it is not false, the universe in itself cannot be not called *sat* in the true sense (since it sometimes completely disappears) because it does not continue over all three aspects of time. Without necessity of illusion or change operating, an object can still exist as *sat* without producing the faults of illusion of Brahman and change in Brahman, by explaining that the world exists within Paramātmā or his śakti.

*Sad eva saumyedam agra āsīd:* this world (idam) existed as sat before the creation, O gentle one! (Chāndodgya Upaniṣad 6.2.1) The word *sat* indicates that Brahman and his śakti in subtle state had attained oneness. Sat-kārya-vāda operates by taking support of this subtle state.

The following doubt may arise. If the universe which is a manifestation of the material cause is temporary, how can it said to be *sat*? If it is temporary, why is it not included within Vivarata-vāda, since it is erroneous like the shell mistaken for silver? This doubt is removed in the following:

*sata idaṁ utthitaṁ sad iti cen na nu tarka-hataṁ*  
*vyabhicarati kva ca kva ca mṛṣā na tathobhaya-yuk*  
*vyavahṛtaye vikalpa iṣito ’ndha-paramparayā*  
*bhramayati bhāratī ta uru-vṛttibhir uktha-jaḍān*

**The universe arises from the real Lord. If one claims the world is false, that claim cannot be true since it is illogical. Sometimes objects in the world are very different from the Lord. Some say the world is illusory but it is not. The Lord also is not illusory or the cause of illusion. The illusory world is imaginary. There may be illusion in carrying out transactions, giving insubstantial results like transactions of like a series of blind men. The words of the Vedas with their meanings also bewilder persons whose senses are dulled by the sacrificial mantras. SB 10.87.36**

The universe *(idam*) is real, a proven object *(sat).* Since *(yat*) it arose from the Lord (*sataḥ*) it is seen to be non-different from the Lord, just as earrings which arise from gold are non-different from gold. The universe has actually arisen (*utthitam).* It is not (*na*) some false attribution like mistaking shell for silver. The author states his position. Such an idea is defeated by logic (*tarka-hatam)*. Since it comes from the Lord (ablative case *sataḥ*), falsity would contradict the statement of difference (something coming from something.)

“We do not insist on non-difference but we object to saying that there is difference based on something arising from the Lord, like an earring arising from gold. This means they are non-different.” The argument is attacked for a logical fallacy. Sometimes *(kva ca*) the qualities in the cause are not in the effect (*vyabhicarati):* there is no rule that the effects and cause must have completely the same qualities. Though light arises from burning, one does not see burning as a quality of light.

*dve rūpe brahmaṇas tasya mūrtaṁ cāmūrtam eva ca*  
*kṣarākṣara-svarūpe te sarva-bhūteṣv avasthite*  
*akṣaraṁ tat paraṁ brahma kṣaraṁ sarvam idaṁ jagat*

**Brahman has two forms: with a body and without a body. The first is temporary and the second is eternal. They are situated in all beings. The eternal is the supreme Brahman and the temporary is everything in this universe. Viṣṇu Purāṇa 1.22.53-54**

After this it is said:

*eka-deśa-sthitasyāgner jyotsnā vistāriṇī yathā*  
*parasya brahmaṇaḥ śaktis tathedam akhilaṁ jagat*

**Just as the light of a fire situated in one place spreads its illumination, the śakti of the supreme Brahman spreads out as the whole universe. Viṣṇu Purāṇa 1.22.55**

Śrīdhara Svāmī explains this verse as follows. How does the temporary form, different from the Lord, arise from the eternal Lord? This is explained by an example. Just as a light of a lamp, which is different from the lamp, spreads it illumination, so the spreading produced by the śakti of the Lord becomes this whole universe.

Let us return to the verse. “If there is no rule of having the same qualities in the cause and effect, then the Lord and the universe must be in a relationship like shell and silver.” Sometimes there is illusion, such as a shell appearing to be silver. But where an object partakes (*yunakti)* of being visible and can be used (*ubhayam*), it is not illusion *(na tathā mṛṣā*).

“One can see transactions involving actions and objects taking place with false coins made of iron.” Illusion (*vikalpa*) is involved in carrying out the buying and selling (*vyavahṛtayaḥ*) but the actual transaction of selling consisting of objects and actions is not an illusion. Similarly giving worthless coins in charity will not give one pious credits. Or, having purchased poison made from ginger, if one consumes it, thinking it is ginger, one will not become healthy, but will die. Still, the reality of the universe must be accepted since it involves objects and actions. On the one hand, one can certainly have fear of a false snake made of iron, and this is illusion. But the cause of fear is not illusion, being a real object.

Carrying out transactions with false coins is not the same as using real coins, even if is done skillfully. It is like transactions carried out by a series of blind men. Since the transaction cannot be accomplished properly even if done skillfully, it is merely appearance of a genuine transaction, like a blind man leading another blind man. It cannot be considered otherwise. A trace of smoke can be mistaken for a fire. But there is reality in the performance of actions using objects, since this is different from dealing with illusory objects.

The theory of vivarta or illusion having been refuted, the proponents of eternal nature of pious acts come forward. In śruti it is said *apāma somam amrṭā abhūma cāturmāsa-yājinaḥ sukṛtam bhavati:* we drank soma and became immortal; the sacrificers of cāturmāsa attain imperishable results. (Ṛg Veda 8.48.3) Since the results of pious acts are here said to be eternal, how are the results temporary? O Lord! Your words *(bhāratī)* bewilder the foolish people enamored with rituals and śraddhā rites (*uktha-jaḍān*), by their profuse interpretation of the meaning of the statements (*uru-vṛttibhiḥ*).

The meaning is this. The Vedas do not advocate that the results of karmas are eternal, but rather simply praise these pious acts with words that should not be taken literally, for by accepting that ultimately one order is given in the Vedas, other statements resolve into one conclusion. Other statements say the opposite of the above. *Tad yatheha karma-jito lokaḥ kṣīyate evam evāmutra puṇya-jito lokaḥ kṣīyate:* as the results of actions of this life are temporary, so the results attains fomr next life are temporary. (Chāndogya Upaniṣad 8.1.6) Thus people enamored with karma are mistaken. The world is real but temporary since it has the quality of change inherent in it. Bhaṭta says *athavetihāsa-pūrāṇa-prāmāṇyāt sṛṣṭi-pralayāv apīṣyete*: on the authority of Purāṇas and Itihāsa, creation and destruction continually take place.

(another explanation)

In response to the proponent of non-difference, the idea that an object possesses three states of existence (*vyavahārika* or practical, *prātītika* or mental, and *pāramārthika* or spiritual as Brahman) is refuted by showing the difference between these states. A pot’s existence, though useful as an object, fails if we claim it is merely conventional reality (*kva vyabhicarati*), since a different object (like a table) cannot be used in its place to perform the function. It fails as a mere mental object, since it cannot be present in another place, and it fails as a purely spiritual object since it disappears after some time.

Sometimes (*kvā)* things are completely illusory *(mṛṣā*) such as mistaking a shell for silver, since the object cannot be used for action. A real existence is concluded from a combination of these two states (*ubhaya-yuk*): a real object like a pot is combined with illusion, like mistaking shell for silver. The existence of the supreme cause is not like this *(na tathā*). Everywhere, at all times, the supreme cause acts as the basis for all actions performed according to the particular upādhi.

Thus the universe, like the pot, is temporary, since it changes, though it is useful in performing acts. It is not simply a perceptual reality nor is it permanent reality. Since one can see the difference between these states, how can one state become another?

The objection concerning false coins is answered. That which is a substitute (*vikalpaḥ*) can be used according to one’s desire (*vyavahṛtaye iṣitaḥ*). The meaning is this. If one thinks of using false coins, the transaction cannot be accomplish by that means. However genuine coins will work since a different goal has arisen in the heart of the person transacting. False coins are representative of other forms of deceit as well. If one is conscious of some deceit like “I gave the coins in your house” or “I will give coins later” one can try to complete a transaction, even without coins. Therefore the transaction itself, using objects for a purpose, is itself true. In the manner of “even a hundred blind men cannot see,” in the absence of real coins, a truthful transaction cannot be completed by any amount of fake coins. Such behavior takes place with useless means (*andha paramarayā)*. Just as seeing cannot be completed by a hundred blind men, a truthful transaction cannot be accomplished by any amount of false coins.

In this way, the philosophy that existence is mere knowledge (*vijñāna-vāda*) is refuted.

Śaṅkara, in his Brahma-sūtra commentary on denial of the Buddhist claim that everything is impressions, refers to a series of blind men, saying “If one assumes that impressions are without beginning (one impression producing another impression, having no need of external objects to create the impressions), it is unsubstantial, subject to infinite regression like a series of blind men. One’s intentions cannot be fulfilled.”

This should be said. “By whom was this gold purchased?” Someone says, “By a blind man.” How did he know it was gold?” “He was informed by another blind man.” “How did that blind man know?” “He was informed by another blind man.” In this way, by the serious of blind men one can never come to a conclusion. However, if in the series there is one man with eyes, who can reveal truth, the process can be completed. Just as the process is accomplished by one man with sight among the blind, so the transaction is accomplished if there is one real coin. Transactions are accomplished by real objects. Just as investigating gold actually starts with a person having eyes, if a real coin exists, and it is understood to be genuine through examination by experts in the matter, it becomes the basis of producing a false coin.

Thus it is proved that the universe, represented by the coin, is real, different from an object of illusion, since it can actually be used in transacting. This happens because Paramātmā arranges the components of the transactions. Therefore he is praised by Brahmā and others. *Satyasya yonim:* the Lord is the source of reality. (SB 10.2.26) *Tat satyam ity ācakṣate*: he is called reality. (Taittirīya Upanisād 2.6) The rest of the verse can be explained as in the first explanation.

## 71.

The reality of the universe has been accepted. It is temporary or destructible. Its destruction is not absolute. It simply becomes invisible in a state of non-manifestation since it was admitted that the universe is dormant within the Lord (*sat-kārya*). *Yad bhūtaṁ bhavac ca bhaviṣyac ca*: the universe exists in the past, present and future. (Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad 3.8.3) It is not a fact that the universe is non-existent in non-manifestation, like sublating the idea of silver on finding it was actually shell. The universe is in a subtle state during its non-manifestation.

*Paṭavac ca:* the universe is non-different from Brahman as cloth is non-different from threads. (Brahma-sūtra 2.1.19)

According to this, the non-manifested is very subtle. Thus its existence is very different from an illusion of silver. But unlike the ātmā, the universe does not exist in one continuous state without changes. To strengthen this conclusion the śrutis say afterwards:

*na yad idam agra āsa na bhaviṣyad ato nidhanād*  
*anu mitam antarā tvayi vibhāti mṛṣaika-rase*  
*ata upamīyate draviṇa-jāti-vikalpa-pathair*  
*vitatha-mano-vilāsam ṛtam ity avayanty abudhāḥ*

**If the universe were not created after destruction, it could not be said that it came from you. After destruction and during maintenance, it exists in you, who remain full of pleasure. It appears false to others, by imagining it to be made of illusory objects. Those who speak these mental concoctions and those claim that the world is completely non-different from you are fools. SB 10.87.37**

If (*yat)* the universe (*idam*) did not exist previously (*agre āsa),* it could not exist at all. Absence of the augment *a* in the verb (*abhavisya*t) is poetic license. It would be like a flower in the sky. The śrutis say that universe existed previously. *Sad eva saumyedam agra āsīt*: O gentle one, this universe existed previously. (Chāndogya Upaniṣad 6.2.1) *ātmā vā yad idam agra āsīt:* the Lord existed previous to the universe. (Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad 1.4.1) This universe in a causal state was situated in a subtle condition, one with the Lord and then manifested as an effect by expanding. Because it is destructible (*nidhanāt*), it appears illusory like mistaking shell for silver. Its existence cannot be proved. It is inferred (*anu mitam*) to exist (*vibhāti*) in you during creation and not before or after.

The reason for the real creation (rather than an illusion) is given. You do not have a taste for bliss from other objects. Other objects cannot manifest since you experience in this way. And thus how can low objects like shell be imposed as illusion on you?

*dadhati sakṛn manas tvayi ya ātmani nitya-sukhe*  
*na punar upāsate puruṣa-sāra-harāvasathān*

**Having turned their minds toward you, the ever-blissful soul of all existence (*****ātmani nitya-sukhe*****), they no longer dedicate themselves to serving family life at home, which simply robs a man of his good qualities. SB 10.87.35**

By accepting that you transform through your inconceivable śakti, though you are unchanging, we compare the universe to the types of different objects (*vikalpa)* made of matter (*dravīna-jāta),* not to some imaginary substance. Since this is so, only the unintelligent think (avayanti) that the universe is a form of Brahman *(ṛtam*), a mental error (*vitatha-mano-vilāsam*), since the Lord can actually never be the basis of illusion. *Ṛta* means “The Lord is devoid of any relationship with falsity.”

The intention of the proponents of Sat-kārya-vāda is now explained. The pot produced by clay along with a potter either existed or did not exist (previously). If it existed, it is being made again. If it does not exist, how can it be produced, since it is impossible to have a relationship with the activities of the potter, like holding a flower in the sky, since such things are illusory? Therefore the form is not manifest, but the pot also does not absolutely not exist. It is situated as a lump of clay, unmanifest as a pot. Just as it is produced by adding activities of the potter to produce it, so the universe is produced by addition of activities of your natural śaktis, situated in you.

Persons who claim to explain the Vedānta but have opposite ideas are contrary to the Vedānta. Mind is a result of the elements. This is well known. It is contrary to logic to say the world is a mental state. It is impossible to make mind and ahaṅkāra from mind (since mind cannot be the cause and the effect.) This fault applies to atheistic philosophies also. This is criticized in Padma Purāṇa:

*Śruṭayaḥ smṛtayas caiva yuktayaś ceśvaraṁ param*  
*Vadanti tad- viruddhaṁ yo vadet tasmān na cādhamaḥ*

**The śrutis, smṛtis and Vedānta speak of the supreme Lord. There is no one lower that a person who speaks contrary to this.**

*asatyam apratiṣṭhaṁ te jagad āhur anīśvaram \|*  
*aparaspara-saṁbhūtaṁ kim anyat kāma-haitukam \|\|8\|\|*

**They say the world is false, without a foundation, and without a creator. It arises by the innate properties of matter. It arises by lust alone. BG 16.8**

The atheists are described in the Gītā. The world is false (*asatyam*) and cannot be designated (*apratiṣṭham)*, since it is indescribable, being existent and non-existent. The conception of God is false (*aniśvaram*) since the world arises from continuous, beginningless ignorance *(jagat aparaspara-sambhūtam*). A*paraspara* means continuous action. It arises from desire alone like a dream. Verse 16.8 shows the faulty mentality of such persons and verse 16.9 criticizes their goal. They think the universe is created by jīva’s ignorance, a covering on the greatness of Brahman.

It is said *tad-ananyatvam:* from the Brahman arises the world which is non-different from it. (Brahma-sūtra 2.1.15) The commentary of Śaṅkāra says, “The śrutis and smṛtis say that prakṛti is the māyā-śakti of the omniscient īśvara. Names and forms, composed of vidyā and avidyā, appearing as if the Lord himself, act as a seed of the material world and are indescribable as both truth and illusion.”

This however contradicts the words of the Lord. Śaṅkara says that names and forms are neither real nor unreal, being vyavahārika. Bhāgavatam says that names and forms are real, arising from the śakti of the Lord. 3 *Vidyāvidye mama tanū viddhi*: know that vidyā and avidyā are my śaktis. (SB 11.11.3) Thus the above was explained according to Māyāvāda. Śiva in speaks to Pārvatī about the contrary philosophies in Padma Purāṇa:

*māyāvādam asac chāstraṁ pracchannaṁ baudham ucyate*  
*mayaiva kathitaṁ devi kalau brāhmaṇa-rūpiṇā*  
*brahmaṇaś cāparaṁ rūpaṁ nirguṇaṁ vaksyate māyā*  
*sarvasya jagato ‘py asya mohanārthaṁ kalau yuge*  
*vedānte tu mahā-śāstre māyāvādam avaidikam*  
*mayaiva vakṣyate devi jagatāṁ nāśa-kāraṇāt*

**Māyāvāda scripture, called covered Buddhism, was spoken by me in the form of a brāhmaṇa in Kali yuga. I will speak about the inferior Brahman without qualities in order to bewilder the whole world in Kali yuga. I will speak Māyāvāda which is not authorized by the Vedas, in the great scripture called Vedānta, to destroy the world.**

It is also said in Padma Purāṇa that by the order of the Lord this philosophy was created to bewilder the demons. In Śiva Purāṇa it is said:

*dvāparādau yuge bhūtvā kalayā manuṣādiṣu*  
*svāgamaiḥ kalpitais tvaṁ ca janān mad-vimukhān kuru*

**At the end of Dvāpāra yuga, turn the people against me with imaginary scriptures produced by your expansion among men.**

The Lord speaks the above verse.

In Nṛsiṁha Purāṇa Yama speaks:

*viṣādhara-kaṇa-bhakṣa-śaṅkaroktīr*  
*daśabala-pañcaśikhākṣapāda-vādān*  
*mahad api su-vicārya loka-tantraṁ*  
*bhagavad-upāstim ṛte na siddhir asti*

**The words Patañjali, Kaṇāda, Śaṅkara, Buddha, Sāṅkhya, and Gautama, though well composed as systems of philosophy for this world, are not successful since they do not worship of the Lord.**

All their philosophical works, not mantra sections of the Vedas, are condemned. As well specific names are mentioned. They cannot be viewed in any other way. The views of the author of Vedānta-sūtras in statements like *ānanda-mayo ‘bhāsāt* are completely spoiled by their words. What is praiseworthy in their works is the small portion which accepts the existence of the Lord. The extremely atheistic parts should be rejected.

Thus the conclusion is that the independent Lord is the creator of everything. The jīva, by his ignorance or his own powers, is not the creator. This is mentioned by Bādarāyaṇa in many places. *Saṁjñā-mūrti-kliptis tu trivṛt kurvata upadeśāt:* the Lord mixes the names and forms after making the elements threefold, because that is the direct statement. (Brahma-sutra 2.4.20)

*Tan-mano ‘sṛjata manaḥ prajāpatim*: the Lord created the mind and the mind created Prajāpati. Mind here means Aniruddha, the presiding deity of the all minds. This is similar to *bahu syāṁ prajāyeya:* may I become many and increase progeny. (Chāndogya Upaniṣad 6.2.3) Or the Lord created a decision and his decision created Prajāpati. The supreme Lord, possessor of inconceivable śakti by his nature, should not produce insignificant illusions since he is the lord of all cintāmaṇis, the cintāmaṇi personified. He thus has nothing to do with producing fake gold.

Madhva quotes the following śruti. *Athainam āhuḥ satya-karmeti satyaṁ hy evedaṁ viśvam asṛajata*: they call the Lord true action; this Lord created this true world. As well it is said:

*satya-vrataṁ satya-paraṁ tri-satyaṁ*  
*satyasya yoniṁ nihitaṁ ca satye*  
*satyasya satyam ṛta-satya-netraṁ*  
*satyātmakaṁ tvāṁ śaraṇaṁ prapannāḥ*

**O Lord, you are true to your promise, truth incarnate, pervading all aspects of time with truth, before the creation, during its existence and after its destruction. You are the revealer of true words and vision. We surrender to you, the embodiment of truth. SB 10.2.26**

You are true to your vow (*satya-vratam*), true to surrender (*satya-param*), true in creation, maintenance and destruction (*tri-satyam*), the real cause of the real universe (*satya-yonim).* You are situated as antaryāmī within the true universe. You are the real cause of reality (*satyasya satyam)*. You inspire true words and vision *(ṛta-satya-netram*). Your form is true (*satyātmakam*). This is the meaning of these phrases. The first three phrases and *satyasya yonim* which are followed by *nihitam ca satye* can be given another meaning out of context only with difficulty, using incompatible argument (like half an aged woman). It is proper that the Lord speaks truthfully what he had promised. Otherwise the natural meaning and order would be broken when Brahmā uttered the prayers. Therefore the universe is real.

The śrutis speak to the Lord.

## 72.

The phrase *na yad idam agra āsā*: the universe did not exist previously (SB 10.87.37) should be understood to mean that the material world remains in dormant state during pralaya, according to Sat-kārya-vāda. When absolute destruction takes place with liberation (for the jīva), there is no destruction of the elements like earth as well. The jīva is released from accepting material bodies gross and subtle forever in atyantika-pralaya.4 It is not proper that the elements composed of Paramātmā’s natural śaktis should be absolutely destroyed when a jīva attains realization. When Parīkṣit and others attain liberation, one hears that the places made of portions gross elements like earth in which their bodies were situated remained. The subtle elements such as buddhi, portions of hiraṇyagarbha (the subtle body), will also remain. Absolute destruction (*atyantika-laya*) simply means that a jīva gives up his adhyāsa or covering.

*ghaṭe bhinne ghaṭākāśa ākāśaḥ syād yathā purā*  
*evaṁ dehe mṛte jīvo brahma sampadyate punaḥ*

**When a pot is broken, the portion of sky within the pot becomes sky element as before. In the same way, when the gross and subtle bodies die, the living entity becomes Brahman. SB 12.5.5**

*ahaṁ brahma paraṁ dhāma brahmāhaṁ paramaṁ padam*  
*evaṁ samīkṣya cātmānam ātmany ādhāya niṣkale*  
*daśantaṁ takṣakaṁ pāde lelihānaṁ viṣānanaiḥ*  
*na drakṣyasi śarīraṁ ca viśvaṁ ca pṛthag ātmanaḥ*

**“I am that Brahman, the supreme state of being. Brahman is I, the supreme position.” The esoteric meaning is: I am the worshipper of the Lord. I am a conscious particle of the Lord. I belong to the Lord.” Considering this and considering the lotus feet of the Lord, surrender to the Lord. You will not see Takṣaka, your body or the universe separate from the Lord, because of fainting with bliss on seeing Kṛṣṇa. 5 Considering this, place yourself in the Brahman devoid of** ***upādhi*****s. You will not see Takṣaka biting your foot with fire and poison and licking it with his tongue. You will not see your body or the universe to be separate from Brahman. SB 12.5.11-12**

In verses such as this, the relation with upādhis is rejected but falsity of the world is not advocated.

Intelligence

Intelligence and other factors should be explained. The intelligence and senses are considered Paramātmā’s natural śaktis, because they are take shelter of Paramātmā, are revealed by him and cannot be separated from him.

*buddhīndriyārtha-rūpeṇa jñānaṁ bhāti tad-āśrayam*  
*dṛśyatvāvyatirekābhyām ādy-antavad avastu yat*

**Brahman manifests in the forms of intelligence, the senses and the objects of sense perception, since it is their ultimate basis, reveals them and is essential for their existence. Whatever has a beginning and an end is insubstantial because of being the opposite. SB 12.4.23**

Knowledge or Paramātmā manifests as the internal and external senses. He is non-different from them since he is their shelter. Neuter gender is poetic license. Thus there is also great difference as there is difference between a king and servant. Other reasons are given. He is the revealer of the senses (*dṛśyatva)*. And they cannot exist without Paramātmā (*avyatirekha)*. Intelligence and the senses are the Lord in the sense that they are composed of the natural śakti of Paramātmā, just as fire spreads light but is non-different from the light (*eka-deśa-sthitasyāgner jyotsnā vistāriṇī yathā)*.

Illusions which have beginning and end (such as mistaking shell for silver) lack non-difference from their source *(ādy-antavat avastu yat*). The illusion of silver can never be identical with the shell, since shell does not act as the illusory silver’s cause and shelter, does not have the power to reveal illusion of silver, and lacks non-difference from illusion of silver. The silver is not non-different from the shell. (If the world which is illusion arises from Brahman) by knowing Brahman one cannot know the illusion (since the two are not non-different). This contradicts the promise that by knowing Brahman, all things become known. This concept of illusion arising from Brahman is included in Asat-kārya-vāda (the effect is not inherent in the cause).

## 73.

An example of shelter and object of shelter by distinguishing the aṁśa from the substance acting as the shelter is given to make things clear.

*dīpaś cakṣuś ca rūpaṁ ca jyotiṣo na pṛthag bhavet*  
*evaṁ dhīḥ khāni mātrāś ca na syur anyatamād ṛtāt*

**A lamp, the eye that views by the light of that lamp, and the visible form that is viewed are non-different from the element light. In the same way, intelligence, the senses and sense perceptions have no existence separate from the supreme reality, although that Absolute Truth remains totally distinct from them. SB 12.4.24**

The lamp, eye and form are non-different from the light because they are aṁśas of the element light. Similarly intelligence and the senses are non-different from Paramātmā (*ṛtāt).* Moreover, just as the element light is not contaminated by the faults of the lamp or eye, so Paramātmā is not contaminated by faults of intelligence or senses. Paramātmā is totally distinct *(anyatamāt).*

## 74.

It was stated that intelligence, the senses and sense perceptions were composed of the natural śakti of Paramātmā. They were also described as having faults, because of impurity of matter. Thus Paramātmā, endowed with his antaraṅga-śakti and taṭastha-śakti, is also different from the elements which are part of the bahiraṅga-śakti. In order that one gives up the covering of the senses, sense perceptions and intelligence, how they cover the pure jīva is described:

*buddher jāgaraṇaṁ svapnaḥ suṣuptir iti cocyate*  
*māyā-mātram idaṁ rājan nānātvaṁ pratyag-ātmani*

**The three states of intelligence are called waking consciousness, sleep and deep sleep. But, my dear King, the variegated experiences created for the pure living entity by these different states are nothing more than illusion. SB 12.4.25**

Waking, sleeping and deep sleep are functions of intelligence. They are illusory states (*māyā-mātaram) c*reated by a covering made of māyā, of great variety (*nānātvam*), called viśva, taijasa and prajñā, for the pure jīva (*pratyag-ātmani*).

## 75.

Paramātmā has no contact with the material world with its intelligence and senses.

*yathā jala-dharā vyomni bhavanti na bhavanti ca*  
*brahmaṇīdaṁ tathā viśvam avayavy udayāpyayāt*

**Just as clouds in the sky come into being and are then destroyed, the material universe with its parts is created and destroyed within Brahman. SB 12.4.26**

A cloud, which is vapor, and is the transformation of water, heat, and air in the form of wind, becomes visible in the sky with the appearance of its whole form and then becomes invisible. But the clouds do not touch the sky. Similarly this universe, manifest *(udaya)* by the glance of the Lord, becomes visible in Brahman and then disappears. After disappearing, it remains in a subtle form, since its cause endowed with śakti to produce the universe remains. Thus it is said *sato ‘bhivyañjakaḥ kālaḥ: prakrṭi* is the material cause; the *puruṣa* is the foundational cause; time, the indirect cause, is the agitator of *prakṛti;* I am all three. SB 11.24.19

Satkārya-vada

## 76.

An example is given to explain that the cause continues to exist.

*satyaṁ hy avayavaḥ proktaḥ sarvāvayavinām iha*  
*vinārthena pratīyeran paṭasyevāṅga tantavaḥ*

**O King! The cause of the universe is said to be factual just as threads of a cloth exist even without the cloth. SB 12.4.27**

The cause of all gross objects (*sarvāvayavinām*) is said to be real. This is seen in the world. Threads exist without the gross form (*vina arthena)* of the cloth.

## 77.

The existence of the effect in the cause is explained by negating illusion:

*yat sāmānya-viśeṣābhyām upalabhyeta sa bhramaḥ*  
*anyonyāpāśrayāt sarvam ādy-antavad avastu yat*

**What is experienced by Brahman and its effect is an illusion since they are mutually dependent. Everything has a beginning and end and therefore is unreal. SB 12.4.28**

The meaning is this. If it is said that previously the universe did not exist in a subtle state, but only pure Brahman existed everywhere (*sāmānya),* Brahman would transform into the universe, a particular effect (*viśeṣa*) by some śakti as a cause and would become non-existent or illusory *(bhramaḥ*), since what is produced by the cause becoming the effect is an illusion. This is Vivarata-vāda. Pure Brahman transforms into the universe by śakti in the form of ignorance. We do not agree with this view since Sat-kārya-vāda with pariṇāma of śakti was already proved.

## 78.

“You tell us about some effect which did not previously exist, similar to that in Ārambha-vāda Vaiśeṣikas and Nyāyikas say that an object though compromised of atomic parts, is a completely new creation. The effect, the object, is not present in subtle form in the cause, atoms. 6 and Vivarta-vāda.”

*anyonyāpāśrayāt sarvam ādy-antavad avastu yat*

**They are mutually dependent. Everything has a beginning and end and therefore is unreal. SB 12.4.28**

The effect, which did not previously exist, has beginning and end and cannot be defined as real (*avastu)* since there is interdependence of cause and effect: as long as the effect does not appear, earth or shell as a cause is not accomplished. If the cause is not accomplished, the effect does not appear. The cause and effect suffer from the fault of interdependence. If the cause is accomplished the effect can exist.

On the other hand, actually the effect exists in a subtle condition in the cause. The effect existed previously. The cause is accomplished by the gross material cause in the form of clay etc.

## 79.

The universe is the natural śakti of Paramātmā. That is summarized here:

*vikāraḥ khyāyamāno 'pi pratyag-ātmānam antarā*  
*na nirūpyo 'sty aṇur api syāc cec cit-sama ātma-vat*

**This universe and even a single atom within it have no definition without reference to the Supreme Soul. If that were so, then objects related to the Lord would be spiritual and non-different from the Lord. SB 12.4.29**

Though manifested (*khyāyamānaḥ)*, even a slight transformation (universe) cannot be defined as independent of Paramātmā (*pratyag-ātmānam).* What is stated is oneness. If it exists without Paramātmā, the universe would be equal to his spiritual form-- self-manifesting and eternal like Paramātmā (*ātmavat).*

## 80.

“If there is no change without Paramātmā, a state of absence of upādhis cannot take place, since Paramātmā has upādhis in the form of the universe. Is absence of upādhis (Paramātmā) different from having upādhis (the universe)?”

*na hi satyasya nānātvam avidvān yadi manyate*  
*nānātvaṁ chidrayor yadvaj jyotiṣor vātayor iva*

**There is no material duality in the Absolute Truth. If a person thinks there is duality he is ignorant like seeing space within two pots as different space, or two lamps as different fire or two prāṇas in two bodies as different prāṇa, when actually there is only one space, one fire and one prāṇa. SB 13.4.30**

There is no variety in Paramātmā *(satyasya).* If you think there is variety, you are ignorant *(avidvān*), like seeing the difference (*nānātvam*) between sky in the great sky and in a pot, difference between light in the sky and in a room or air outside and inside the body.

## 81.

Since nothing can exist without Paramātmā (SB 12.4.29), all words describe Paramātmā. That is explained with an example:

*yathā hiraṇyaṁ bahudhā samīyate*  
*nṛbhiḥ kriyābhir vyavahāra-vartmasu*  
*evaṁ vacobhir bhagavān adhokṣajo*  
*vyākhyāyate laukika-vaidikair janaiḥ*

**Just gold is perceived in different forms by various types of manufacturing in the ordinary world, the Supreme Lord, inaccessible to material senses, is described in various words, both ordinary and Vedic, by different types of men. SB 12.4.31**

Gold is known by many different names, in the form of gold earrings and bracelet. Similarly the Lord is expressed by all words both secular and Vedic. It is said *sarva-nāmābhideyaś ca sarva-vedeḍitaś ca saḥ:*  the Lord is worshipped by all names in the Vedas. (Skanda Purāṇa).

## 82.

Having explained that the world is the natural śakti of Paramātmā and hinting that the jīva by knowledge can destroy the world (his relationship with it), the author explains the ahaṅkāra, which creates an upādhi and is made of Paramātmā’s śakti, but is an obstacle to revealing jīva’s svarūpa, in order to teach rejection of its adhyāsa (imposition) in order to attain liberation.

*yathā ghano 'rka-prabhavo 'rka-darśito*  
*hy arkāṁśa-bhūtasya ca cakṣuṣas tamaḥ*  
*evaṁ tv ahaṁ brahma-guṇas tad-īkṣito*  
*brahmāṁśakasyātmana ātma-bandhanaḥ*

**The cloud is a product of the sun and is revealed by the sun. But the cloud covers the eye, another portion of the sun. The effects of Brahman are revealed by Brahman. Similarly the ahaṅkāra, the effect of Brahman, makes the jīva, a portion of Brahman, bind himself up. SB 12.4.32**

The rays of the sun become transformed into clouds and pour rain for it is said:

*agnau prāstāhutiḥ samyag ādityam upatiṣṭhate*  
*ādityāj jāyate vṛṣṭir vṛṣṭer annaṁ tataḥ prajāḥ*

**Oblations offered to Agni go directly to the sun. From the sun comes rain. From the rain comes food and from food comes progeny.**

The meaning is this. The power of the sun, revealed (*darśitaḥ)* only by the sun, becomes a dense cloud *(ghanaḥ*), a form of darkness on earth and heaven for the eyes, though arising from a portion of the sun. Similarly the material ahaṅkāra (*aham)*, arising as an effect of Paramātmā’s śakti (*brahma-guṇaḥ),* revealed by Paramātmā (*tad-īkṣitaḥ),* becomes an obstacle to revealing the svarūpa of the jīva, who is a small aṁśa of Paramātmā as taṭastha-śakti (*brahmāṁśakasya*).

## 83.

Giving up the adhyāsa (mistaken identity) does not take place by itself but by the strength of inquiry about Paramātmā. To express this, the previous example is continued.

*ghano yadārka-prabhavo vidīryate*  
*cakṣuḥ svarūpaṁ ravim īkṣate tadā*  
*yadā hy ahaṅkāra upādhir ātmano*  
*jijñāsayā naśyati tarhy anusmaret*

**When the cloud produced from the sun is destroyed, the eye can see its own form and the sun. Similarly, when the spirit soul destroys his material covering of false ego by inquiring into Paramātmā, he realizes the ātmā and the Lord. SB 12.4.33**

The cloud produced from the power of the sun is not destroyed by the power of the eye but by the power of the sun. Ahaṅkāra flees or is destroyed, by inquiry about Paramātmā (*ātmanaḥ),* by his mercy. This shows that ahaṅkāra is not destroyed just by knowledge gained by a person. Vivarta-vāda does not accept this point. Ahaṅkāra is here an upādhi on the jīva, and does refer to the ahaṅkāra arising from jīva’s svarūpa.

The eye sees the real form of the sun after attaining qualification by strength of the sun, without the covering of great darkness caused by the cloud. The eye then knows its own svarūpa (*svarūpam īkṣate*) or manifests its power. Sometimes while seeing itself, the eye also sees the sun. Similarly one becomes qualified to meditate on Paramātmā.

## 84.

This is explained further.

*yadaivam etena viveka-hetinā*  
*māyā-mayāhaṅkaraṇātma-bandhanam*  
*chittvācyutātmānubhavo 'vatiṣṭhate*  
*tam āhur ātyantikam aṅga samplavam*

**O King! When the illusory false ego that binds the soul has been cut off with the sword of discriminating knowledge and one remains fixed in one’s svarūpa with firm realization of Acyuta, the Supreme Soul, it is called the** ***ātyantika-pralaya*****. SB 12.4.34**

By the previously mentioned sword of discrimination, bondage caused by the ahaṅkāra produced by māyā, not the ahaṅkāra inherent in jīva’s svarūpa, is cut. One remains situated in one’s svarūpa *(avatiṣṭhate).* Not only that, one has realization *(anubhavaḥ*) of Paramātmā (*ātmā*) named Acyuta. Śukadeva speaks the verse.

## 85.

The following is the view of one group. The Supreme Lord has two śaktis: svarūpa-śakti and māyā-śakti. The svarūpa-śakti reveals the powers of the Lord. The māyā-śakti shows the creation of the universe to the jīvas bewildered by its illusory powers. Because of this māyā, a single person endowed with various types of knowledge performs actions in the world. This is not the perception of the Advaitins since in this philosophy an object (universe) is revealed by real agent (the Lord) to a real seer (jīva) by a real śakti (māyā-śakti). Let that be.

*satyaṁ na satyaṁ śrī-kṛṣṇa-pādābjāmodam antarā*  
*jagat satyam asatyaṁ vā ko 'yaṁ tasmin durāgrahaḥ*

**Why waste time speculating whether the fragrance of Kṛṣṇa’s lotus feet is real or unreal, whether the universe is real or unreal?**

One should understand the truth of a real world as stated in SB 10.87.36 by accepting Śrīdhara Svāmī’s commentary. Since he concludes that non-difference exists in this world, some conclude that he also infers non-difference of Vaikuṇṭha and other objects. We can confirm the qualities of an object capable of worldly perception by examples of other objects proved by perception. However, what is not subject to worldly perception can only be understood by persons who know scriptures and this cannot be changed at any time. In establishing that the jīva and the Lord are non-different, the non-difference means that they both have conscious forms.

Thus the supreme Lord creates the universe by his natural māyā-śakti and jīva is bewildered by it. Eight verses destroy doubt through questions and answers. Vidura speaks:

*brahman kathaṁ bhagavataś cin-mātrasyāvikāriṇaḥ*  
*līlayā vāpi yujyeran nirguṇasya guṇāḥ kriyāḥ*

**O brāhmaṇa! How is it possible that the Lord, who is spiritual, without change, and beyond the guṇas, becomes connected with actions and guṇas even if it is play for him? SB 3.7.2**

O brāhmaṇa! The Lord (*bhagavataḥ*), endowed with powers in Vaikuṇṭhā, has a svarūpa of consciousness naturally through his svarūpa-śakti (*cinmātrasya).* He is untouched by the guṇas (*nirguṇasya*). He does not undergo transformation (*avikāriṇaḥ)* like a material agent, since he is eternally endowed with unlimited forms having unlimited activities arising from his svarūpa-śakti and does not attain another condition on performing various actions.

How can actions of creation and destruction, and the guṇas, touch the Lord? Since they contradict his nature as a conscious substance, the guṇas and actions should not contact him. They do not contaminate him since, as the Lord, he acts out of play *(līlayā vāpi).* The question is asked while accepting the fact that the Lord is consciousness alone, without guṇas and without change. The Lord may have a form of consciousness alone. But how does he accept the guṇas and material actions? That is the question.

## 86.

For the Lord who is consciousness alone, lowly guṇas and actions are impossible. The question is double. Do they contact the Lord even if it is done as play *(līlayā)?*

*krīḍāyām udyamo ’rbhasya kāmaś cikrīḍiṣānyataḥ*  
*svatas-tṛptasya ca kathaṁ nivṛttasya sadānyataḥ*

***Kāma*** **is the impetus for playing seen in children. The impetus for the Lord’s pastimes should be different. How can the desire for play in the Lord arise from** ***kāma*****, since the Lord is self-satisfied? Since nothing exists except the Lord how can he play because of the jīva and the universe? SB 3.7.3**

The impetus of children in playing is desire. The Lord’s desire for play should be different (*anyataḥ*) in terms of objects and other children inspiring the action. The Lord is satisfied in himself, by the powers of his svarūpa. He is always detached. How can he desire to play because of jīvas or the universe *(anyataḥ)?*

## 87.

One should not say the Lord does not contact the guṇas or perform actions. That is well known:

*asrākṣīd bhagavān viśvaṁ guṇa-mayyātma-māyayā*  
*tayā saṁsthāpayaty etad bhūyaḥ pratyapidhāsyati*

**The Lord creates this universe by māyā composed of the three guṇas, and maintains it by māyā and destroys it by māyā. SB 3.7.4**

The Lord creates the universe by māyā which is under his shelter and endowed with the three guṇas. He maintains it (*saṁsthāyayati)* and again destroys it by reversal (*pratyapidhāsyati)*.

## 88.

How does the jīva become bewildered by māyā?

*deśataḥ kālato yo ’sāv avasthātaḥ svato ’nyataḥ*  
*aviluptāvabodhātmā sa yujyetājayā katham*

**How can the jīva whose knowledge cannot be destroyed by place, time, condition, nature or other cause become associated with ignorance? SB 3.7.5**

How does the jīva *(asau)* whose knowledge cannot be overcome place, time, condition or nature become associated with ignorance *(ajayā*)? Knowledge cannot be reduced by distance (place) or defects of a place as is the case with the eye’s ability to see. Knowledge of the jīva is not destroyed by time, like lightning (which vanishes after a moment). It is not destroyed by circumstance, like memory. It cannot be destroyed by itself, like the illusion of silver in the shell. It cannot be destroyed by other objects such as a pot. Knowledge cannot be reduced by other things such as the pot since ātmā is the shelter of unimpeded knowledge inherent in its svarūpa.

## 89.

Another contradiction is pointed out:

*bhagavān eka evaiṣa sarva-kṣetreṣv avasthitaḥ*  
*amuṣya durbhagatvaṁ vā kleśo vā karmabhiḥ kutaḥ*

**The Lord is situated in all bodies. Why does the jīva then suffer from ignorance and become afflicted by karmas? SB 3.7.6**

The one Lord, Paramātmā is also situated in all bodies of all jīvas. Then why should the jīva (*amuṣya)* lose his inherent knowledge (*durbhagatvam*) and suffer from karma? This should not be so. If two objects are situated in water, why should one be affected by water and not the other?

## 90.

*seyaṁ bhagavato māyā yan nayena virudhyate*  
*īśvarasya vimuktasya kārpaṇyam uta bandhanam*

**This māyā which cannot be understood by logic and which belongs to the Supreme Lord but is not his svarūpa, is the cause of deprivation and ignorance for the jīva who has the possibility of realizing the form, knowledge and bliss of the Lord. SB 3.7.9**

That *(sāḥ),* by which creation takes place, is the śakti called māyā, belonging to the Lord *(bhagavataḥ*) who possesses inconceivable svarūpa śakti. This māyā (*yat*) is contrary to logic (*nayena virudhyate*): it is inconceivable. Though the two śaktis are both inconceivable, because māyā is manifested by the Lord (being his śakti) and because the svarūpa-śakti is antaraṅga, dealing only with the spiritual world, the Lord is not touched by the guṇas of māyā and its activities of making the universe.

The Lord is not just consciousness alone. There is another meaning by making different words out of the same statement. It is true that the Lord does not contact the guṇas or material actions but the bahiraṅga-śakti called māyā contacts the guṇas, though he possesses the guṇas. By māyā which obtains strength by taking shelter of the Lord, karma for actions performed is imposed on the jīva. It is said *ṛte ṛtham yat pratīyeta:* by māyā, objects are perceived as real. (SB 2.9.33)

That māyā is described in the verse. Because of māyā *(yat)* which takes shelter of the Lord, the Lord is not made into an unworthy object *(na yena).* (Māyā does the work of bewildering the jīva, not the Lord.) One cannot criticize the sun for movement or various colors produced by its shadow. By this, attributing bad qualities to the Lord is avoided. Thus the verse is like *itthaṁ bhūta-guṇo hari* (SB 1.7.11), praising the nature of the Lord. Thus, a special meaning, favorable to the main meaning, appears based on suggestion of words *(śabda-dhvani): yat na yena virudyate instead of nayena virudhyate.* Another meaning is “Because of māyā and his actions in relation to her, the Lord is not made into an impersonal object, without actions.”

In the Ninth Chapter of Sixth Canto it is said *duravabodha iva tavāyam*: it seems difficult to understand your actions in relation to the material world. Then again with *tatra bhavān* (SB 6.9.35) the question arises whether the Lord as antaryāmī is an enjoyer like the jīva in accepting piety and sin. Then in SB 6.9.36 the conclusion is reached by indicating that the Lord has inconceivable śakti.

na hi virodha ubhayaṁ bhagavaty aparimita-guṇa-gaṇa īśvare 'navagāhya-māhātmye 'rvācīna-vikalpa-vitarka-vicāra-pramāṇābhāsa-kutarka-śāstra-kalilāntaḥkaraṇāśraya-duravagraha-vādināṁ vivādānavasara uparata-samasta-māyāmaye kevala evātma-māyām antardhāya ko nv artho durghaṭa iva bhavati svarūpa-dvayābhāvāt.

Those two conditions seen in you are not a contradiction. What is impossible for you, who are Bhagavān, full of six qualities, full of unlimited qualities, the supreme controller, whose glories cannot be understood by the non-devotee, who are beyond the arguments of stubborn philosophers whose hearts are disturbed by deliberating on scriptures without touching the truth, through speculation, conjecture, judgment and false proofs? You are beyond all material qualities, you are pure spirit, but you screen yourself from view by your *yoga-māyā.* Though you possess only one form, nothing is impossible for you.

That verse shows that the Lord’s svarūpa-śakti is inconceivable by using various adjectives to describe his ātmā-māyā. Though he does not have two forms, by his inconceivable śakti his production of the universe is included in him.

Then the passage says:

*sama-viṣama-matīnāṁ matam anusarasi yathā rajju-khaṇḍaḥ sarpādi-dhiyām.*

**You accept the opinion of those who say that Bhagavān is the Brahman with material variety** ***(viṣama)*** **though they are actually two aspects of one form** ***(sama).*** **However, this opinion is like thinking a rope is a snake. SB 6.9.37**

The meaning is “You appear in this way to people of high and low consciousness.”

In SB 6.9.34, *aśarīra* means “without bodily activities”. *Aśaraṇa* means “without the shelter of earth etc.” Even in verse 35, though the Lord is inferred to be neutral, it should be understood that he is an agent. The prominence of his svarūpa-śakti is shown.

SB 2.9.34, by showing that māyā is like a shadow, indicates that the Lord is untouched by māyā. This is also understood from the following:

*tvam ādyaḥ puruṣaḥ sākṣād īśvaraḥ prakṛteḥ paraḥ \|*  
*māyāṁ vyudasya cic-chaktyā kaivalye sthita ātmani \|\|*

**You are the original Lord, the controller, beyond material energy. You are situated in your spiritual form, being one with your spiritual energy, and separate from the material energy. SB 1.7.23**

*māyā paraity abhimukhe ca vilajjamānā \|\|*

**Māyā, being ashamed*****,*** **cannot stand before the Lord. B 2.7.47**

Thus, the idea that the Lord acts against the jīva is rejected. Jīva’s relationship with avidyā results from the Lord’s māyā, which has been shown to be inconceivable.

By māyā, the jīva *(vimuktasya*) who has the ability to realize his svarūpa (īśvarasya), has his knowledge of ātmā covered (*kārpaṇyam*) and enters the net of guṇas previously shown (bandhanam). It is said *tat-saṅga-bhraṁśitaiśvaryam*: the jīva in association with māyā loses all his powers. (SB 6.5.15)

The śrutis also indicate this in their prayers.

*sa yad ajayā ajām anuśayīta guṇāṁś ca juṣan*  
*bhajati sarūpatāṁ tad anu mṛtyum apeta-bhagaḥ*  
*tvam uta jahāsi tām ahir iva tvacam ātta-bhago*  
*mahasi mahīyase ’ṣṭa-guṇite ’parimeya-bhagaḥ*

**The jīva contacts matter by the influence of māyā, takes on similar form due to upādhis, and enjoys material objects. He thus experiences saṁsāra. You, however, avoid the material energy in the same way that a snake abandons its old skin. Glorious in your possession of eight mystic powers, you display unlimited greatness. SB 10.87.36**

By saying that the Lord possesses māyā (*bhagavataḥ māyā*), it is understood that the Lord is devoid of māyā. Similarly the phrase “māyā of Indra” means “Indra is in control of illusion.” Previous statements can be understood in the same way.

## 91.

That the jīva does not actually have misery and bondage but only appears to have this by the Lord’s māyā is illustrated with an example.

*yad arthena vināmuṣya puṁsa ātma-viparyayaḥ*  
*pratīyata upadraṣṭuḥ sva-śiraś chedanādikaḥ*

**Because of māyā, the jīva’s loss of knowledge and bliss makes its appearance without cause or purpose. The loss is illusory, just as the seer of a dream experiencing his head being cut off is illusory. SB 3.7.10**

Because of māyā (*yat),* though it is without purpose in past, present and future *(arthena vinā api*), the jīva forgets his identity and thinks he is something else (*ātmā-viparyaya*). This is the purpose. In a dream it is perceived by the jīva (*upadrastuḥ*) that his head is cut off, which is impossible. His head has not been cut off and no one has seen this take place. But the Lord’s māyā, accomplishing this, imposes this display on the jīva. *Māyā-matraṁ tu kārtsnyenānabhivyakta-svarūpatvāt*: the cause of dreams is the Lord's *māyā* because the dreams by nature are not revealed to everyone. (Brahma-sūtra 3.2.3)

## 92.

Though the jīva is pure, he takes on the quality of upādhis because of the upādhis. This is illustrated with an example.

*yathā jale candramasaḥ kampādis tat-kṛto guṇaḥ*  
*dṛśyate ’sann api draṣṭur ātmano ’nātmano guṇaḥ*

**The qualities belonging to the subtle body do not belong to the** ***ātmā*** **but appear to be so, just as the trembling quality in the water imposed on the reflection of the moon is not the trembling of the moon, though it appears to be so. SB 3.7.11**

Just as qualities like quivering of the reflection of the moon are seen, created by the medium (upādhi) of water, though the moon in the sky does not quiver, similarly the qualities of the material upādhis appear on the pure ātmā who becomes the seer-- the ātmā in a covered state (*draṣṭuḥ),* the material ahaṅkāra, represented by the reflection, which identifies the self with the upādhis because of māyā, thinking, “I am that material upādhi.” The ātmā is pure but he sees himself as a material body by identification. It is said:

*nṛtyato gāyataḥ paśyan yathaivānukaroti tān*  
*evaṁ buddhi-guṇān paśyann anīho ‘py anukāryate*

**Just as one may imitate persons whom one sees dancing and singing, similarly the soul, although never the doer of material activities, is thus forced to imitate the qualities of the intelligence. SB 11.22.53**

It is also said *śuddho vicaṣṭe hy aviśuddha-kartuḥ*: the pure jīva becomes absorbed in the actions of the conditioned soul. (SB 5.11.12) The affix *vi* in *vicaṣṭe* indicates absorption.

Thus the Lord, possessing very power, inconceivable antaraṅga-śakti in his svarūpa, is not touched by māyā, even though it is also strong and inconceivable. But the jīva is touched by māyā. That is the conclusion. Śukadeva speaks the verse.

Actions of the Lord, without prejudice or hatred

## 93.

Though the Lord’s creation, maintenance and destruction of the universe have been explained, the conclusion is again presented to answer specific doubts, in the manner of uprooting a stump of a tree.

“One hears of the various avatāras who side the devatās, giving them mercy with smiles and gestures of fearlessness, while killing demons in battle. Are these forms and pastimes done by the supreme Lord or not? If done by the supreme Lord, then the Lord must be material, and must be partial, taking sides. If the Lord does not make these forms or perform these pastimes, then they are not part of the Lord’s svarūpa. This goes against your proposition.”

Concerning this doubt, this should be said. It is true that the supreme Lord does nothing in regards to protection of the universe. However he reveals pastimes and various avatāras, manifesting through his svarūpa-śakti, in order to give bliss to Vaikuṇṭha associates who appear along with him and to other devotees including devatās who control the universe. It is said in Padma Purāṇa:

*muhūrtenāpi saṁhartuṁ śakto yadyapi dānavān*  
*mad-bhaktānāṁ vinodārthaṁ karomi vividhāḥ kriyāḥ*  
*darśana-dhyāna-saṁsparśair matsya-kūrma-vihaṅgamāḥ*  
*svāny apatyāni puṣṇanti tathāham api padmaja.*

**Even though I can kill the demons in a moment, I perform various actions to give pleasure to my devotees. Just as the fish by seeing, the turtle by thought and the bird by touch, nourish their young, I nourish my devotees.**

Hari-bhakti-sudhodaya says:

*nityaṁ ca pūrṇa-kāmasya janmāni vividhāni me*  
*bhakta-sarveṣṭa-dānāya tasmāt kiṁ te priyaṁ vada*

**I am fully satisfied eternally. I take various births in order to fulfill the desires of my devotees. Tell me your desire.**

Kuntī says the Lord descends in order to arrange for bhakti (*bhakti-yoga-vidhānārthaṁ* SB 1.8.20). According to the commentary of Śrīdhara Svāmī, *bhakti-yoga-vidhānārtham* means “you have appeared for the performance of bhakti.” Brahmā says:

*prapañcaṁ niṣprapañco ’pi viḍambayasi bhū-tale*  
*prapanna-janatānanda- sandohaṁ prathituṁ prabho*

**My dear master! Although you have nothing to do with material existence, you come to this earth and imitate material life just to expand the varieties of ecstatic enjoyment for your surrendered devotees. SB 10.14.37**

Brahmā also states that the Lord appears by his svarūpa-śakti.

*eṣa prapanna-varado ramayātma-śaktyā*  
*yad yat kariṣyati gṛhīta-guṇāvatāraḥ*  
*tasmin sva-vikramam idaṁ sṛjato ’pi ceto*  
*yuñjīta karma-śamalaṁ ca yathā vijahyām*

**When I create this universe endowed with his power, may the Lord, who benedicts the surrendered souls and manifests attractive qualities when he appears in this world by his internal energy, engage my mind in his pastimes which he performs, so that I can avoid the sins involved in creating. SB 3.9.23**

*Gṛhīta-guṇāvataraḥ* means “he who appears as an avatāra with attractive qualities like mercy.”

Though the Lord appears to give bliss to the devotees, secondarily, actions which protect the universe and side with the devatās occur through his māyā. Some devotees in this world gather together, expressing their prema for the Lord to each other. They gather some mṛdaṅga players unknown to them, and they dance madly out of bliss on hearing the Lord’s qualities. By this action, they destroy all inauspiciousness in the universe and increase auspiciousness. It is said:

*vāg gadgadā dravate yasya cittaṁ*  
*rudaty abhīkṣṇaṁ hasati kvacic ca*  
*vilajja udgāyati nṛtyate ca*  
*mad-bhakti-yukto bhuvanaṁ punāti*

**A devotee whose speech is choked up, whose heart is melted, who cries continually and sometimes laughs, sometimes feels ashamed, sometimes sings loudly and sometimes dances—a devotee thus fixed in loving service to me purifies the entire universe. SB 11.14.24**

It is said:

*sṛṣṭy-ādikaṁ harer naiva prayojanam apekṣya tu*  
*kurute kevalānandād yathā mattasya nartanam*

**The Lord creates the world out of pure bliss, with no purpose, like the creation made by a madman. Nārāyaṇa Saṁhitā**

One should not infer that the inner satisfaction is not present in the Lord or the devotees if the Lord must give the devotees bliss and they must give him bliss. One should also not say that in ignoring others while giving bliss to the devotees, the Lord shows partiality. In answer to the first doubt, having attained a body of pure sattva made intense by spiritual purity, the devotee gains the highest satisfaction. But the Lord gives further bliss to him, since he is affectionate to the devotees. That quality of being affectionate to the devotees acts favorably to him rather than diminishing his nature.

Parīkṣit speaks to the sages:

*nehātha nāmutra ca kaścanārtha*  
*ṛte parānugraham ātma-śīlam \|\|*

**You have no goal except to give mercy to others in this world and in the next. Actually that is your very nature. SB 1.19.23**

In the story of Jaḍa-bharata it is said*sindhu-pataya ātma-satattvaṁ vigaṇayataḥ parānubhāvaḥ parama-kāruṇikatayopadiśya:* Jaḍa Bharata, having the highest realization, though insulted by the King, out of great mercy instructed the King on spiritual matters. SB 5.13.24

Nārada in his previous life described the sages:

*te mayy apetākhila-cāpale ’rbhake*  
*dānte ’dhṛta-krīḍanake ’nuvartini \|*  
*cakruḥ kṛpāṁ yadyapi tulya-darśanāḥ*  
*śuśrūṣamāṇe munayo ’lpa-bhāṣiṇi \|*

**Though the sages saw everything equally, they showed mercy to me, by which I, though a boy, developed sense control, gave up child’s play and all agitation, became obedient, served attentively, and spoke little. SB 1.5.24**

*namo ’kiñcana-vittāya nivṛtta-guṇa-vṛttaye \|*  
*ātmārāmāya śāntāya kaivalya-pataye namaḥ \|\|*

**I offer my respects to he whose treasure is unalloyed devotees, who is devoid of material interest, who is disinterested in those who are not devotees, who forgives his devotees, and helps the devotee desiring liberation. SB 1.8.27**

*Akiñcana-vittāya* means “to he who has devotees as his treasure.” If this quality did not exist, there would arise the fault of ingratitude in the Lord.

With the quality of affection for his devotees, by destroying devotee’s suffering and giving them bliss, the Lord’s own bliss develops.

There is a function of the svarūpa śakti, which is the highest essence called hlādini. The essence of the hlādini is bhakti. That bhakti called rati always exists in the Lord and his devotee. Thus it is said *bhagavān bhakti-bhaktimān*: the Lord has devotion to his devotee. (SB 10.86.59) Thus there is no destruction of satisfaction in the Lord since the Lord is satisfied by the devotion (part of the Lord’s svarūpa-śakti) situated in the devotee.

Moreover, because bhakti is the Lord’s śakti, by its very nature it is different and non-different from him. *Ye yathā māṇṁ prapadyante tāṁs tathaiva bhajāmy aham:* I respond according to the surrender of my devotee. (BG 4.11) Because bhakti appears to be different in the devotee’s heart, the Lord experiences astonishing bliss, thinking “His bhakti gives me joy.” The svarūpa of bhakti is the quality of appearing different in the Lord and his devotee.

Viṣṇu expresses this to Durvāsā:

*ahaṁ bhakta-parādhīno hy asvatantra iva dvija*  
*sādhubhir grasta-hṛdayo bhaktair bhakta-jana-priyaḥ*

**O brāhmaṇa! I am completely under the control of my devotees. I am not at all independent. My heart is controlled by the pure devotees. What to speak of my devotee, even those who are devotees of my devotee are very dear to me.**

*nāham ātmānam āśāse mad-bhaktaiḥ sādhubhir vinā*  
*śriyaṁ cātyantikīṁ brahman yeṣāṁ gatir ahaṁ parā*

**O brāhmaṇa! Without the devotees, who take shelter of only me, I do not desire to enjoy my own bliss by my six great qualities.**

*ye dārāgāra-putrāpta- prāṇān vittam imaṁ param*  
*hitvā māṁ śaraṇaṁ yātāḥ kathaṁ tāṁs tyaktum utsahe*

Since pure devotees give up their homes, wives, children, relatives, riches and even their lives simply to serve me, without any desire for material improvement in this life or in the next, how can I give up such devotees at any time?

*mayi nirbaddha-hṛdayāḥ sādhavaḥ sama-darśanāḥ*  
*vaśe kurvanti māṁ bhaktyā sat-striyaḥ sat-patiṁ yathā*

**As chaste women bring their gentle husbands under control by service, the pure devotees, who see others’ suffering as their own and are completely attached to me in the core of the heart, bring me under their full control.**

*mat-sevayā pratītaṁ te sālokyādi-catuṣṭayam*  
*necchanti sevayā pūrṇāḥ kuto 'nyat kāla-viplutam*
